This weekend, I achieved a new milestone. I delivered my first TEDx talk.

Yes, it was still about AI, but not as a business tool for ROI or productivity. This time, I spoke about AI as a life companion. About how it can shape our mindset, improve the quality of our lives, and become part of how we think and grow.

I’ll be honest, I was nervous. storytelling and emotion aren’t usually my stage comfort zone. Add to that a packed week, I delivered a 4.5-hour RAG workshop with my friend on Saturday, and the weight of everything else going on. I wondered if I could do justice to it.

But the moment I stepped on stage, the nerves vanished. It felt natural, like talking to my mom and others like her in the audience. I wasn’t just there as an AI professional,I was there to connect as a human.

None of this would have been possible without my strongest support system. My husband who patiently sat with me while I shaped every part of the talk (yes, I chewed his brain endlessly 🙈). My mom and in-laws who listened to me rehearse and gave me their honest feedback.

I had my permanent, constant audience who never fails to cheer me on, so how could it not be good ♥️

The lesson for me: when life feels like a tsunami of deadlines, stress, and challenges you come out stronger once you get through it. You realize there isn’t anything you can’t do if you put your mind to it.

Sometimes the process is hectic. But the end of the path is beautiful 😃 

If you’re standing in front of a challenge right now, take it head-on. You’ll surprise yourself with how much strength you already have!

TEDxMilpitas TEDx TED Conferences

--- Post 6 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 4,128 reactions, 123 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 21,896)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7363014161007570945

I was invited to attend Buildathon organized by DeepLearning.AI and AI Fund this Saturday, and I finally got to meet someone I’ve looked up to since the very start of my ML journey- Andrew Ng.

His machine learning course was the very first thing I took when I started out, and it set the gold standard for how I think education should be. Even today, whenever I want to learn a new technology, I find myself returning to DeepLearning.AI, whether it’s the longer programs or short courses on new toolkits. It’s also the #1 place I point my mentees and students to when they’re starting their AI journey.

Meeting Andrew in person was surreal. In the morning, I only got a couple of minutes with him after his session, but later in the day, we ended up chatting for nearly 45 minutes. That conversation was easily the highlight of my weekend.

We spoke about the future of AI engineering, the product management bottleneck, and how companies should think about getting user feedback in this new world. What struck me most was his perspective:

👉 Instead of “move fast and break things,” we should aim to move fast and be responsible.

👉 With AI-assisted coding, software development cycles are changing, code is no longer the main artifact; it’s about how quickly you can prototype and learn.

👉 The future of AI engineering blends rapid prototyping, agentic workflows, RAG, evals, and MCP/tool use into a new discipline.

👉 Vibe Coding should be all about Rapid Engineering 

What inspired me most is that Andrew brings both the rigor of academia and the practicality of building real-world systems. He’s also the one who first made me think deeply about data-centric AI, focusing on how data drives models, instead of chasing models first and realizing the data doesn’t fit later. That mindset continues to guide me today.

Walking away from our chat, I felt reminded of why I love this field. AI is evolving faster than ever, but the real impact comes from those who not only build quickly but also build responsibly.

💡 Takeaway for anyone entering this space: Don’t shy away from learning to code because “AI will automate it.” That’s bad advice. Coding is just becoming easier, and this means everyone- engineers, PMs, designers, can build with AI. The opportunities are only expanding.

Forever grateful for this encounter. Sometimes you meet your role models, and they turn out to be even more inspiring than you imagined.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7355262864573554688

If you’re an AI professional, these are 10 research papers I’d highly recommend reading 👇

Whether you’re an engineer, researcher, PM, or founder in AI, these will give you depth, context, and clarity on where we came from, and give you sense of where we’re headed.

They’ve shaped everything from GPT to LoRA to tool-augmented agents. Whether you’re building, scaling, or researching LLMs, this is your foundation.

→ Attention Is All You Need
https://lnkd.in/dzGaatsJ
Introduced Transformers, the architecture that powers all modern LLMs.

→ BERT
https://lnkd.in/da9CReM3
Taught models to understand context from both directions using masked tokens.

→ GPT (v1)
https://lnkd.in/dsbSYYjp
Showed how unsupervised pretraining plus task finetuning unlocks strong performance.

→ RLHF: Learning to Summarize with Human Feedback
https://lnkd.in/dCqv4e2a
Introduced RLHF, the foundation of how models like ChatGPT are aligned to human preferences.

→ LoRA
https://lnkd.in/db7Tgrhp
Efficient method to adapt LLMs using low-rank matrices, making finetuning cheaper and scalable.

→ Retention Is All You Need
https://lnkd.in/dpnrApJd
A new way to handle context, mixing ideas from attention, recurrence, and convnets.

→ Chain-of-Thought Prompting
https://lnkd.in/dPJUR7dn
Prompting LLMs to “think out loud” improves reasoning and complex task solving.

→ The Illusion of Thinking
https://lnkd.in/dGm49u_T
A cautionary paper, showing that LLMs can sound smart while being confidently wrong.

→ Knowledge Distillation
https://lnkd.in/dwYZSSfQ
Shrink big models into smaller ones without losing much performance.

→ RLVR: Verifiable Rewards
https://lnkd.in/djKRBAU6
Extends RL into real-world domains using logic-backed, verifiable reward signals.

If you’re a technical professional and can carve out some time, print these papers out, grab a highlighter, and read through them, this helps me best understand papers.

But if time is tight, upload them to NotebookLM and use it as your study buddy. You can ask questions, walk through diagrams, and let the model help you understand them like a coach.

What’s your favorite paper that should’ve made this list? 👇

〰️〰️〰️

Hundreds of AI minds gathered in Central Park on a chilly Saturday morning, it was incredible :)

The past weekend in New York felt like one of those moments where community completely surprises you in the best way.

I was traveling to the city for a work event, and my friend Pranjal and I had a thought- What if we host a small AI meetup on Saturday morning in Central Park? Nothing fancy, just a space for people to connect.

So we put up a quick Luma link and shared it on LinkedIn. We expected maybe a handful of people. Instead, the sign ups crossed 450+. 

We put a small table with coffee, muffins, cookies and some cool swag- thanks to Teachable. We weren’t sure how it would go. We just hoped people would show up.

What happened next honestly moved me.

We reached the park at 9.20am to set up for a 10am meetup, and people were already there. In the cold. On a Saturday morning. People had traveled from Boston, Maryland, upstate New York, New Jersey, even Philadelphia. I kept talking to folks thinking, you really came all this way… for this?

The meetup was meant to last two hours. People stayed until 3pm! Almost six hours of conversations about AI, careers, ideas, and everything in between. No microphones. No agenda. Just humans connecting because they care about the same things.

This experience reminded me of something I tend to forget- community doesn’t need perfect planning. It needs intention. It needs genuine people. And it needs a space where everyone feels welcome.

I’m grateful to every single person who showed up. You made this morning unforgettable, and you reminded me why I love building in this space.

Shout-out to Aditya Suresh and Pranam Daga for helping us with putting this together ❤️

--- Post 12 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 2,007 reactions, 138 comments, 235 reposts (Score: 11,885)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7358891950302818305

Most people think RAG is just “vector DB + LLM.”

But as you scale real-world use cases, Naive RAG breaks fast.

Here’s a breakdown of the 4 types of RAG and how they evolve:

→ 📚Naive RAG
The entry point. You embed the query, retrieve top-k chunks, and stuff them into a prompt.
Works fine for simple Q&A, but struggles with multi-hop reasoning, long context, and hallucinations.

→ 🛠️Advanced RAG
This is where real engineering begins.
You layer in pre-retrieval filtering, hybrid indexes, reranking, query rewriting, memory, and post-retrieval prediction.

You move from static retrieval to modular pipelines like:
Retrieve → Read → Predict or Rewrite → Retrieve → Rerank → Read

Useful when accuracy, context handling, or traceability matters.

→ ➿Graph RAG
Structured meets semantic.
You extract or connect to a knowledge graph, pair it with your vector DB, and retrieve both relational and unstructured data.

Prompt gets augmented with graph paths and node metadata, enabling explainable reasoning.
Used in enterprise search, healthcare, finance, and anywhere structured logic plays a key role.

→ 🤖Agentic RAG
The most powerful RAG pattern today.
Now, the model doesn’t just retrieve—it plans, acts, and routes.
It decides:
- What to retrieve
- What function or tool to call
- How to persist results

It combines prompt + retrieved data + tool schema to dynamically invoke APIs or external actions.
Your RAG stack now includes: tool functions, graph DBs, relational memory, and agent logic.

If you’re building agents, copilots, or production-grade assistants, Agentic RAG is where the industry is heading.

〰️〰️〰️

If you're feeling overwhelmed with how fast AI is evolving, you're not alone.

Every day there’s a new paper, a new framework, a new agent loop, and it’s easy to feel like you’re falling behind.

But the good news is that you don’t need to learn everything all at once. What you need is structure.

So I put together a 10-level AI Agents Learning Roadmap that takes you from foundations to production, layering your learning in a way that’s actually doable.

💡My recommendation: spend 2–3 weeks on each level. Learn the concepts, implement small projects, and build your intuition.

If you're moving faster or slower based on time or experience, that’s okay too.
And when something new drops? That can be your Level 11. Don’t let “newness” derail your plan. Just start here.

👇 Here’s the roadmap:
🔖 Level 1: GenAI & Transformer Foundations
 Tokens, embeddings, transformers, decoding, and inference with open-weight models.

🔖 Level 2: Prompting & Language Model Behavior
 Prompt types (CoT, ReAct, ToT), decoding strategies, context design, and adversarial prompting.

🔖 Level 3: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)
 Chunking, embeddings, vector DBs, RAG pipelines, and RAG evaluation.

🔖 Level 4: LLMOps & Tools
 LangChain, LangGraph, Dust, CrewAI, tool use, function calling, and synthetic data.

🔖 Level 5: Agents & Agent Frameworks
 Agent types, memory, planning, LangChain agents, LangGraph loops, and evaluation.

🔖 Level 6: Memory, State & Orchestration
 Vector and symbolic memory, episodic vs persistent state, memory compression.

🔖 Level 7: Multi-Agent Systems
 Hub-and-spoke vs decentralized, message passing, collaborative agents, agent teams.

🔖 Level 8: Evaluation & Reinforcement Learning
 LLM-as-a-Judge, RLHF, RLVR, reward modeling, and self-correcting loops.

🔖 Level 9: Protocols & Safety
 MCP, A2A, safety alignment, guardrails, traceability, and autonomous policy updates.

🔖 Level 10: Build & Deploy
 FastAPI, Streamlit, GGUF, QLoRA, caching, monitoring with LangSmith, Arize, Trulens.

📌 Bookmark this.
🛠️ Build something after every level.

And if you're wondering what tools to explore along the way → Start with Hugging Face (to explore LLMs and SLMs), you can use Ollama (to run SLMs on your laptop, like Phi-4, TinyLlama), or Fireworks AI (to run LLMs via endpoint, like Qwen 3, Kimi K2, DeepSeek R1), then explore LangChain & LangGraph (these two tools will teach you a lot), then you can move into learning Agentic frameworks like CrewAI, Microsoft AI Frontiers. 

💻 Pro-tip: Start with cookbooks!

〰️〰️〰️

Eight years ago, I boarded a flight from India to the U.S., headed to Columbia University for my Master’s.

I thought I knew what the next few years would look like. I couldn’t have been more wrong, and in the best possible way.

Looking back, these eight years feel like decades. Every twist and turn, every unexpected pivot, has shaped me in ways I never could have imagined. Things I once didn’t even dare to dream are now my reality. And yet, I know there’s so much more to learn, so much further to go.

If I could speak to my younger self in 2017, I’d tell her:
→ Things may not make sense until the very last moment, but they will fall into place.
→ What feels like failure in the moment is often just life steering you toward a better path.
→ Every time something doesn’t work out, come back stronger. The next milestone will be even bigger.

One pattern I’ve noticed in my own journey:
Every time I’ve “failed,” I’ve returned more driven, more focused, and more determined. That energy, if channeled right, can be the fuel that powers your next leap.

For anyone at a crossroads- whether you’re moving countries, starting a new role, or pursuing a completely different path, here’s my advice:

❤️ If you truly want something, don’t ask yourself if you can do it. Tell yourself you have to. Remove the option of self-rejection.

❤️ Ask yourself daily: “What am I doing today to get closer to my goal?” That goal could be two years away or ten, but the daily steps matter more than the timeline.

❤️ Growth comes from leaning into discomfort, from showing up even when things feel uncertain, and from refusing to shut the door on yourself.

Eight years in, the biggest lesson I’ve learned is simple:
🫰 Don’t wait for perfect conditions. Take the leap, trust the process, and let the journey surprise you.

============================================================

I didn’t think anything could make me switch browsers after over a decade on Chrome. But after using Comet by Perplexity (their latest launch of agentic browser), I’m seriously rethinking that.

Over the past few days, I’ve been using Comet as my primary browser, and it’s a complete game changer.

💡 Here are a few ways I’ve been using it:

→ Drafting lesson plans and slides directly in the browser with contextual AI, right from searching to getting draft slides

→ Summarizing long research papers, reports, and YouTube videos in one click, and taking all that over to docs for my content drafts

→ Reviewing partnership emails and identifying brands that genuinely align with my voice, and even asking Comet to write thoughtful replies to them 

→ Acting as my AI-powered social media manager, analyzing the performance of my LinkedIn posts and suggesting ways to improve engagement and clarity, and putting all of that into my Notion (*Happy tears*, no copy pasting for me anymore)

→ Helped me find cheapest tickets to New York for my b'day trip, without me browsing and comparing through 10s of websites

It’s fast, intuitive, and super powerful, exactly how an AI-native browser should feel!!

For those might be unfamiliar, agentic browsers like Comet do more than search. They DO stuff for you- retrieving, analyzing, comparing, summarizing, and even communicating, so you spend less time switching tools and more time getting things done.

This isn’t just a browser with AI bolted on, it’s a reimagined way to interact with the internet, and it is CRAZY!

In my opinion, we’re moving toward a world where:
→ Websites may become less important than the structured data behind them
→ Human interaction with UIs could drastically shrink, delegated instead to AI agents
→ The core of browsing becomes semantic, intent-driven, and automated
→ We are moving beyond AI acting like a disintegrated tool, to becoming deeply embedded into all the tools you are using, at the same time! 

🔥 Huge kudos to the Perplexity team for building something this elegant yet powerful. And thanks Aravind Srinivas for looping me in for early access of Comet 🙌

I’ll be putting together a full breakdown of how to use Comet with tips, advanced workflows, and examples on how I’m using it. Stay tuned for that 😉

Every Diwali, I find myself hitting a quiet reset button.

It’s more than just lights, sweets, and fireworks - it’s that feeling of renewal. A reminder that we can begin again, no matter how the year has unfolded.

Each year, this festival grounds me in gratitude and hope. It reminds me that even when things don’t go as planned, we can still create moments of meaning, sometimes in the most unexpected places.

Last year, I celebrated Diwali surrounded by my entire family, laughter echoing through our home.
This year looked different. We found ourselves celebrating in the middle of the mountains, just calm skies, fresh air, and quiet reflection.

And somehow, it was equally beautiful.

For me, Diwali isn’t just about where you are, but the light you carry with you- in your work, your relationships, and your own growth journey.

Here’s to new beginnings, to chasing what lights you up, and to carrying that energy through the rest of the year.

ICYMI - OpenAI ChatGPT just dropped one of its most underrated features yet: Study Mode 📚 

Instead of just giving you answers, Study Mode guides you through a topic like a real tutor would. You can access it directly in ChatGPT, just toggle into Study Mode from the mode switcher.

Once inside, you can:
→ Ask it to explain any topic, step by step
→ Practice problems with guided feedback
→ Get quizzed, generate flashcards, or simulate interviews
→ Build a custom study plan based on your goals

Here’s what I tried 👇 
→ Had it walk me through a explaining how Mixture-of-Experts work, layer by layer
→ Built a custom weekly study plan for people who want to move from SWE to Data Scientist
→ Asked it to help me understand LCM paper by Meta, step-by-step (it also questions my understanding!)

What’s impressive is how thoughtfully it takes your input- goals, struggles, preferred learning style, and builds from there. This isn’t just a better search, it is actually doing a structured, and interactive learning.

And the best part?
This isn’t just for students.
→ Get help to prepare for interviews with custom roadmap
→ Professionals can upskill with targeted feedback
→ Parents can use this to support their kids' learning journeys
→ Curious minds of any age can explore new topics with depth
→ Kids can build critical thinking muscles instead of just googling answers

In a world where it’s easy to default to fast answers, this adds friction in a good way. It helps you slow down, reflect, and grow.

It’s now available to everyone (free and paid), and it looks like it's also coming soon as part of ChatGPT Edu!

Are you wondering where the AI industry is actually headed? 
Every week brings a new framework, a new toolkit, a new acronym. But beneath all the noise, one truth is becoming clearer: the real moat isn’t the model, it’s the combination of data and AI. 
 
AI is the toolkit. 
Data is the substance. 
AI gives us reasoning power. 
Data gives us understanding. 

That connection came to life at Snowflake BUILD 2025, where the  focus wasn’t on hype, it was on grounding AI in reality. 
 
We saw how Snowflake Intelligence lets teams ask complex questions in natural language and get trusted answers from governed data. How the Cortex Agents API enables developers to build domain-specific agents that reason and collaborate. And how the Managed MCP Server securely bridges external AI systems to enterprise data, turning “agentic AI” from an idea into something production-ready. 
 
Even unstructured data, the 80 % of information hidden in PDFs, chats, and documents, is now first-class and accessible through simple AISQL queries. 
 
This feels like a turning point. 
The next era of AI won’t be defined by bigger models, but by deeper integration between data and reasoning. 
 
AI without data is just potential. 
Data without AI is just storage. 
Together, they become intelligence.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7425247804123463682

Introducing DreamZero: we trained a robot foundation model that can do zero-shot, open-world prompting for new verbs, nouns, and environments. DreamZero is our first "World Action Model" (WAM): if the world model backbone can "dream" the right future in video pixels, then the robot can execute well in motors.

Our team had tons of fun at the lab typing anything we like into an open text prompt, and watch the robot perform tasks it was never trained on. An emergent capability we didn't quite expect! Obviously not GPT-3 reliable yet, but we are marching into the GPT-2 era.

Discoveries:

- Model and data recipe co-evolve. Compared to VLAs, WAMs learn best from diverse data, breaking away from the conventional wisdom that lots of repeated demos per task are the bread and butter. Diversity >> repetitions.

- X-embodiment is extremely hard. Pixels are the answer. Different robot morphologies traditionally have a hard time sharing knowledge well. But if we put video first, pixels become the universal bridge connecting different hardware - even videos of human first-person view. DreamZero shows significant robot2robot and human2robot transfer. With only 55 trajectories on a *new*, unseen hardware (~30 min of teleop), it adapts so quickly and retains zero-shot prompting ability.

Yesterday I posted about the "Second Pre-training Paradigm": world models are the next-gen foundation of Physical AI, not language backbones. 

Today, we are proving it works. And 2026 has just begun.

- Paper: World Action Models are Zero-Shot Policies.
- Website: https://lnkd.in/gHVdTBiX
- Robot execution demos across various verbs, nouns, and environments: https://lnkd.in/gw_55DsK
- Our model is open-weights! https://lnkd.in/gvuYZ6y5
- This is a huge team work at NVIDIA. All credits go to the wonderful team who poured their hearts into it!

Just wrapped up an incredible few days at AI4 2025 in Las Vegas.

I had the chance to give a talk on something I care deeply about → building open-source agentic AI systems at scale. At Fireworks AI, this is where so much of our energy goes, making it possible for developers to not just experiment with agents, but actually run them in production: low-latency inference, customize the model with SFT & reinforcement FT, orchestration, model evaluation, that works beyond demos.

That’s the part of AI I find most exciting right now, not just pushing models bigger, but making them practical and reliable for real-world workflows.

Outside of my talk, I joined a creator panel, which was such a refreshing change of pace. We got into how AI is reshaping the way people create- whether it’s helping speed up workflows, sparking new kinds of ideas, or just giving creators more freedom to focus on the parts they love most. 

It was really fun to step out of the “enterprise AI” lens and hear how people across different creative spaces are experimenting with these tools.

I also had the chance to sit down with Amit Jain, CEO of Luma. Our conversation was one of my favorites of the week- we talked about the role of generative models in creativity, and what it really means to build technology that empowers rather than replaces. 

Hearing his perspective on where Luma is going and how they think about the intersection of AI + creativity was incredibly inspiring.

And then, of course, the surreal moments → getting to hear from Geoffrey Hinton and Fei-Fei Li live. Hinton reflected on both the promise and risks of scaling AI, while Fei-Fei reminded us that AI in education should be empowering (not cheating) and that human-centered AI has to stay at the core of this revolution.

Leaving Vegas inspired and more energized than ever to keep building what’s next. 🚀

============================================================

⚠️ 🚨 Swati Jha decided to copy my content word by word! 
I recently discovered, when someone tagged me in her post calling out that she had copied my content.

No attribution. No original thinking. Just a direct lift, passed off as their own work.

AI is not something you cosplay into through content farming. It is a discipline that requires depth, accountability, and real world experience. Treating it like a growth hack does real damage, especially to people who are still learning and cannot yet tell the difference between signal and noise.

⚠️ Here is my note to anyone consuming content in this space.

1. Please be intentional about who you follow. Look beyond job titles and polished bios. Pay attention to the circles people operate in. See who consistently engages with their work, who comments thoughtfully, and whether those voices have real credibility. Notice which in person conferences they show up to, which credible institutions they are actually part of, and whether they are contributing meaningfully to the ecosystem beyond social media posts.

2. Creating content with AI tools has become easy. Being CREDIBLE is NOT!

3. Right now, social media is flooded with people with no depth claiming expertise. That is not harmless. It actively misleads learners and lowers the bar for an entire field that already struggles with hype.

4. Be mindful of who you learn from. In fast moving technical spaces, the wrong voices can genuinely make or break what you understand.

5. Depth still matters. Integrity still matters. And expertise is more than a well designed post!

Most people think you need to be an engineer to break into AI product management. You don’t!

What you do need is a structured way to build intuition for AI, sharpen your product skills, and show proof of execution.

I put together this roadmap to becoming an AI Product Manager, based on what I’ve seen work in real-world teams:

→ Step 0: Master core product skills
Roadmaps, user stories, product metrics. Start with the basics of product management.

→ Step 1: Learn AI fundamentals
Understand training vs inference, supervised vs unsupervised, data → model → deployment. You don’t need to code deeply, but you need to grasp the flow.

→ Step 2: Develop product intuition with AI
Experiment with LLMs, explore prompt engineering, study successful AI products and how they solve real-world problems.

→ Step 3: Build & ship something small
Automate a workflow, build a chatbot, or use no-code/low-code tools. Execution > theory.

→ Step 4: Learn MLOps & Infra
Not at the engineer level, but enough to know evaluation, retraining, and scaling challenges. Product managers who understand infra earn instant credibility.

→ Step 5: Grow your network
Write, share, and engage with the community. The fastest way to build trust is to show you’re actively contributing.

The field is still wide open. And the best way to stand out isn’t to read more theory- it’s to build, learn fast, and show your work.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025

If you’re an AI professional, these are 10 research papers I’d highly recommend reading 👇

Whether you’re an engineer, researcher, PM, or founder in AI, these will give you depth, context, and clarity on where we came from, and give you sense of where we’re headed.

They’ve shaped everything from GPT to LoRA to tool-augmented agents. Whether you’re building, scaling, or researching LLMs, this is your foundation.

→ Attention Is All You Need
https://lnkd.in/dzGaatsJ
Introduced Transformers, the architecture that powers all modern LLMs.

→ BERT
https://lnkd.in/da9CReM3
Taught models to understand context from both directions using masked tokens.

→ GPT (v1)
https://lnkd.in/dsbSYYjp
Showed how unsupervised pretraining plus task finetuning unlocks strong performance.

→ RLHF: Learning to Summarize with Human Feedback
https://lnkd.in/dCqv4e2a
Introduced RLHF, the foundation of how models like ChatGPT are aligned to human preferences.

→ LoRA
https://lnkd.in/db7Tgrhp
Efficient method to adapt LLMs using low-rank matrices, making finetuning cheaper and scalable.

→ Retention Is All You Need
https://lnkd.in/dpnrApJd
A new way to handle context, mixing ideas from attention, recurrence, and convnets.

→ Chain-of-Thought Prompting
https://lnkd.in/dPJUR7dn
Prompting LLMs to “think out loud” improves reasoning and complex task solving.

→ The Illusion of Thinking
https://lnkd.in/dGm49u_T
A cautionary paper, showing that LLMs can sound smart while being confidently wrong.

→ Knowledge Distillation
https://lnkd.in/dwYZSSfQ
Shrink big models into smaller ones without losing much performance.

→ RLVR: Verifiable Rewards
https://lnkd.in/djKRBAU6
Extends RL into real-world domains using logic-backed, verifiable reward signals.

If you’re a technical professional and can carve out some time, print these papers out, grab a highlighter, and read through them, this helps me best understand papers.

But if time is tight, upload them to NotebookLM and use it as your study buddy. You can ask questions, walk through diagrams, and let the model help you understand them like a coach.

What’s your favorite paper that should’ve made this list? 👇

Had a wonderful day at TechCrunch Disrupt.
I hosted two roundtable discussions with startups where we dove deep into prototyping, fine-tuning, and evaluations for generative AI models and applications.

One big takeaway stood out:
Many teams are still heavily relying on proprietary models, and their biggest reason is user experience.

That insight genuinely surprised me. As someone leading Developer Relations, one of my core goals is to make open-weight (open-source) models just as seamless to use as proprietary ones.

I truly believe that open source offers unmatched customization and optimization. And the fact that open models are now benchmarking at par with closed-source systems tells you how far this ecosystem has come.

My belief is that the next wave of AI adoption won’t be about a single model, it’ll be compound AI:
→ A mix of models working together
→ Tuned with proprietary data
→ Customized beyond prompt-level control

That’s where the future is heading, and that’s the world we’re building at Fireworks, where open models are powerful, practical, and production-ready.

--- Post 29 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 1,153 reactions, 96 comments, 166 reposts (Score: 7,057)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7355630845778452480

If you’re serious about building AI agents, start here 👇

To design agents that actually reason, plan, and act. You need to understand the spectrum of agent architectures.

Here are 9 types of AI agents every AI engineer should know:

💡 1. Simple Reflex Agents
React to current input using rule-based logic (IF-THEN).
→ No memory or learning.
Example: A thermostat that turns on if temp < 20°C.

💡 2. Model-Based Reflex Agents
Use an internal model of the world to infer hidden state.
→ Enable smarter decisions in partially observable settings.
Example: A robot navigating a known room layout to avoid collisions.

💡 3. Goal-Based Agents
Act to achieve defined objectives.
→ Involve search, planning, and pathfinding.
Example: Navigation system choosing optimal route to destination.

💡 4. Utility-Based Agents
Maximize a utility function (e.g. safety, speed, efficiency).
→ Handle trade-offs between competing outcomes.
Example: A self-driving car balancing passenger comfort with urgency.

💡 5. Learning Agents
Improve via feedback—using RL, supervised learning, or both.
→ Adapt and evolve over time.
Example: Chess agent refining tactics through self-play.

💡 6. Multi-Agent Systems (MAS)
Multiple agents interact, collaborate, or compete.
→ Used in swarm robotics, distributed simulations, and games.
Example: Financial agents negotiating trades in a simulated economy.

💡 7. Agentic AI Systems (LLM-based agents)
This is where it gets powerful.
→ Built on large language models + tools + memory + control flow.
→ Can decompose tasks, call APIs, search docs, invoke sub-agents.
Examples: Autogen, CrewAI, LangGraph.

💡 8. Embodied Agents
Agents with a physical presence—robots, drones, etc.
→ Interact with the real world via sensors and actuators.
Example: Boston Dynamics Spot navigating stairs autonomously.

💡 9. Cognitive & Conversational Agents
Human-facing agents designed for natural language interaction.
→ Chatbots, virtual assistants, AI tutors.
Examples: ChatGPT, Alexa, Claude.

If you’re an engineer, don’t just study these, build them. Get hands-on with open-source agent stacks:

→ Autogen (multi-agent orchestration):
https://lnkd.in/d36cU42f
→ CrewAI (LLM agents with roles + memory):
https://lnkd.in/dHBCPmkX
→ LangGraph (agentic control flow, built on LangChain):
https://docs.langgraph.dev

Start with their cookbooks or clone a working repo.
The fastest way to understand agents… is to ship one.

--- Post 30 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 1,188 reactions, 102 comments, 5 reposts (Score: 6,970)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7358297546110205952

I am an accidental creator, and who knew I would cross 100k on Instagram 🫢 
When I started on LinkedIn 10 years ago, it was all about sharing my thoughts, connecting with people in the ML space, collaborating on research projects, and just putting my work out there. 

Weirdly, I realized that I started building a huge community (what is now over 500k). In last few years, as the platform grew into a creator ecosystem- well, I became a "creator". And honestly, I have loved being one. 

I wanted to motivate more people to get in the data & ML space, make learning fun, and most importantly reach newer audience who are not on LinkedIn and democratize AI- so obviously I turned to Instagram, and started creating micro-learning videos on AI.

Fast forward to now- 100K followers on Instagram.
But the truth? The number isn’t what I’m proud of. It’s the lessons I’ve learned by showing up every day.

→ 1. Niches are overrated, angles are underrated.
People don’t follow you just because you post about AI. They follow you because you have a unique way of explaining it. Your clarity. Your analogies. Your POV. That’s the edge.

→ 2. Hooks matter more than you think.
You have 1-2 seconds to make someone stop scrolling. I started treating the first line of every reel like a startup pitch- clear, urgent, human.

→ 3. Your face, voice, and perspective build trust.
The highest-performing content wasn’t fancy. It was me, unscripted, explaining something I cared about. The more real I was, the more people resonated.

→ 4. High-signal content > high-effort production.
I used to think I needed perfect lighting and edits. Nope. Some of my best videos were one-takes on the couch. If the message hits, the rest doesn’t matter.

→ 5. Learning doesn't have to be boring.
I started mixing humor, trends, storytelling, and analogies. That’s how learning sticks.

→ 6. Motivation is great, but discipline keeps you going.
Some posts flop. Some go nowhere. You can’t let either define you. What helped me was having a system- batching, planning, showing up anyway.

→ 7. Track your content like a product.
Every week, I review what worked. What got saved? Shared? DMed? I treat content like a feedback loop, test, iterate, learn.

→ 8. Templates aren’t cringe- they’re clarity.
The more I consumed content, the more I learned how to communicate better. Using a proven structure doesn’t water down your voice, it helps people understand it.

→ 9. DMs and comments are gold.
Likes are nice, but the real feedback comes from DMs. That’s where people ask questions, share struggles, and tell you what actually helped.

→ 10. Create for yourself, but listen to your audience.
If you only share what you love, you might miss what they need. If you only serve what they want, you burn out. The sweet spot? A balance. Ask your audience what they’re stuck on. 

If you’ve been thinking about putting your thoughts out there-just start.
Don’t let overthinking get in the way.
Find your style. Share your voice. Go.

What an unforgettable weekend at Kennedy Space Center, watching the Starcloud-1 payload lift off aboard a SpaceX Falcon 9. 

Huge congratulations to the Starcloud team for pulling off such a bold first step toward orbiting AI factories.

ICYDK, Starcloud is a Redmond-based startup reimagining the future of data centers, not on Earth but in space. Their first orbiter, StarCloud-1, carries a powerful NVIDIA H100 GPU into orbit, marking the first time these chips have left Earth.

AI workloads are growing faster than our infrastructure can keep up. We’re running out of land, power, and even fresh water to cool data centers. StarCloud’s vision is simple but groundbreaking. Take compute to space, where solar energy is endless, cooling happens naturally, and scaling isn’t bound by geography.

By building data centers in orbit, they can process massive Earth-observation and AI workloads directly in space, sending only the insights back to Earth. This approach reduces resource strain, latency, and environmental impact. It’s one of those rare ideas that feels both futuristic and inevitable.

Standing there, watching Falcon 9 climb through the clouds, knowing it carried the first H100s into orbit, was surreal. The tech, the courage, and the mission behind it reminded me why I love being part of this ecosystem.

I’m so glad I met Philip Johnston when he was in the YC cohort and had the chance to invest in the team early on. We also recorded a super interesting podcast diving deep into this vision, so stay tuned, I’ll be sharing that soon.

Only upwards and upwards. 🚀

============================================================

--- Post 33 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 1,252 reactions, 46 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 6,738)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7385468411952222208

One of the traits I’m most proud of is something I wish more young girls were encouraged to have: the habit of questioning authority.

Growing up, many of us were taught to listen to our fathers, brothers, teachers, and bosses but rarely to ourselves. We were conditioned to follow the loudest or most confident voice in the room instead of our own.

I didn’t grow up like that because my mom never raised me that way.

She was bread-winner of our family, balancing work and home on a tight budget. But she never once said, “Do as I say.” Every decision was a conversation. She’d share her thoughts, listen to mine, and always end with, “Do what you think is right.”

That sentence shaped everything about who I became. It made me curious and confident, and it taught me to challenge rules even when it made others uncomfortable.

When I decided to move abroad for higher education, almost everyone around me questioned it. Some even said, “Why don’t you just get married instead?” But my mom stood by me. She wanted me to be independent, educated, and unafraid to think for myself.

That kind of freedom to think, to choose, and to question is what builds real confidence. It’s what helps women become decision-makers in their own lives instead of followers in someone else’s story.

If there’s one thing I want every girl reading this to remember, it’s this: you don’t owe obedience to anyone who tries to decide your path. You owe it to yourself to think, to question, and to choose.

We’ve spent years teaching boys to be bold. It’s time we teach girls to be decisive. That’s how we change the narrative.

============================================================

I’ve always been passionate about teaching, but for me, teaching has never been about knowing everything. It’s about learning, unlearning, and growing together.

When I first started sharing on LinkedIn, I was a student in India trying to learn from people across the world. I wanted to understand how they thought, what they built, and how they navigated their careers. Those early conversations gave me perspective and shaped who I am today.

Over time, teaching became a habit. It keeps me curious. It keeps me accountable. It pushes me to read, learn, and stay updated so that I can help others feel more confident in this fast-changing AI world.

Helping someone gain clarity about their next step or seeing them upskill because of something I shared is genuinely one of the most rewarding feelings.

Around three years ago, that same passion led me to topmate.io. I still remember speaking with Dinesh and Ankit when they were just starting out. Through Topmate, I’ve hosted workshops, webinars, shared digital resources, and mentored hundreds of professionals. It’s been one of my favorite platforms to connect, teach, and give back to the community.

Today, I’m grateful to be featured among the Top 100 creators on Topmate. But more than that, I’m proud of what this journey represents, that teaching can be your way of learning, that giving back can be your path to growth.

Happy learning ❤️

--- Post 38 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 1,044 reactions, 53 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 5,768)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7370272220314558464

In the last few years, I’ve made one major shift: I protect my energy like it’s my most valuable asset.

When I was starting out in my career, I said yes to everything, and honestly, that wasn’t a mistake.

✅ I needed to explore
✅ I needed to experiment
✅ I needed to learn what I didn’t want before I could figure out what I did

That phase helped me grow faster than I ever imagined.
But I’ve learned that what gets you here doesn’t always get you there.

There’s a point in your career where the game changes.
It’s not about saying yes to more - it’s about saying yes to the right things.

💡Think of it like exploration vs. exploitation in decision theory:
→ In the early phase, you say yes to a lot - because you’re exploring what fits, what excites you, what builds your skill stack
→ But as you grow deeper into your craft, your leadership, or your trajectory - it’s time to exploit what works and focus your energy

Here’s what I’ve learned:

1️⃣ Energy is the real productivity unlock
If you don’t have energy, it doesn’t matter how well you plan your day, you won’t be able to show up with clarity or momentum.

What’s helped me:
→ Surrounding myself with people who add energy, not drain it
→ Avoiding conversations or dynamics that feel heavy or unproductive
→ Designing my calendar around when I do my best thinking (and protecting that time)
→ Letting go of things that feel mentally noisy but don’t move me forward

When your energy is protected, everything from problem-solving to communication improves.

2️⃣ Productivity ≠ longer hours

Working more hours isn’t a signal of effectiveness, it’s often a sign of scattered priorities or lack of focus.

What’s helped me:
→ Prioritizing deep, focused work blocks over reactive multitasking
→ Treating rest as part of the workflow, not a reward after burnout
→ Building in short breaks to reset, especially between context switches
→ Letting go of the need to “fill” the day, and instead asking: What actually moves the needle?

3️⃣ Alignment > approval
Not every opportunity is meant for you, and trying to please everyone eventually dilutes your direction.

What’s helped me:
→ Asking whether an opportunity aligns with where I want to go
→ Choosing projects that energize me, even if they’re not the most popular ones
→ Walking away from things that don’t feel like a good values fit
→ Remembering that saying no is sometimes the clearest sign of focus

You don’t need to prove your value everywhere. You need to be intentional about where you grow.

So no, it’s not wrong to say yes to everything - it’s just a question of phase.
If you’re in the explore phase, say yes.
If you’re in the refine phase, protect your yes.

That awareness alone can change the trajectory of your growth.

Happy learning ❤️

============================================================

--- Post 39 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 953 reactions, 86 comments, 18 reposts (Score: 5,661)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7386542123199737856

🚨BREAKING NEWS: OpenAI just launched their new AI browser, ChatGPT Atlas, and I got early access to it.

After having testing if for several hours- first take is that I love it. I can instantly see all my copy-paste and jumping around tabs go away. 

When I was replying to emails, I didn’t have to switch tabs to ChatGPT. I just asked Atlas, “make this reply sound more concise,” and it edited inline, inside Gmail. Then I was reviewing a document, and instead of copying text over, I asked it for a summary right there. It understood the page context instantly.

Next, I tried something more complex. I asked Atlas to plan a one-week trip to Alaska over the holidays. It pulled up flight options, hotels, and even built a full itinerary, all in one flow, no spreadsheets or extra tabs. I also asked it to help me meal prep and order the groceries for me on instacart- did a pretty good job with just a little feedback (checkout the screenshots below)👇 

Atlas integrates contextual grounding (it knows the page you’re on), browser memory (it remembers what matters and can resurface past work), and inline inference (you can write, edit, or ask questions directly on the page). It’s powered by the same ChatGPT stack but natively optimized for browsing.

Then there’s Agent Mode which currently in preview for Plus and Pro users. This is where it starts to act. You can say, “book the hotel from my Alaska search” or “compare these two articles,” and it handles it for you. Early days, but this is what agentic workflows will look like once embedded directly in everyday tools.

What I also like: OpenAI has made memory optional. You can clear browsing data, use incognito mode, or control which sites it can see. And yes, your ChatGPT memory syncs across the browser. That means the context you’ve built in ChatGPT carries into your browsing session.

If you’ve built Custom GPTs, they’re now accessible inside Atlas too. I can connect it to my AishGPT! Basically your content writer, research assistant, or code reviewer or any other customGPT you use can live in your browser - not as a separate chat window, but as a co-pilot that interacts with whatever’s on your screen.

📺 You can test it out for yourself: https://lnkd.in/d2heMDuR

============================================================

--- Post 42 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 948 reactions, 81 comments, 23 reposts (Score: 5,596)
Media: video
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419919903358488576

hashtag
#WaPoPartner
The Washington Post recently launched Ask The Post, and it’s a good example of how AI should be used.

Ask The Post is an AI chatbot that lets users ask questions and receive answers generated exclusively from The Post’s reporting. 

It doesn't invent information, it summarizes what their journalists have already reported, with clear sources in every response, and will admit when it doesn’t know the answer.

Why this matters: 
✦ It prioritizes understanding over engagement 
✦ It’s built for serious questions, not surface-level answers 
✦ It’s transparent about where information comes from

It’s AI with clear boundaries and built on human expertise, which tends to be far more trustworthy and accurate. 

I hope more AI products take this approach. 


hashtag
#ad

============================================================

--- Post 44 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 936 reactions, 65 comments, 106 reposts (Score: 5,542)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7373385181547724800

If you’re an aspiring ML or AI engineer,
here are 5 projects you can build right now to get your hands dirty with the latest tools, and actually understand how things work end-to-end 👇🏽

🛠️ 1. Build an ML Pipeline from Scratch
Project: Predict which students are at risk of dropping out
→ Pull attendance + grades → clean in Pandas/Polars
→ Train with LightGBM → track with MLflow
→ Deploy via FastAPI + Docker → push to AWS Lambda
→ Set up CI/CD with GitHub Actions
💡 This is a full ML lifecycle in one project.

💬 2. Build a RAG Chatbot
Project: A chatbot that answers questions from course notes
→ Chunk + embed using LlamaIndex / Cohere / VoyageAI
→ Store in Weaviate or FAISS
→ Use an open-source LLM like Mistral or Qwen
→ UI in Gradio or Streamlit
💡 Great hands-on intro to how GenAI apps actually work.

🧠 3. Fine-Tune an LLM for a Domain
Project: A medical or finance assistant
→ Collect PubMed papers or SEC filings
→ Fine-tune with QLoRA/DPO
→ Start with Mistral or DeepSeek Coder
→ Evaluate on factuality + accuracy
→ Deploy with Modal or Fireworks AI
💡 Helps you go deeper into foundation model adaptation.

🚨 4. Monitor for Model Drift
Project: A fraud detection system that stays effective post-launch
→ Serve with BentoML
→ Track drift using Evidently AI or WhyLabs
→ Use Airflow/Prefect for retraining
→ Visualize everything in Grafana
💡 Most ML projects die post-deployment, this one shows you can maintain.

🍽️ 5. Build a Multimodal AI App
Project: Snap a photo of food → get nutrition + recipes
→ Use Florence-2 or CLIP for vision embeddings
→ Add Qwen-VL or LLaVA for reasoning
→ Build the UI with Streamlit
💡 You’ll learn how to mix modalities and push boundaries.

None of these are “easy,” but that’s the point.
They’ll teach you the actual workflow, data prep → modeling → deployment → monitoring.
And they’ll stand out when someone looks at your GitHub or portfolio.

If you end up building one of these (or already have), drop a link in the comments, I’d love to see it 🙌🏽

〰️〰️〰️
Follow me (Aishwarya Srinivasan) for more AI insight and subscribe to my Substack to find more in-depth blogs and weekly updates in AI: https://lnkd.in/dpBNr6Jg

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

There is a quiet moment every November when life slows down just enough for reflection. I never grew up celebrating Thanksgiving. Back home in India, I didn’t even know what it meant. But after moving to the US, watching my friends celebrate, watching sitcoms where families gathered around food and gratitude, I started understanding the deeper meaning behind it.

A moment to pause.
A moment to look around.
A moment to look back.

This year, gratitude feels a little different for me. A little heavier in the best possible way.

I feel grateful for a life where I get to wake up every day and do work I genuinely love. AI is chaotic and intense and fast. Yet it gives me energy. It gives me purpose. And I feel lucky to be part of this ecosystem during a time when everything is shifting.

I feel grateful for the people who hold me steady.
A mom who pushed me when the world expected her to play it safe. A mom who defied every norm to carve space for me to choose ambition without apology.

A husband who is involved in my life in the most beautiful way supportive is honestly an understatement. He is a true partner in every single thing I do. He listens to every idea, every frustration, every new plan I dream up and he backs me with a kind of belief that strengthens my own. Chinnu amma and Chinnu appa who patiently listen to my ten year entrepreneurial plans and cheer for me with the kind of warmth only family can give.

Mentors and colleagues who let me thrive creatively who challenge me in the right ways who make every room I walk into feel lighter and more possible.

I feel grateful for health. For a body I get to take care of. For the time I carve out to move, to eat well, to nurture myself. It took years to realize that taking care of your body is also taking care of your dreams.

And I feel grateful for the little girl I used to be the one who sat with a notebook and wrote her goals on every page the one who kept believing she would get there someday. If I could talk to her today, I would tell her that she is doing great and that her stubborn belief in herself was worth it.

Thanksgiving may not be something I grew up with, but it has become a reminder to pause and appreciate the rarest gift of all the good people around you. People who are genuinely supportive, genuinely positive, and genuinely rooting for you.

I am grateful for all of it.
And grateful for everyone who has been part of my journey so far.

If you are reading this, I hope you take a moment to celebrate the smallest wins, the unexpected joys, and the people who make your life feel expansive.

============================================================

This year’s Snowflake BUILD reminded me why I love being at the intersection of data and AI.

There’s a quiet revolution happening, not just in models or infrastructure, but in how data and AI are starting to think together.

Snowflake is redefining what that connection looks like: turning data clouds into intelligence layers that can reason, act, and learn.

Here are my five takeaways from BUILD 2025:

1️⃣ AI is getting context-aware.
The launch of the Managed MCP Server was a clear step toward connecting reasoning with memory. It bridges agents, data, and external systems seamlessly, a sign that agentic AI is finally moving from concept to production.

2️⃣ Cortex AI SQL is where AI meets analytics.
Forget prompt-engineering; this is about asking real business questions in natural language and getting answers grounded in governed data. It’s basically- AI meeting everyday decision-making.

3️⃣ Cortex Agents show what “agentic” really means.
They’re not chatbots — they’re teammates. They can chain tasks, analyze results, and act across workflows, bringing true autonomy into enterprise systems.

4️⃣ The Gen2 Warehouses make AI-native infrastructure real.
Everything, right from compute to latency, is being rebuilt for intelligent applications. It’s the foundation of an AI-ready enterprise

5️⃣ The biggest shift is cultural.
Teams are no longer adding AI; they’re designing with it. Data, ML, and product are finally collaborating as one.

What stood out to me most was the clarity of purpose: The future of AI isn’t about replacing intelligence, it’s about amplifying it through data.

--- Post 49 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 830 reactions, 55 comments, 21 reposts (Score: 4,742)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7409456919750545408

If you are an AI engineer trying to deeply understand how multi-agent systems actually behave in production, this is worth your time.

I recently went through a book that Galileo shared with me, and I really appreciated how grounded and practical their thinking around multi-agent systems is. 

If you are building multi-agent systems today, here are a few best practices that stood out to me:

✦ Use multi-agent architectures only when tasks are truly independent and parallelizable. Otherwise, coordination cost will outweigh the benefits.

✦ Decompose for specialization, not novelty. Focused agents with narrow scope reduce hallucinations and improve reliability.

✦ Treat context as fragile and expensive. Aggressively prune, and rely on retrieval-based memory instead of passing long histories between agents.

✦ Avoid loading all tools upfront. Dynamically surface only the tools relevant for a given step to prevent tool confusion.

✦ Be cautious with write-heavy workflows. Multi-agent systems work best for read-heavy tasks and break down quickly with shared state mutations.

✦ Choose architecture intentionally. Centralized designs prioritize consistency, decentralized designs prioritize resilience.

✦ Design orchestration layers to be removable. As base models improve, you should be able to collapse agents into simpler systems without rewriting business logic.

✦ Measure outcomes, not just model metrics. Track task completion and tool selection quality to find real failure modes.

I strongly believe that multi-agent systems are powerful, but only when engineered with restraint and clarity.

Thank you to the Galileo team for sending this over. I found it very thoughtful, practical, and very aligned with how serious AI systems should be built.

============================================================

A lot of people ask me, what’s the roadmap to become an AI engineer?

It’s not just about finishing a few ML courses, it’s about mastering the fundamentals, applying them in real projects, and building the skills to ship AI systems into production.

Here’s a step-by-step path that works in 2025.

📓Step 0 – Build a Strong Technical Foundation
→ Learn Python deeply (AI’s primary language) plus working knowledge of C++, Java, or R.
→ Master data structures, algorithms, and software engineering principles.
→ Strengthen math for AI: linear algebra, calculus, probability, and statistics.

📌 Tip: Code along with tutorials and build small utilities like dataset parsers, algorithm implementations, or math simulations.


📗Step 1 – Learn ML & AI Fundamentals
→ Understand core ML concepts: supervised vs. unsupervised learning, regression, classification, clustering.
→ Explore deep learning: CNNs, RNNs, Transformers, NLP, generative AI.
→ Work hands-on with PyTorch, TensorFlow, and scikit-learn.

📌 Tip: Build a neural network from scratch using only NumPy to really understand how backpropagation works.


📘Step 2 – Apply Knowledge Through Projects
→ Start with small, scoped projects: image classification, NLP chatbots, recommendation engines.
→ Use public datasets from UCI, Kaggle, or Hugging Face Datasets.
→ Contribute to open-source AI projects or Kaggle competitions.

📌 Starter Ideas:
→ Sentiment analysis with real-time inference
→ Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) PDF summarizer
→ Price prediction model with explainable AI dashboards


📕Step 3 – Learn Data Engineering, MLOps & Deployment
→ Practice data preprocessing and visualization with pandas, NumPy, matplotlib.
→ Learn to deploy models on AWS Sagemaker, GCP Vertex AI, or Azure ML.
→ Master MLOps: CI/CD pipelines, retraining workflows, monitoring, model version control.
📌 Tip: Build an end-to-end pipeline - from raw data ingestion to a deployed model API - using Docker + FastAPI.


📙 Step 4 – Specialize in an AI Domain
→ Choose a focus: Computer Vision, NLP, Generative AI, Reinforcement Learning, or Robotics.
→ Read research papers, replicate results, and keep up with top AI conferences (NeurIPS, ICML).
📌 Tip: Maintain a “replication repo” where you implement recent papers in your chosen field.


📔Step 5 – Build a Portfolio & Network
→ Showcase your projects on GitHub with clear READMEs and deployment instructions.
→ Write technical blogs, create short videos, or speak at meetups about your projects.
→ Join AI communities (Slack, Discord, LinkedIn), attend hackathons, and network with peers.

If you’re starting today, pick one problem you care about, find a dataset, and commit to shipping a working MVP in 3 weeks. That’s how you start building momentum ❤️

--- Post 55 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 771 reactions, 36 comments, 14 reposts (Score: 4,243)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7404554852065779712

Just finished Designing Autonomous AI by Kence Anderson.
 
This book defined the organizing structure of multi-agent systems before they became the movement they are today and introduced a term no one is talking about but should be: Machine Teaching.

Three ideas stood out most:
✦ Programming is not the same as teaching:
Programming produces automation that works when conditions don’t change. Teaching provides autonomy that can adapt when conditions shift.
When you teach toward a goal, you break down the skills to achieve it, then build those skills through practice. That is how you develop real capability.

✦ Every goal carries a tradeoff:
Intelligent systems never optimize one thing in isolation. They balance accuracy, latency, cost, performance exploration, and exploitation.
No single model or algorithm can resolve all of these tensions at once. Progress comes from developing individual skills that work together. Each one moves the system toward expertise. They become reliable design patterns for how decisions get made.

✦ Autonomy becomes modular:
The book makes a compelling case that autonomy behaves more like a modular system than a monolithic brain. You combine skills, you orchestrate them, and you compose them into agents that perceive, decide, and adapt together.

You may have systems that already perform a single skill reliably. You don’t need to discard them, you can (and should) integrate them into the larger architecture of your multi-agent system.

That is what the future of autonomy looks like:
✦ Algorithms that deliver precision
✦ Skill-based orchestration that keeps systems adaptable
✦ Design patterns that bring structure
✦ Simulation and learning loops that reveal the edge cases
✦ Human judgment for guiding the full process

Autonomy is not about removing people. It’s about building systems that learn from us, reason with us, and improve safely before they meet the real world.

If you're an AI professional, whether hands-on or not, I would recommend this book :)

============================================================

Over the last few years, I’ve coached and spoken to so many professionals about LinkedIn. Students, engineers, founders, senior leaders. And I keep hearing the same hesitation.

“I don’t want to become a LinkedIn influencer.”

And I always say the same thing back.

Using LinkedIn intentionally is not about becoming an influencer. It’s about having a voice, building credibility, and staying visible to the people you actually want to work with.

Here are the three most common mistakes I see people make.

⚠️ Mistake #1: Showing up randomly

People post whenever they feel like it. No structure. No rhythm. Sometimes they disappear for weeks.
The fix is not posting every day. The fix is clarity. Decide what you want to talk about, who you want to talk to, and how often you realistically want to show up. You control the frequency, the format, and the depth. Consistency can look very different for different people.

⚠️ Mistake #2: Treating LinkedIn like a job-search button

A lot of people only start networking when they need something. A role. A referral. An intro.
That rarely works. Relationships take time. The strongest opportunities come from staying in touch, engaging with people’s work, and adding value long before you need anything in return.

⚠️ Mistake #3: Overthinking authenticity

People think they need to sound “expert enough” or polished enough to post. So they either copy what others are doing or don’t post at all.
The truth is, authenticity doesn’t mean oversharing. It means sharing what you know, what you’re learning, and what you’re seeing from your own experience. You don’t need to impress everyone. You just need to resonate with the right people.

When done right, LinkedIn helps you build influence with the exact audience you care about. And that influence opens up career paths, collaborations, and opportunities you genuinely cannot plan for in advance.

A lot of people get stuck because they don’t know how to start, how to structure their content, or how to stay consistent without feeling fake. That’s exactly why I put together the LinkedIn Growth Playbook.

It’s a full brain dump of everything I’ve learned over the last 10 years. What worked, what didn’t, how the platform evolved, how opportunities came in, and yes, even how monetization works.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026

Perplexity recently dropped a 44-page white paper on how they use AI at work.
It’s an absolute gem. 

It’s a mental model for how AI actually changes your career trajectory.

Here’s a quick TL;DR 👇 

✦ Work happens in three layers
- Most people apply AI randomly. The guide argues order matters:
- Block distractions first (admin, busywork, context switching)
- Scale yourself next (research, synthesis, creation at team-level)
- Then aim for results (revenue, promotions, deal velocity)

✦ Context switching kills curiosity
- We get interrupted every ~11 minutes.
- The fix is not “focus harder.”
- Use AI to unify workflows instead of juggling tools
- Eliminate transitions so deep thinking survives

✦ Stop completing tasks. Start designing systems
- Don’t ask AI to “write an email.”
- Design workflows where research flows into pricing, which flows into sales messaging
- You act like a manager while AI carries context across steps

✦ Assistant vs Agent matters
- Assistant = understanding (summaries, explanations, fact checks)
- Agent = doing (multi-step actions, browser control, execution)
- Knowing when to switch modes is a real leverage point

✦ AI lets you solve out-of-domain problems
- Borrow ideas across industries
- Run deep research
- Produce exec-level outputs as a team-of-one

✦ AI is a career accelerator
Use it to:
- Document your real impact objectively
- Generate performance reviews from data
- Build smarter lead gen, outreach, and win-loss analysis

The mental shift I loved most:
- Traditional work = bricklayer
- You carry bricks, mix mortar, build the wall yourself.
- AI-native work = architect + foreman

If you’re serious about using AI beyond productivity, this white paper is worth the read.

If you’re getting started in the AI engineering space and want to understand how to actually build an AI agent, here’s a structured way to think about it.

Over the last several months, I’ve been building, testing, and teaching agentic AI systems, and I realized most people jump straight into frameworks like LangGraph, CrewAI, or AutoGen without fully understanding the system design mindset behind them.

Here’s a 12-step framework I put together to help you design your first AI agent, end-to-end.

🧩 From defining the problem to scaling it reliably.

→ Start with Problem Formulation & Use Case Selection - clearly define the goal and validate that it needs agentic behavior (reasoning, tool use, autonomy).

→ Map the User Journey & Workflow - understand where the agent fits into human or system loops.

→ Build your Knowledge & Context Strategy - design a RAG or memory pipeline to give your agent structured access to information.

→ Choose your Model & Architecture - open-source, fine-tuned, or multimodal depending on the use case.

→ Define Agent Roles & Topology - whether it’s a single-agent planner or a multi-agent ecosystem.

→ Layer on Tooling & Integration - secure APIs, function calling, and monitoring.

→ Then move into Prototyping, Guardrails, Benchmarking, Deployment, and Scaling - optimizing for accuracy, latency, and cost.

Each layer matters because building an AI agent isn’t about wiring APIs, it’s about engineering autonomy with accountability.

Now that you have this template, pick a use case that excites you - maybe something that improves your own productivity or automates a workflow you repeat daily. Or look online for open project ideas on AI agents, and just start building.

🚨 OpenAI just dropped its first open-weight models in 6 years: gpt-oss-20B and gpt-oss-120B.

Both models use a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture with 32 experts (20B) and 128 experts (120B), with 4 active per token. 

They support long-context (up to 131K tokens), built-in tool use (browser, code interpreter), and variable reasoning levels (low, medium, high).

The models were post-trained using reinforcement learning techniques similar to o3, and support structured multi-turn conversations using a new Harmony Chat Format.

In benchmark evaluations:
→ gpt-oss-120B performs on par with o4-mini across tasks like AIME, GPQA, and MMLU
→ gpt-oss-20B performs competitively despite being significantly smaller

I put together a short video walkthrough covering the architecture and benchmarks, with visuals.

Both models are now available on Fireworks AI for inference and experimentation!

If you’re preparing for your next AI engineering interview, there’s one topic that separates beginners from builders: Context Engineering.

It’s not about writing better prompts, it’s about building better thinking pipelines.

LLMs can’t reason in isolation. They depend on engineered context – retrieval (RAG) to find relevant data, summarization and pruning to stay within memory limits, query rewriting to clarify intent, and memory to sustain continuity.

Every GenAI system that scales relies on context as its backbone.

I get asked this all the time by aspiring AI engineers: “What projects should I build that actually matter?”

So I put together 5 portfolio ideas that reflect what real AI teams are building in 2026.

None of these are half-day projects. They are not meant to be.

A strong portfolio project should stretch you. It should challenge your analytical thinking. It should force you to think in terms of system design, trade-offs, evaluation, latency, cost, and production constraints. If it feels easy, you are probably staying at the surface.

When you build, go deep.

- Do not just generate code and move on. 
- Define the problem clearly. 
- Choose the dataset/eval set intentionally. 
- Justify the model. 
- Explain your architecture decisions. 
- Document your trade-offs. 
- Add evaluation. Add monitoring. 
- Treat it like something you would ship in a real company.

Then host it properly. GitHub, Hugging Face Spaces, wherever you prefer.

And write about it.

Share:
- What problem you solved
- What dataset you used
- What models you chose and why
- Your architecture diagram
- Tools and frameworks you selected
- What did not work

That write-up is often more impressive than the repo itself.

If you build one of these and share it here, tag me. I genuinely enjoy reading well-thought-out engineering work.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026

If you are getting started in AI engineering in 2026, here is something that took me a while to fully appreciate.

GenAI apps do not run on one database.
They run on a mix of databases, each doing a very different job.

You do not need to go deep into all of these right away. But you should at least know what exists and why it exists.

From a GenAI point of view, these are the main database types worth knowing about:

✦ Vector databases
✦ Graph databases
✦ Document databases
✦ Relational databases
✦ Key value stores
✦ Time series databases
✦ Streaming databases
✦ Data warehouses
✦ Lakehouses
✦ Feature stores
✦ OLAP databases
✦ Hybrid transactional analytical databases
✦ Knowledge bases
✦ Multimodal databases
✦ Agent memory stores

Most beginner confusion comes from thinking there is a single “best” database for GenAI.

In practice, real systems usually combine a few of these, depending on what you are building.

You do not need to master them all.
You just need to recognize them when you see them in the wild.

That awareness alone makes learning everything else much easier.

Seven years ago, I walked into IBM as an intern on the Data Science Elite Team, led by Dr. Seth Dobrin.

I still remember chatting with him in the coffee pantry on my first week. I had a dozen ideas and zero clarity on what to focus on. Seth told me something that stayed with me ever since - “follow your interests and play to your strengths.”

That’s how I ended up choosing reinforcement learning for machine trading as my first-ever project. That one decision shaped how I think about experimentation and learning to this day.

Over the years, Seth has continued to be my mentor, and now, a friend. He’s one of those rare leaders who’s genuinely approachable and always available for his team. It’s one thing to listen to people’s ideas, and another to actually create opportunities for them to grow. Seth has always done the latter.

I met him again recently at the Masters of Scale Summit - that’s usually where we end up catching up, at conferences. Every time we meet, it feels like picking up right where we left off.

What inspires me most is that he’s still building, still innovating, now with Arya Labs. 

Traditional AI approaches face a trade-off: general-purpose models like LLMs are flexible but prone to hallucination, while physics-based simulations are accurate but rigid and expensive. Arya Labs eliminates this trade-off through a Deterministic Reality Architecture - blending the flexibility of AI with the rigor of physics-based systems.

It’s amazing to see how the spirit of innovation and curiosity he fostered in his team back then continues to reflect in what he’s building today.

Grateful for mentors who shape not just your skills, but your mindset.

--- Post 69 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 604 reactions, 45 comments, 94 reposts (Score: 3,658)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7403527586133389312

If you are preparing for a data science or ML engineering interview, keep this cheat sheet handy.

Most interviews today expect you to be comfortable with modern AI toolkits. But understanding how models actually learn is not optional. It is a core skill. You will often be asked how you would solve a specific problem, and not every problem is a generative AI problem. That is why revisiting these foundational learning paradigms is still essential.

This visual breaks down the three core ways traditional ML models learn from data:

✦ Supervised learning focuses on learning from labeled data to make accurate predictions.
✦ Unsupervised learning uncovers hidden structure, clusters, or patterns when no labels are available.
✦ Reinforcement learning teaches an agent to interact with an environment and optimize long term reward.

So where do generative AI models fit in?

✦ For most large language models, the pre training phase uses self supervised learning. The model is trained on massive amounts of raw text and learns to predict missing or next tokens using the input itself as the supervision signal. There are no human created labels. 

✦ This is why self supervised learning is treated as a subset of unsupervised learning. After pre training, many models are fine tuned with supervised instruction data and sometimes with reinforcement learning from human feedback.

Before generative AI captured mainstream attention, we already had transformer based models like BERT and GPT 2 operating in this category. BERT used masked language modeling and next sentence prediction. GPT style models relied on next token prediction. These were early examples of self supervised generative modeling that laid the foundation for today’s LLMs.

If you are interviewing for any data or AI role, being able to explain these paradigms clearly will help you reason through real world scenarios and select the right approach. I will be sharing more interview focused resources soon.

✦✦✦

--- Post 71 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 573 reactions, 54 comments, 68 reposts (Score: 3,541)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7428606431181934592

As an AI engineer, LLM Twin is an important architecture to understand.

An LLM Twin refers to a system where a language model is designed to represent the knowledge, writing patterns, and decision logic of a specific person, team, or organization. The goal is consistency and reliability, not novelty.

At its core, an LLM Twin is built around how information flows. Think of it as 4 steps:

1️⃣ First, content is collected from real sources: blogs, internal documents, posts, code repositories, or knowledge bases. This data reflects how the person or organization actually thinks and communicates.

2️⃣ Next, the data is structured for two purposes.
One path prepares instruction-style examples that help the model learn how to respond.
Another path prepares retrieval data so the system can pull relevant context at inference time. This ensures responses stay grounded in real material.

3️⃣ Then comes training and evaluation. Models are fine-tuned where appropriate, tested against clear criteria, and versioned before deployment. This step looks very similar to traditional ML workflows, just adapted for language models.

4️⃣ Finally, at inference time, user requests are combined with retrieved context and passed through the deployed model. Monitoring is critical here: quality, latency, and cost all need to be observed continuously.

Where is this used in practice?

✦ Personal or creator assistants that help scale writing and communication
✦ Company-wide assistants that maintain a consistent product or brand voice
✦ Internal tools that answer questions based on code, docs, or prior decisions
✦ Support systems that reflect how experienced engineers respond to issues

Why should AI Engineers understand this?
This architecture reflects how GenAI systems are actually built and maintained. It forces you to think beyond prompts and focus on data quality, retrieval design, evaluation, and long-term system behavior.

My 2 cents 🤌
This is a GREAT topic to build a portfolio project around!! 

Credit: LLM Engineer’s Handbook by Paul Iusztin and Maxime Labonne. I really like how this book breaks down AI engineering clearly and goes deep into different GenAI system architectures without hand-waving.

============================================================
