“Too talkative.”

That was one of the most common remarks scribbled onto my early elementary school report cards. Despite excelling academically, I was constantly reminded that I was “too talkative.” 

In retrospect, I recognize this as one of the flaws in our educational system: an inability to view certain traits- such as a child’s curious, expressive nature, as strengths instead of distractions. 

Rather than channel my energy toward something constructive- like public speaking, debate, or creative projects, my teachers often treated my talkativeness as a behavior to be corrected.

The consequence of such repeated criticism? By the time I reached high school, I had become shy and hesitant to speak up. It wasn’t until my undergraduate years that I rediscovered my passion for public speaking, presenting research papers at conferences and engaging a larger audience. 

⭐️That journey illuminated a vital truth: the very trait once seen as a hindrance was, in fact, one of my greatest strengths.

“Your uniqueness is your superpower, let it shine.”

In a world where AI can rapidly gather facts and figures, the human qualities of creativity, authenticity, and empathy stand out more than ever. These traits- our quirks, passions, and perspectives, build real trust and foster meaningful connections. 

💡A mentor once told me, “If you can invest time to improve yourself, invest in enhancing the things you’re already good at.” That’s where you will truly differentiate yourself.

So, if anyone has ever made you feel you don’t fit the “norm,” remind yourself that what sets you apart is precisely what makes you remarkable. Lean into it. Cultivate it. Share it. Because that uniqueness is your superpower.

What’s your unique trait? Tell me in the comments below 👇 

Share this if it resonates with you ♻️❤️

--- Post 76 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 545 reactions, 62 comments, 43 reposts (Score: 3,431)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7375546180975448064

Most RAG pipelines today still follow a pretty straightforward setup:
→ Chunk the data
→ Embed it
→ Store in a vector DB
→ Retrieve top-k chunks at query time
→ Feed to the LLM

It works, but it’s brittle.

What often gets overlooked is the quality of those chunks, and how much context they carry.

Here’s a breakdown of how retrieval strategies can evolve, from basic setups to more context-aware and production-ready designs:

1️⃣ Standard RAG
– No context awareness, just pure similarity matching. Fast but shallow.

2️⃣ Contextual Retrieval Preprocessing
– Each chunk is enriched during preprocessing using prompt-based methods (e.g. with Claude). This helps situate the chunk within the broader document, drastically improving relevance at retrieval time without increasing runtime cost.

3️⃣ Combined Retrieval (Contextual + Rank Fusion)
– This approach layers semantic search, lexical matching (TF-IDF), and context-enriched chunks. It adds reranking at runtime to refine top-k selections and boosts performance even further.

If you’re building RAG applications in production, contextual preprocessing and intelligent reranking can make or break quality.

You don’t always need a bigger model. Sometimes, you just need better context.

🔥I’m also co-hosting a hands-on session next week: RAG – Towards Mastery with Arvind

We’ll go deep into:
- RAG as a memory layer for LLMs
- Advanced context engineering strategies
- Architectural trade-offs for scaling RAG
- Two live builds: multimodal RAG and Graph RAG

It’s a 3-hour deep dive for AI engineers and builders.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025

If you’re getting started in AI, you need a holistic understanding of how the entire ecosystem fits together, where each layer sits, how the toolkits connect, and how it all flows end to end.

Once you see how these layers interact, everything starts to make sense 👇

→ Foundation Models - The base layer of the stack. These are the massive models trained by labs like OpenAI (GPT), Meta (Llama), and Anthropic (Claude). They’re the engines that power the rest of the ecosystem.

→ Inference & Platforms - Once models exist, they need to be served efficiently. Inference providers like Fireworks AI, Hugging Face, AWS Bedrock, and Google Vertex AI make this possible. Some platforms go further, supporting fine-tuning, deployment, and monitoring, blending infrastructure with orchestration.

→ Frameworks - The developer toolkit layer. Frameworks like PyTorch and TensorFlow (for ML) or LangChain, LangGraph, and CrewAI (for GenAI and agents) help developers turn raw model capabilities into usable systems.

→ Tools & Integrations - The practical layer for builders. Libraries like Scikit-learn, Pandas, and Weights & Biases for classic ML and experiment tracking. LlamaIndex and vector databases (Pinecone, Weaviate) give LLMs memory. Streamlit and Gradio help you build quick interactive demos.

→ Applications & Products - The top layer, where end-users interact. From ChatGPT, Perplexity, and MidJourney to AI copilots inside Microsoft Office or customer support assistants, this is the visible layer powered by all the ones below.

→ Horizontal vs Vertical AI - Some apps are general-purpose (like ChatGPT or Notion AI), while others are domain-specific, built for healthcare, law, retail, or finance, solving specialized problems deeply.

Understanding this flow gives you clarity on what’s happening behind the scenes, how everything connects, and where your own skills can create the most impact.

--- Post 78 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 485 reactions, 83 comments, 40 reposts (Score: 3,335)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7358159919977545728

Prompt engineering is not dead, but it’s no longer enough. Context Engineering is what is filling the gaps 👇

If you’ve ever built a serious LLM workflow, you already know: most failures don’t come from bad prompts, they come from missing, outdated, or misformatted context.

That’s why I’ve shifted from obsessing over the perfect "prompt", to designing robust context systems around the model.

Andrej Karpathy famously tweeted- "+1 to context engineering, over prompt engineering"

Let’s talk about Context Engineering.

🔍 What is it?

Context engineering = building dynamic systems that deliver the right info, in the right format, at the right time so the model can actually accomplish the task.

It’s more than just phrasing. It’s about memory, retrieval, structured outputs, history, and tool awareness.

Context engineering is what allows the model to act coherently across long conversations, use retrieved knowledge intelligently, and collaborate with other agents/tools.

🧱 Four Pillars of Context Engineering:

→ Write: Persist info that lives outside the context window
✦ Scratchpads, logs, long-term memory

→ Select: Pull in only relevant context
✦ Redis retrieval, memory recall, tool specs

→ Compress: Summarize to save tokens
✦ Auto-trimming (like Claude), compact context windows

→ Isolate: Prevent interference between contexts
✦ Multi-agent sub-contexts, sandboxed processes

🔄 Lifecycle of Context Engineering:
Acquire → Process → Store → Retrieve → Update
It’s an entire system. Not a one-shot prompt.

🧠 Prompt vs. Context Engineering:

✦ Prompt Engineering = phrasing the instruction
✦ Context Engineering = building the system around the model (memory, retrieval, tools, workflows)

If you’re building agents, multi-turn apps, or systems with state, context engineering is non-negotiable.

⚙️ Some tools I use:
→ LangGraph / LangSmith for tracing + long-term state
→ LlamaIndex, vector DBs for retrieval
→ Orchestration libs for multi-agent workflows

🚫 Common pitfalls to avoid:
→ Overengineering too early
→ Garbage in, garbage out (poor validation)
→ Token bloat, context drift, or stale data
→ Ignoring security/privacy of memory layers

The future isn’t just about prompt engineering. It’s about designing intelligent context layers around your models.

Save this for your next AI system design session.

============================================================

--- Post 80 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 565 reactions, 28 comments, 88 reposts (Score: 3,281)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7407461214991065090

Building RAG systems in production is not about “just add a vector DB.”

Modern RAG is a stack, and thinking in layers is what separates toy demos from systems that actually scale.

Here’s a practical way to look at the RAG developer stack, based on what teams are using right now.

✦ LLMs (reasoning layer)
Your generator. Model choice impacts latency, cost, and instruction following.
GPT-5, Claude 3.5, Gemini 2.0, Llama 3.3, Qwen2.5, DeepSeek V3, Mistral Large, Grok
Closed or open, this is where reasoning quality lives.

✦ Frameworks / Orchestration (control layer)
Where RAG logic is composed and extended into agents.
LangChain, LangGraph, LlamaIndex, Haystack, Semantic Kernel, OpenAI Agents SDK, CrewAI
This layer controls prompting, tool use, memory, and multi-step flows.

✦ Vector Databases (retrieval layer)
Low-latency similarity search with filtering and hybrid retrieval.
Pinecone, Qdrant, Weaviate, Milvus, Chroma, pgvector
Production RAG depends heavily on indexing strategy and metadata design here.

✦ Data Extraction & Ingestion (source of truth layer)
Garbage in still means garbage out. This layer matters more than people think.
FireCrawl, Crawl4AI, LlamaParse, Docling, MegaParse, Unstructured
Clean chunking, layout awareness, and document structure are key.

✦ Open LLM Hosting / Inference (execution layer)
How your models actually run at scale.
vLLM, Together AI, Ollama, Groq, Hugging Face, Fireworks AI
This affects throughput, cold starts, and cost efficiency.

✦ Text Embeddings (semantic layer)
Everything retrieval depends on.
OpenAI, Cohere, Google, Voyage AI, BGE, Nomic, Fireworks AI
Embedding choice often matters more than model size.

✦ Evaluation & Observability (trust layer)
You cannot improve what you cannot measure.
Langfuse, DeepEval, Ragas, Giskard, LangSmith, TruLens
This is how you debug hallucinations, regressions, and retrieval failures.

Most of these tools are open source.
Most production issues show up at the boundaries between layers, not inside a single tool.

If you’re building RAG as part of real AI workflows, thinking in stacks instead of tools will save you months.

Just a few days ago, I wrapped up an energizing day at the Buildathon hosted by DeepLearning.AI and AI Fund in partnership with MongoDB at the Silicon Valley AI Hub in Menlo Park

It’s events like these that remind me why I enjoy being part of this community, the chance to see what people can rapidly build in a day, hear different perspectives, and think about where AI development is headed next.

Here are some of my key takeaways:

👉 AI-assisted coding is changing the tempo of engineering
The fireside chat between Tengyu Ma (MongoDB, Voyage AI by MongoDB) and Yaad Oren (SAP) really hit home. Instead of “AI will replace coding,” the more nuanced (and useful) lens is: AI is accelerating the pace of development. By eliminating boilerplate and reducing feedback loops, teams can ship faster while keeping quality high.

👉 Context is everything.
Voyage AI’s new model, voyage-context-3, stood out to me. Traditional embedding models force devs to slice content into tiny chunks and hope the meaning holds. This model flips that: it reads the whole document, then creates embeddings that already understand the context. It simplifies everything. You don’t need fancy chunking logic or special retrieval tricks, just plug it in and go.

👉 And then Andrew Ng dropped this gem:
“In the new AI era, the bottleneck isn’t the model- it’s the product cycle.”
That hit hard. The teams that will win aren’t the ones with the biggest models, they’re the ones that can execute the fastest. Code assistants, context-aware tools, and unified data access are no longer “nice-to-have”, they’re essential if we want to move at the speed of user feedback.

All in all, an incredible day of learning and inspiration. The landscape is shifting fast, but one thing’s clear: rapid, thoughtful engineering will be the real differentiator.

Thank you MongoDB for inviting me to this inspiring event

--- Post 84 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 533 reactions, 41 comments, 45 reposts (Score: 3,165)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7354298186355695618

We’re hosting a RAG workshop this Saturday (over 300 people have already signed up!) for engineers who want to go beyond demos and start building real systems.

If you're working with GenAI, Retrieval-Augmented Generation is one of the first things you should learn. It's how we give large language models access to dynamic, real-world data, without fine-tuning.

RAG works by combining two systems:
→ a retriever that finds relevant context from your own data
→ a generator that uses that context to produce grounded outputs

Here’s what that looks like under the hood:

1️⃣ Convert your knowledge base into embeddings
- Start with internal docs, PDFs, wikis.
- Use an embedding model (OpenAI, Cohere, HuggingFace) to turn them into dense vectors that capture meaning.

2️⃣ Store those vectors in a database
Use a vector store like FAISS, Weaviate, or Pinecone to index and search them efficiently.

3️⃣ Embed the user query
When a prompt comes in, embed it the same way and search for the top-k most similar chunks.

4️⃣ Inject retrieved context
Take the query and relevant chunks, format them into a new prompt, and send it to the LLM.

5️⃣ Generate an answer
The model now responds using both the query and external knowledge—without retraining.

This is the architecture behind nearly every production LLM app today.
If your model needs access to fresh data, or your use case can’t afford hallucinations, you’re going to need RAG.

👩‍💻 In the workshop, Arvind and I will walk through:
→ How to build a basic RAG system using LangChain and Pinecone
→ What makes retrieval work well (indexing, reranking, hybrid search)
→ How to debug retrieval quality and prompt construction
→ No-code and low-code options if you're experimenting
→ Real-world architectures we’ve seen in the field

============================================================

Most people think you need to be an engineer to break into AI product management. You don’t!

What you do need is a structured way to build intuition for AI, sharpen your product skills, and show proof of execution.

I put together this roadmap to becoming an AI Product Manager, based on what I’ve seen work in real-world teams:

→ Step 0: Master core product skills
Roadmaps, user stories, product metrics. Start with the basics of product management.

→ Step 1: Learn AI fundamentals
Understand training vs inference, supervised vs unsupervised, data → model → deployment. You don’t need to code deeply, but you need to grasp the flow.

→ Step 2: Develop product intuition with AI
Experiment with LLMs, explore prompt engineering, study successful AI products and how they solve real-world problems.

→ Step 3: Build & ship something small
Automate a workflow, build a chatbot, or use no-code/low-code tools. Execution > theory.

→ Step 4: Learn MLOps & Infra
Not at the engineer level, but enough to know evaluation, retraining, and scaling challenges. Product managers who understand infra earn instant credibility.

→ Step 5: Grow your network
Write, share, and engage with the community. The fastest way to build trust is to show you’re actively contributing.

The field is still wide open. And the best way to stand out isn’t to read more theory- it’s to build, learn fast, and show your work.

--- Post 86 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025

Media: image

If you’re building with LLMs in production, evaluation isn't a final checkbox. It is the system.

I’ve been diving into Galileo’s LLM-as-a-Judge e-book recently, and it lays out what many of us in applied GenAI already know:
Most eval pipelines are brittle, narrow, and not built to scale with the complexity of real-world outputs.

So what does a real eval system look like?
Here’s what the best teams are doing (and what I think more infra stacks should bake in)👇

1️⃣ LLM-as-a-Judge works, but only when built carefully
You can’t just ask a model “Which output is better?” and call it evaluation. You need:
→ Task-specific criteria (factuality, clarity, structure, tone)
→ Structured response formats (JSON > star ratings)
→ Chain-of-thought prompting to force reasoning
→ Bias mitigation strategies (verbosity, positionality, authority bias, etc.)
Otherwise, your judge inherits the same blind spots as the model it’s evaluating.

2️⃣ SLMs are underrated in this space
Fine-tuned small language models are faster, cheaper, and often more focused than GPT-4 for evaluation.
Galileo’s Luna-2 is a good example, designed specifically to evaluate quality, not generate it. And they run fast enough to integrate into your dev loop.

3️⃣ LinkedIn’s eval stack is worth studying
They built a framework to evaluate ~500 LLM conversations per day internally.
→ Automated model-based scoring for iteration speed
→ Domain-specific criteria for tasks like job matching
→ Detailed error segmentation to get past the 80% quality plateau
→ Continuous tracking of hallucination, tone, and coherence over time
This isn’t just eval as QA, it’s eval as infrastructure.

I love reading these e-books and case studies from leading AI companies, so many applied insights you don’t get from academic papers.

If you’re working on LLM evaluation and want a deep dive into best practices, bias mitigation, and system design, definitely check out the Galileo ebook on LLM-as-a-Judge.

While at AWS re:Invent, I sat down with Dan Kusel, GM and Managing Partner at IBM, and our conversation reinforced something every senior AI leader already knows but often struggles to operationalize. Impact doesn’t come from standing up more AI projects. It comes from sequencing the right ones.

IBM didn’t get value from AI by chasing flashy use cases. They focused on very real, very unglamorous internal workflows. By applying agentic AI inside core workflows like HR, finance and procurement, they unlocked three point five billion dollars in value. 

A few things stood out for leaders running AI portfolios.

✦ ROI clarity is non negotiable. Before teams talk architecture, they need to define the business shift they expect. Otherwise everything looks like a good candidate for AI, and nothing performs.

✦ Governance is strategy in practice. The most mature organizations use governance to direct investment toward high leverage workflows and away from experiments that won’t compound.

✦ Value has multiple currencies. Some initiatives won’t produce immediate cost savings but materially improve brand, experience or long term capability. Senior leaders win when they know which category they’re playing in.

TL;DR. Sustainable AI advantage isn’t about pace. It’s about “placement”. And leaders who are able to make that distinction are the ones who are able to see a real business impact with AI.

LLMOps is becoming the new DevOps for AI engineers.
Getting a prompt to work is the easy part. The real challenge is making your LLM applications repeatable, scalable, and reliable in production. That’s where LLMOps comes in.

Think of it as the operating system for LLM-driven applications, from data prep to responsible deployment.

Here are the core components of an LLMOps pipeline (see diagram 👇):

➡️ Model Customization: data preparation, supervised fine-tuning, evaluation
➡️ Behind the Scenes: foundation + fine-tuned models, pre-processing, grounding with external knowledge, post-processing with responsible AI filters
➡️ LLM Response Layer: prompting, user interaction, and outputs
➡️ Pipelines: orchestration (data versioning, configs, workflow design) and automation (deployment, execution, monitoring)

As engineers, the craft isn’t just in building the model, it’s in building the system around the model.

💡 Here are some excellent repos/resources to explore:

👉 Prompt orchestration & pipelines → Haystack, LangGraph
👉 Evaluation & Responsible AI → Ragas, LlamaIndex evals
👉 Data prep & tuning → OpenPipe, Axolotl
👉 Deployment → vLLM, Ray Serve, Fireworks AI

If you’re building production-grade AI, don’t stop at the model. Learn to think in terms of LLMOps pipelines- orchestration, automation, and continuous improvement.

If you’re preparing for an AI engineering interview, I put together 30 questions that will give you a broad perspective of the different areas you’re expected to understand.

As you go through them, notice where things feel unfamiliar. If a topic is new to you, check how aligned it is with the role, the team, and the company you’re applying to. If the alignment is strong and the concept still feels unfamiliar, that’s a clear blind spot. Go study that deeper.

Some tips:
✦ The initial question in an AI interview is almost always vague. It’s meant to be. Don’t rush into an answer. Ask follow-ups, state your assumptions, structure your thinking, and then walk through your approach. You can always pause and ask whether you’re heading in the right direction.

✦ If you genuinely don’t know something, say it. Just add what you do know and what you’ve actually worked on instead. Trying to make things up shows immediately and works against you.

✦ Confidence and clarity go further than perfect answers.

Last week at TEDAI San Francisco, I sat in on one of my favorite sessions, The Philosophy of AI, where my friend Chip Huyen joined Walter DE BROUWER, James Joaquin, Bryan McCann, and Steven Levy for a conversation that made everyone in the room pause.

It was such an important conversation, that reminded me why we need to talk about AI not just as a technology, but as a reflection of how we define intelligence, creativity, and human agency.

Chip made a point that stuck with me. She said the real story of AI progress isn’t about novel ideas, it’s about scale, about what happens when better data and more compute compound over time. What many once called “brute force” turned out to be a turning point for the field. I loved that Chip shared how she strictly sees AI as a tool!

Walter and James brought a fascinating perspective, describing AI as a new species that we’re learning to coexist with. They challenged the audience to see this evolution not through fear, but through responsibility- to ask what kind of intelligence we want to create, and what kind of relationship we want with it.

The conversation also touched on Agentic AI, systems that can plan and take action. Chip’s view was simple but powerful, learning is a privilege, and AI should be seen as a collaborator that extends human creativity, not replaces it.

Bryan raised questions around awareness and trust- reminding us that as machines become more capable, it’s on us to stay self-aware as builders.

My biggest takeaway, AI will keep getting better at optimization and automation, but meaning and intention will always come from us.

Our role isn’t to make AI more human, it’s to make humanity more intentional in how we build it.

Happy building ❤️

--- Post 94 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7396959158274613248

If you’re an AI engineer trying to understand agentic systems, keep reading 👇

A single-agent system relies on one model to do everything: reasoning, planning, execution, and validation. It’s efficient for simple, sequential tasks but quickly breaks down as workflows grow more complex or interdependent.

A multi-agent system, on the other hand, distributes intelligence across specialized agents that collaborate. Each has its own role, memory, & context - and together, they solve problems that a single model can’t handle alone.

But here’s what most people miss: there’s no standard architecture.

A multi-agent system is like Lego blocks, you design it for your use case.

You can build:
→ a supervisor agent managing sub-agents
→ a hierarchical setup with layered control
→ or a hybrid model combining centralized planning and peer coordination
The challenge isn’t in building agents, it's in managing how they talk to each other.

Three reasons multi-agent systems often fail:

❌Memory fragments across agents.
Each agent keeps its own context. When one agent hands work to another, subtle details get lost or duplicated. Without a shared memory layer or context synchronization, agents start “forgetting” what the others already know.

❌Operational costs multiply.
What costs $0.10 for a single agent might cost $1 when ten agents coordinate. Every message, validation step, or context handoff adds latency, token cost, and complexity. At scale, coordination overhead often outweighs the benefit of specialization.

❌ Write conflicts cascade.
If multiple agents write to the same source of truth: a shared database, knowledge base, or document, their changes can collide. One agent updates a schema, another updates logic on top of the old version, and suddenly the system’s state fragments.

TL;DR: Not every workflow needs a multi-agent setup.

When tasks are independent, read-heavy, and parallelizable, multi-agent systems shine. But for sequential logic and tight dependencies, a single-agent pipeline is still faster, cheaper, and easier to maintain.

As always, Galileo has published a new phenomenal free handbook- Mastering Multi-Agent Systems.
 
If you want to go deeper into how these systems work, why they fail, how to evaluate them, explore different architectures, and even build LLMOps around them, this is one of the best reads out there: https://lnkd.in/diptdEC2

I had the chance to attend honeycomb.io's Observability Day, and it was one of the most insightful events I’ve been to recently.

The keynote by Charity Majors really stood out. She walked us through the shift from monitoring to true observability- what it means, why it matters, and the real costs of getting it wrong. She shared stories from her time at Meta’s observability engineering team and highlighted the trade-offs around pricing, risks, and best practices that made me rethink how AI toolkits and software pipelines should be instrumented.

Another highlight was Inokentii's session on building FinAI at Intercom. He broke down how AI agents make mistakes, what fallbacks look like, and the issues you run into when scaling these systems. I especially loved the way he applied the Rasmussen safety model- how tools can drift into being unsafe, slow, or too costly for engineers, even leading to economic failure. His framing that “the art of observability is surfacing the real operational boundary” really stuck with me.

And here’s the reality:
⚡ With AI coding agents, we’re building faster than ever, and breaking things we can’t always see
⚡ What used to be clean, predictable services are now black boxes wrapped in black boxes.
⚡ Traditional dashboards weren’t designed for emergent AI behavior, hallucinations, or chained agent calls in production
That’s why observability isn’t optional anymore, especially if you’re shipping with LLMs or agents in production.

This is where Honeycomb really shines. They’re rethinking observability from the ground up with:
→ A unified view across logs, metrics, and traces
→ Sub-second queries across massive datasets
→ BubbleUp to surface anomalies before they become incidents
→ OpenTelemetry-native design
→ Even a hosted MCP server that lets AI agents interact directly with observability data

If observability is in your radar, do check out Honeycomb Canvas, a new way to collaborate and visualize observability data in real time: https://bit.ly/4mNe52c

Thank you to the honeycomb.io team for bringing me to this event and collaborating on this post.

If you are an aspiring AI engineer, the first two tools you need to learn to build real generative AI applications are LangChain and LangGraph. These two give you the foundation for everything from simple LLM pipelines to fully adaptive agent systems.

Here is a simple way to think about them.

🔗 LangChain
♦ Gives you the core components you need to structure LLM workflows: prompts, retrievers, tools, output parsers, and memory.
♦ Designed for linear, predictable, step by step flows where the sequence never changes.
♦ Ideal for RAG, document Q&A, chat interfaces, and rapid prototyping.
♦ Keeps your pipeline modular, debuggable, and fast to iterate on.

🐙 LangGraph
♦ Built on top of LangChain to give you a stateful graph instead of a straight sequence.
♦ Lets your agent loop, branch, retry, update state, pick tools, and coordinate multi step reasoning.
♦ Enables long running, adaptive workflows where the system must make decisions over time.
♦ Essential for building real agents that need planning, memory, and tool use.

If you’re an AI engineer, here are the 15 components of agentic AI you should know.

Building truly agentic systems goes far beyond chaining prompts or wiring tools. It requires modular intelligence that can perceive, plan, act, learn, and adapt across dynamic environments - autonomously and reliably.

This framework breaks it down into 15 technical components:

🔴 1. Goal Formulation
→ Agents must define explicit objectives, decompose them into subgoals, prioritize execution, and adapt dynamically as new context arises.

🟣 2. Perception
→ Real-time sensing across modalities (text, visual, audio, sensors) with uncertainty estimation and context grounding.

🟠 3. Cognition & Reasoning
→ From world modeling to causal inference, agents need inductive, abductive reasoning, planning, and introspection via structured knowledge (graphs, ontologies).

🔴 4. Action Selection & Execution
→ This includes policy learning, planning, trial-and-error correction, and UI/tool interfacing to interact with real systems.

🟣 5. Autonomy & Self-Governance
→ Independence from human-in-the-loop oversight through constraint-aware, initiative-taking decision frameworks.

🟠 6. Learning & Adaptation
→ Support for continual learning, transfer learning, and meta-learning with feedback-driven self-improvement loops.

🔴 7. Memory & State Management
→ Episodic memory, working memory buffers, and semantic grounding for contextually-aware actions over time.

🟣 8. Interaction & Communication
→ Natural language generation and understanding, negotiation, and multi-agent coordination with social signal processing.

🟠 9. Monitoring & Self-Evaluation
→ Agents should monitor their own performance, detect anomalies, benchmark against goals, and recover autonomously.

🔴 10. Ethical and Safety Control
→ Safety constraints, transparency, explainability, and alignment to human values - non-negotiable for real-world deployment.

🟣 11. Resource Management
→ Optimizing compute, memory, and energy with intelligent resource scheduling and infrastructure-aware orchestration.

🟠 12. Persistence & Continuity
→ Agents must preserve goal state across sessions, maintain behavioral consistency, and recover from disruptions.

🔴 13. Agency Integration Layer
→ Modular architecture, orchestration of internal components, and hierarchical control systems for scalable design.

🟣 14. Meta-Agent Capabilities
→ Delegation to sub-agents, participation in agent collectives, and orchestration of agent teams with diverse roles.

🟠 15. Interface & Environment Adaptability
→ Adaptation across domains and tools with robust APIs and reconfigurable sensing-actuation layers.

〰️〰️〰️
🔁 Save and share this if you’re designing agents beyond the demo stage.

--- Post 108 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 439 reactions, 44 comments, 31 reposts (Score: 2,697)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7372074214897516545

Just came across a beautiful blog by Thinking Machines Lab that really clears up a common headache with LLMs. 

We know that large language models often have this quirky non-deterministic nature. In other words, feed them the same prompt twice and you might get slightly different outputs. That’s a problem when you need reliable, consistent results. 

Well, it’s not really that the LLM itself is inherently unpredictable. It’s more about the plumbing around it, specifically, how the inference process handles things like batch sizes, floating-point operations, and how requests are grouped together. These factors can cause tiny variations that lead to different outputs, even when everything else seems the same.

What Thinking Machines Lab did is super interesting. They didn’t change how the LLM itself works. Instead, they focused on the inference pipeline. They made the whole process “batch-invariant,” meaning the model’s output stays consistent no matter how the requests are batched or processed.

They’ve made sure that the variability wasn’t really about the LLM’s internal logic, but about how the “plumbing” was handled. Now, with their adjustments, you get a stable, predictable output every time.

So if you’ve ever been puzzled by LLMs being a bit unpredictable, it turns out it was more about the infrastructure around them. Definitely a cool read if you’re into making AI systems more reliable!

============================================================

The days between Christmas and New Year always put me in a thinking mode.

It’s one of the few times in the year when people stop being busy just for the sake of being busy. Calendars open up. The constant rush pauses. And suddenly, there’s mental space that’s usually hard to come by.

I don’t really use this time to replay the entire year. My mind tends to move forward instead. I start thinking about what I want to work on next, what I want to learn more deeply, and what I don’t want to carry with me into the new year.

What I really appreciate about this window is the space to think without urgency. When you’re not reacting to everything around you, it becomes easier to separate what actually matters from what’s just noise or habit.

The small takeaway I’ve learned over the years:
you don’t need a perfect plan right now.

You just need to give yourself enough space to ask better questions.
About where your energy should go.
About what you want more of.
About what you’re ready to let go of.

That kind of clarity rarely comes when you’re rushing.

Wishing you a thoughtful end to the year and a little mental space before whatever comes next.

How do large language models (LLMs) actually learn?

Most people jump straight into tools, prompts, or agents. But under the hood, LLMs evolve through four distinct learning paradigms. Understanding these will dramatically improve how you build, fine-tune, and reason about AI systems.

1️⃣ Model Offline Pretraining (MOP)

This is where everything begins.

✦ The model is trained on massive amounts of static data
✦ Learning happens via next-token prediction using transformers
✦ Gradients flow through loss and backpropagation
✦ This phase builds general language understanding, reasoning patterns, and world knowledge

Think of this as teaching the model how language works, before it ever talks to a human. No interaction. No feedback loop. Just scale, data, and optimization.

2️⃣ Model Online Adaptation (MOA)

Pretraining makes the model knowledgeable. Adaptation makes it useful.

✦ Supervised fine-tuning on task specific data
✦ Instruction tuning to follow human intent
✦ Parameter efficient methods like LoRA and adapters
✦ Alignment techniques like RLHF, DPO, RLAIF

This is where models learn how they should behave, not just what they know.

You are shaping responses, tone, safety, and task performance.

3️⃣ Multi-Agent Orchestration (MAO)

Modern systems rarely rely on a single model call.

✦ Multiple agents communicate and coordinate
✦ Agents specialize in reasoning, planning, critique, or execution
✦ Self-reflection and debate improve outcomes
✦ Tool calling, function execution, MCP enable real-world action

Here, intelligence emerges from interaction, not just parameters.

The system learns by structuring conversations between models.

4️⃣ Multi-Agent Self-Evolving Systems (MASE)

This is where things get really interesting.

✦ Agents interact with environments, not just each other
✦ Feedback signals come from outcomes, tools, or users
✦ Prompts, memory, tools, and workflows evolve over time
✦ Systems optimize behavior continuously

Learning is no longer just training. It is adaptation in the loop. This is how agentic systems start improving themselves.

✦✦✦
♻️ Share this with your network to help them learn about latest data/AI concepts

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025

If you’re serious about building AI agents, start here 👇

To design agents that actually reason, plan, and act. You need to understand the spectrum of agent architectures.

Here are 9 types of AI agents every AI engineer should know:

💡 1. Simple Reflex Agents
React to current input using rule-based logic (IF-THEN).
→ No memory or learning.
Example: A thermostat that turns on if temp < 20°C.

💡 2. Model-Based Reflex Agents
Use an internal model of the world to infer hidden state.
→ Enable smarter decisions in partially observable settings.
Example: A robot navigating a known room layout to avoid collisions.

💡 3. Goal-Based Agents
Act to achieve defined objectives.
→ Involve search, planning, and pathfinding.
Example: Navigation system choosing optimal route to destination.

💡 4. Utility-Based Agents
Maximize a utility function (e.g. safety, speed, efficiency).
→ Handle trade-offs between competing outcomes.
Example: A self-driving car balancing passenger comfort with urgency.

💡 5. Learning Agents
Improve via feedback—using RL, supervised learning, or both.
→ Adapt and evolve over time.
Example: Chess agent refining tactics through self-play.

💡 6. Multi-Agent Systems (MAS)
Multiple agents interact, collaborate, or compete.
→ Used in swarm robotics, distributed simulations, and games.
Example: Financial agents negotiating trades in a simulated economy.

💡 7. Agentic AI Systems (LLM-based agents)
This is where it gets powerful.
→ Built on large language models + tools + memory + control flow.
→ Can decompose tasks, call APIs, search docs, invoke sub-agents.
Examples: Autogen, CrewAI, LangGraph.

💡 8. Embodied Agents
Agents with a physical presence—robots, drones, etc.
→ Interact with the real world via sensors and actuators.
Example: Boston Dynamics Spot navigating stairs autonomously.

💡 9. Cognitive & Conversational Agents
Human-facing agents designed for natural language interaction.
→ Chatbots, virtual assistants, AI tutors.
Examples: ChatGPT, Alexa, Claude.

If you’re an engineer, don’t just study these, build them. Get hands-on with open-source agent stacks:

→ Autogen (multi-agent orchestration):
https://lnkd.in/d36cU42f
→ CrewAI (LLM agents with roles + memory):
https://lnkd.in/dHBCPmkX
→ LangGraph (agentic control flow, built on LangChain):
https://docs.langgraph.dev

Start with their cookbooks or clone a working repo.
The fastest way to understand agents, is to ship one 😃

--- Post 116 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7376080856362172416

When you’re just starting your career, the biggest trap isn’t lack of skills, it’s letting your early career show up as underconfidence.

My top tip: don’t just show up, lead with curiosity.

This is something I’ve been following since 2019, right when I started my career. Within my first year at IBM, I was able to set up a meeting with Rob Thomas (SVP at the time) to discuss product vision and how data science and AI tools could be incorporated into IBM’s product lines. 

That didn’t happen because I had years of experience, I had none. It happened because I emailed him with specific details: I showed my work from internships, sent clear questions, and laid out an agenda of what I wanted to discuss. That clarity and preparation made it worth his time.

And that principle has stayed with me ever since. Even today, when I meet VPs, CTOs, or startup founders, I make sure I’m not just there to listen, I come in with intention. 

Here’s how you can apply this:

→ Share something you’ve read (a new framework, case study, or article). Lead with, “I came across this, what’s your perspective?”

→ Ask open-ended questions that spark discussion, not just answers.

→ Reframe introductions. Instead of “I’m new,” try “I’ve been exploring X, curious how your team approaches it.”

→ Capture your learnings. After a talk, paper, or shadowing session, write 2–3 takeaways and share them, it signals thought leadership.

→ Remember: humility doesn’t mean invisibility.

What’s funny is that sometimes when I’m speaking with top leadership, they’ll say, “Oh, I didn’t know about that.” That’s validation: years of experience aren’t the only currency. Your curiosity, preparation, and ability to share knowledge are equally powerful.

Early in your career, people don’t expect you to have all the answers. But they do notice if you bring ideas, energy, and intent into the room.

Confidence isn’t about pretending you know everything, it’s about showing you’re hungry to learn, and making others want to learn with you.

============================================================

If you’re an AI engineer/ or aspiring to be one, and looking to build strong, technical portfolio projects, this one’s for you.

I’ve pulled together 5 practical project ideas that go beyond toy examples and give you real exposure to open-weight models, multimodal inputs, long-context reasoning, tool-use via MCP, and even on-device AI.

Each project includes an open-source reference repo so you don’t have to start from scratch, you can fork, build, and iterate.

1️⃣ Autonomous Browser Agent
Turn any website into an API. Give the agent a natural-language goal, it plans, acts in a real browser, and returns structured output.
→ Model: DeepSeekV3 via Fireworks AI Inference
→ Planner: LangGraph
→ Browser control: Playwright MCP server or browser-use
→ Optional memory: mem0
🔗 Repo: shubcodes/fireworksai-browseruse

2️⃣ 1M-token Codebase Analyst
Load massive repos like PyTorch into a single 1M-token window and answer deep questions about architecture and logic, no brittle chunking.
→ Model: Llama 4 Maverick served via Fireworks AI (KV-cache paging)
→ Long-context tuning: EasyContext
→ Interface: Gradio or VS Code extension
🔗 Repos: adobe-research/NoLiMa, jzhang38/EasyContext

3️⃣ Multimodal Video-QA & Summariser
Ingest long-form videos and output timeline-aligned summaries and Q&A. Combine visual frames with ASR transcripts for deep comprehension.
→ Model: MVU (ICLR ’25) or HunyuanVideo
→ Retrieval: LanceDB hybrid search
→ Serving: vLLM multimodal backend + FFmpeg
🔗 Repo: kahnchana/mvu

4️⃣ Alignment Lab (RLHF / DPO)
Fine-tune a 7B open-weight model using preference data and evaluate its behavior with real alignment benchmarks.
→ Framework: OpenRLHF with Fireworks AI endpoints
→ Evaluation: RewardBench, trlX
→ Dataset: GPT-4o-generated preference pairs
🔗 Repo: OpenRLHF/OpenRLHF

5️⃣ Local-first Voice Assistant with Memory
Build a privacy-first voice assistant that runs fully offline, remembers users, and syncs memory when online.
→ Model: Mobile-optimized Llama 3.2 with ExecuTorch or Ollama
→ ASR and TTS: Whisper.cpp + WhisperSpeech
→ Memory: mem0 via OpenMemory MCP
🔗 Repo: mem0ai/mem0

My two cents:
→ Don’t wait for the “perfect” starting point. Fork one of these repos, add a feature, refactor the flow, swap the model. That’s how you learn.

→ If you’re stuck starting from scratch, lean on these foundations and build iteratively.

→ You don’t need to be perfect at coding everything, you can pair up with tools like Cursor, or use coding copilots like Claude, or GitHub Copilot to break through blockers.

→ Prefer working on visible, end-to-end workflows. Even better if you can ship a demo or write a detailed blog post about what you learned.

→ If you’re not ready to build a full product, even contributing to an existing open-source agent or LLM inference repo is a great start.

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7356350217178206211

If you’re building a RAG pipeline and not thinking deeply about your chunking strategy, you’re likely leaving performance on the table.

RAG isn’t just about retrieval quality, it’s about how retrievable your knowledge even is.

Here’s why chunking matters 👇

The way you break down your documents directly impacts:
→ What gets retrieved
→ How well the model understands the context
→ Whether the response is coherent, factual, or hallucinated

⚠️ Poor chunking = irrelevant results
🤖 Smart chunking = better grounding, higher accuracy, and faster response time

I have broken down 11 advanced chunking strategies (below), from fixed-length and sentence-based to LLM-suggested and modality-aware. Each serves a different purpose depending on your document structure and use case.

Some underrated techniques to try:
→ Overlap Chunking for preserving context across boundaries
→ Semantic-Based Chunking for meaning-preserving slices
→ Modality-Aware Chunking when working with mixed text + image + diagrams
→ Metadata-Enhanced Chunking to improve ranking and retrieval filtering
→ Adaptive (Hybrid) Chunking for multi-modal, multi-section docs

And yes, chunking isn’t one-size-fits-all. You often need to experiment across types, based on whether your RAG is for:
- Customer support
- Financial reports
- Technical documentation
- Scientific research
- Multi-modal user manuals

🔧 Want to try these?
Here are a few tools that let you implement or test chunking strategies:
→ LangChain TextSplitter classes
→ LlamaIndex NodeParser and SentenceSplitter
→ Unstructured for modality-aware document parsing
→ Haystack PreProcessor module
→ Fireworks AI+ LangChain for testing impact on downstream retrieval

The key takeaway:
Better chunks = better context = better answers

Most people don’t understand the difference between ML engineering and AI engineering.

They use the terms interchangeably. They are not the same.

I’ve spent almost a decade in machine learning, building classical ML pipelines end to end. And what we’re calling “AI engineering” today, especially in the generative era, operates on a very different mental model.

Here’s the clean way to think about it:

ML Engineering → Model & Data Focus
AI Engineering → System & Application Focus

1️⃣ In ML engineering, your center of gravity is the model.

- You start with raw data and features.
- You build data pipelines.
- You design model architecture.
- You train and optimize in PyTorch or TensorFlow.
- You evaluate, tune, validate.
- You deploy with MLOps and monitor drift.

The outcome: accurate, scalable predictive models.

2️⃣ In AI engineering, especially with LLMs, your center of gravity shifts to the system.

- You orchestrate foundational models through APIs.
- You design prompts, RAG pipelines, and context windows.
- You integrate vector databases.
- You build workflows and agents with orchestration layers.
- You monitor UX behavior and system reliability.

The outcome: intelligent applications and product experiences.

In ML, you optimize weights.
In AI engineering, you architect behavior around probabilistic models.

The data hypothesis is different.
The determinism is different.
The CI/CD philosophy is different.
The evaluation strategy is radically different.

If you look at the diagram I shared, you’ll see the contrast clearly: model lifecycle versus application workflow.

I’m putting together a deep technical breakdown on Substack where I’ll go into evaluation patterns, system design tradeoffs, and how the stacks diverge in production- https://lnkd.in/dpBNr6Jg

If you’re building in this space, you need this mental model shift.

============================================================

--- Post 123 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

Layoffs are a reminder that job security is fragile, and here’s what you need to do to future-proof your career.

Over the years, I’ve seen incredibly strong teams get impacted. High performers. Critical contributors. People doing everything “right.” It’s a tough reminder that performance alone is not a long-term safety net.

What has consistently helped people land on their feet faster is having credibility beyond their job title.

Here are three things I genuinely believe make a difference:

1. Build real, in-person relationships beyond your company. Some of the strongest career opportunities I’ve seen come from being in the room, not from online reach. Conferences, meetups, workshops, even small community events. When people have met you, spoken to you, and understand how you think, trust forms much faster.

2. Be intentional about educating others in your field. This doesn’t mean teaching everything you know or positioning yourself as an expert overnight. It can be as simple as explaining how you approach problems, sharing lessons from your work, or helping others build intuition. Teaching creates credibility in a very organic way.

3. Create tangible proof of your skills outside your 9–5. If your work is confidential, take those same skills and apply them elsewhere. Build projects. Collaborate with nonprofits or universities. Ship something real that someone else can look at and say, “I understand what you can do.”

None of this is about vanity. It’s about career resilience. When jobs are transient, your skills, reputation, and network shouldn’t be.

Most retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems work by pulling back documents that look relevant to a query.

But knowledge in the real world is not just text snippets. It’s structured. It’s relational.

That’s where GraphRAG comes in.

🔎 What makes GraphRAG different?
Instead of retrieving isolated chunks of text, GraphRAG retrieves subgraphs of knowledge.

- Information is stored as nodes (entities) and edges (relationships).
- Retrieval means surfacing a connected set of entities and their links.
- The model gets not just what is written, but how concepts connect.

This structure allows for multi-hop reasoning, the model can trace paths through the graph, following relationships that mirror how humans think and learn.

🧠 Why does this matter?
- Traditional RAG → good at finding relevant passages

- GraphRAG → better at preserving context, reasoning across multiple steps, and grounding responses in structured knowledge

It’s not simply an upgrade to RAG.
It’s a shift from retrieving information to retrieving understanding.

As more organizations move from experimenting with LLMs to deploying them in knowledge-intensive workflows, approaches like GraphRAG will become critical.

--- Post 127 ---

Everyone’s debating when we’ll reach AGI.
But very few are asking: what does the path to AGI actually look like?

It’s not a binary switch, it’s a staircase.
One level at a time.

And each level unlocks new capabilities, risks, and design challenges.

This is how I break down the 5 Levels of AGI from a systems engineering lens:

→ Level 1: Conversational AI
Where most models are today- great at text generation, decent at simulating conversation, but reactive and stateless. They wait for your prompt and respond. That’s it.

→ Level 2: Reasoners
These models go beyond surface-level chat. They can tackle complex, multi-step tasks like a human expert- solving math problems, debugging code, writing research summaries, without relying on external tools. It’s a major leap in cognitive capability.

→ Level 3: Agents
Now we’re talking autonomy. These systems can plan, act, and adapt over time. Think agents that remember context, make decisions, manage workflows, and use tools on your behalf. They’re not just responding, they’re operating.

→ Level 4: Innovators
This is the inflection point. AI that doesn’t just follow instructions but generates novel ideas- new inventions, product concepts, even original research. Creativity without being prompted. That’s when AI becomes a true collaborator.

→ Level 5: Organizations
The final frontier. Imagine an AI that runs an entire org- strategizing, optimizing, and executing with precision at scale. At this level, the system isn’t just helping teams… it is the team.

We’re somewhere between Levels 2 and 3 today.
But the shift to 4 and 5 will change everything, from how we build software to how we structure companies.

We’re not jumping from GPT-4(s) to full AGI overnight. (We will see what GPT-5 has to offer today 😄)

We’re climbing the stack- one level of agency, reasoning, and autonomy at a time.

〰️〰️〰️

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

Most people evaluate LLMs by just benchmarks. But in production, the real question is- how well do they perform?

When you’re running inference at scale, these are the 3 performance metrics that matter most:

1️⃣ Latency
How fast does the model respond after receiving a prompt?
There are two kinds to care about:
→ First-token latency: Time to start generating a response
→ End-to-end latency: Time to generate the full response

Latency directly impacts UX for chat, speed for agentic workflows, and runtime cost for batch jobs. Even small delays add up fast at scale.

2️⃣ Context Window
How much information can the model remember- both from the prompt and prior turns?
This affects long-form summarization, RAG, and agent memory.
Models range from:
→ GPT-3.5 / LLaMA 2: 4k–8k tokens
→ GPT-4 / Claude 2: 32k–200k tokens
→ GPT-OSS-120B: 131k tokens

Larger context enables richer workflows but comes with tradeoffs: slower inference and higher compute cost. Use compression techniques like attention sink or sliding windows to get more out of your context window.

3️⃣ Throughput
How many tokens or requests can the model handle per second?
This is key when you’re serving thousands of requests or processing large document batches. Higher throughput = faster completion and lower cost.

How to optimize based on your use case:
→ Real-time chat or tool use → prioritize low latency
→ Long documents or RAG → prioritize large context window
→ Agentic workflows → find a balance between latency and context
→ Async or high-volume processing → prioritize high throughput

My 2 cents 🤌
→ Choose in-region, lightweight models for lower latency
→ Use 32k+ context models only when necessary
→ Mix long-context models with fast first-token latency for agents
→ Optimize batch size and decoding strategy to maximize throughput

Don’t just pick a model based on benchmarks.
Pick the right tradeoffs for your workload.

--- Post 132 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 394 reactions, 33 comments, 39 reposts (Score: 2,378)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7428606345546969088

A lot of people use “Generative AI” as shorthand for LLMs.

And then LLMs become shorthand for transformer-based chat models. That’s way too narrow.

Generative AI is a broader modeling family. Transformers are one important branch, not the whole tree.

Here’s a cleaner breakdown of the main types of generative AI models:

1️⃣ Transformer Models

Used for text, code, reasoning, multimodal generation.

These are attention-based architectures that model relationships across tokens in parallel.

Examples (2026 relevant):
✦ GPT-5
✦ Claude 4
✦ Gemini 2.5 Pro

Most large language models today are transformer-based and autoregressive under the hood.

2️⃣ Diffusion Models

Used for image, video, and increasingly audio generation.

They learn to reverse a noise process step by step, gradually denoising random noise into structured content.

Examples:
✦ Stable Diffusion 3
✦ Midjourney v7
✦ Sora

Diffusion dominates high-fidelity visual generation today.

3️⃣ GANs (Generative Adversarial Networks)

Two networks trained together: a generator and a discriminator.

The generator tries to fool the discriminator. The discriminator tries to detect fake samples. The competition improves realism.

Examples (still relevant in specific niches):
✦ StyleGAN3
✦ NVIDIA GauGAN
✦ ESRGAN (super-resolution)

GANs are less dominant than diffusion now, but still powerful for high-resolution image synthesis and enhancement.

4️⃣ VAEs (Variational Autoencoders)

Probabilistic generative models that learn structured latent representations.

Often used for representation learning, anomaly detection, and as components inside larger systems.

Examples:
✦ Latent/ Conditional VAE
✦ VQ-VAE-2
✦ NVAE

VAEs are foundational in many hybrid generative systems.

5️⃣ Autoregressive Models

Generate outputs token by token, step by step, conditioned on previous outputs.

This applies to text, music, time series, even pixels in older image models.

Examples:
✦ GPT-style decoders
✦ LLaMA 3 family
✦ MusicGen

Important detail: most modern LLMs are both transformer-based and autoregressive. “Transformer” describes architecture. “Autoregressive” describes the generation strategy.

If you’re getting into AI engineering in 2026, it helps to separate, and understand when to use what.

============================================================

I recently started a "Career myths you need to unlearn" series on my Instagram. Here is the latest one: 

𝗠𝗼𝘀𝘁 𝗽𝗲𝗼𝗽𝗹𝗲 𝗱𝗼𝗻’𝘁 𝗴𝗲𝘁 𝗿𝗲𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗲𝗱 𝗯𝘆 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗽𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗲𝘀.
𝗧𝗵𝗲𝘆 𝗿𝗲𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝗺𝘀𝗲𝗹𝘃𝗲𝘀 𝗯𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝘆 𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗻 𝗮𝗽𝗽𝗹𝘆.
You read a job description that lists 15 tools and think,
“I’ve only used 8 of these. I’m not ready yet.”

But here’s the truth:

Most job descriptions are wishlists, not checklists. No hiring manager expects you to know everything on day one.
They care about how you think, how you learn, and how you solve problems when things get messy.

𝗪𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗮𝗰𝘁𝘂𝗮𝗹𝗹𝘆 𝗴𝗲𝘁𝘀 𝘆𝗼𝘂 𝗵𝗶𝗿𝗲𝗱?
→ The ability to break down complex problems, even if you haven’t solved this exact one before
→ The mindset to pick up new technologies quickly and adapt on the fly
→ Collaboration skills when the path isn’t clear or the requirements change mid-project
→ Curiosity, initiative, and a growth mindset 

If you’re job hunting right now, here’s how to think about it:

1️⃣ Stop asking: “Am I ready?” Start asking: “Am I resourceful?”
In tech, no one is fully ready. The field moves too fast. What matters is whether you can figure things out as you go.

2️⃣ Focus on transferable skills.
Think beyond specific toolkits. Ask yourself:
"Have I shipped something real?"
"Have I solved ambiguous problems?"
"Have I picked up new tech stacks quickly in the past?"

3️⃣ Practice growth mindset, not perfection mindset.
A growth mindset means you believe skills are built, not just born.
You don’t need to finish learning before you apply.
You need to commit to learning continuously, and show you’re comfortable in that process.

4️⃣ Apply even if you don’t meet 100 percent of the requirements.
If you match 60-70 percent, and you’re genuinely excited about the role, apply. Write a thoughtful application. Show how you’ll bridge the gap.

Have you ever self-rejected from a role because you thought you weren’t ready?

============================================================

--- Post 138 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 421 reactions, 12 comments, 73 reposts (Score: 2,371)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7374500966638891008

𝗧𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗸 𝗼𝗳 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗮𝘀 𝗴𝗶𝘃𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗔𝗜 𝗽𝗲𝗿𝗺𝗶𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝘁𝗼 “𝗼𝗽𝗲𝗻 𝗮 𝗯𝗼𝗼𝗸” 𝗯𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲 𝗶𝘁 𝗮𝗻𝘀𝘄𝗲𝗿𝘀.

If you’ve bumped into Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and wondered what it really is (and when you actually need it), this mini-primer is for you.

𝗪𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗶𝘀 — 𝗶𝗻 𝗼𝗻𝗲 𝗯𝗿𝗲𝗮𝘁𝗵

RAG pairs a language model with an external knowledge source so answers are grounded in real, up-to-date information instead of just whatever the model remembers from training. That means fewer made-up facts and more verifiable responses. 

𝗪𝗵𝗲𝗻 𝘆𝗼𝘂 𝘀𝗵𝗼𝘂𝗹𝗱 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗰𝗵 𝗳𝗼𝗿 𝗥𝗔𝗚
✅You want a domain-specific assistant (HR policy bot, clinical FAQ, internal IT helper).
✅You need current info beyond a model’s training cutoff.
✅You care about citations and traceability.

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗽𝗶𝗽𝗲𝗹𝗶𝗻𝗲 (𝘀𝗶𝗺𝗽𝗹𝗲 𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻)
✅𝗜𝗻𝗱𝗲𝘅𝗶𝗻𝗴 – Gather your sources (PDFs, sites, databases). Split long docs into smaller, meaningful “chunks,” turn each chunk into an embedding (a numeric vector), and store them in a vector database for fast similarity search.

✅𝗥𝗲𝘁𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘃𝗮𝗹 – Convert the user’s question into an embedding and fetch the closest chunks from the vector store.

✅𝗚𝗲𝗻𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 – Feed the question + retrieved chunks to the LLM to produce a grounded answer (and optionally add citations).

Why chunk? Models don’t magically use long context well; narrowing to the most relevant bits improves precision and keeps prompts lean.

𝗛𝗲𝗹𝗽𝗳𝘂𝗹 𝗮𝗱𝗱-𝗼𝗻𝘀 (𝘂𝘀𝗲 𝗮𝘀 𝗻𝗲𝗲𝗱𝗲𝗱)

✅𝗤𝘂𝗲𝗿𝘆 𝘁𝗿𝗮𝗻𝘀𝗹𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 (𝗛𝘆𝗗𝗘, 𝗺𝘂𝗹𝘁𝗶-𝗾𝘂𝗲𝗿𝘆): Rewrite or expand the question so retrieval finds better matches. HyDE, for instance, has the model draft a hypothetical answer, embed it, and search with that to boost recall. 

✅𝗥𝗼𝘂𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 & 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘀𝘁𝗿𝘂𝗰𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻: If you have multiple stores (policies, product docs, web search), route the query to the best source and add filters (e.g., “last 90 days”).

𝗕𝘂𝗶𝗹𝗱𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗯𝗹𝗼𝗰𝗸𝘀 (𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵𝗼𝘂𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗵𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗮𝗰𝗵𝗲)
✅ 𝗟𝗮𝗻𝗴𝗖𝗵𝗮𝗶𝗻 (𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗶𝗻𝘀): Wire steps like “translate → retrieve → generate → parse” into a clear sequence you can swap and test. 

✅𝗟𝗮𝗻𝗴𝗦𝗺𝗶𝘁𝗵 (𝗼𝗯𝘀𝗲𝗿𝘃𝗮𝗯𝗶𝗹𝗶𝘁𝘆): Trace every run, see timings and inputs/outputs, and debug failures—super handy once you go beyond demos.

𝗦𝘂𝗺𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘆 𝘆𝗼𝘂 𝗰𝗮𝗻 𝘁𝗮𝗸𝗲 𝘁𝗼 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸
✅Start simple: good chunking + a solid vector DB + a clear prompt template.
✅Measure what matters (accuracy on real tasks, not vibes).
✅Iterate: logs and traces will tell you where the bottleneck is. 


If this clicked, follow us on LinkedIn and join us on this 𝟮𝟳 𝗦𝗲𝗽𝘁𝗲𝗺𝗯𝗲𝗿 in our hands-on 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸𝘀𝗵𝗼𝗽 by Aishwarya Srinivasan and Arvind Narayanamurthy. Where we’ll go from fundamentals to building a working pipeline (chunking, retrieval, generation, light eval) 

𝗟𝗶𝗻𝗸 𝘁𝗼 𝗷𝗼𝗶𝗻 𝗶𝗻 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗳𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁!!!

If I could go back in time and tell my student-self one thing, it would be this: don’t just fit into the box your university puts you in.

The curriculum, the classes, the grades- they matter, but they aren’t the whole story. What truly defines your path is how intentionally you use these years. Explore widely. Build boldly. And most importantly, stay focused on where you want to be, not where the system pushes you.

Here’s what I tell my mentees:

➡️ Don’t limit yourself to the syllabus. Take up projects, internships, or research outside class. Whether it’s coding, design, debate, or public speaking, follow those sparks of curiosity.

➡️ Experiment across interests. Join clubs, hackathons, or professional societies. The safest time to try and fail is when you’re still a student.

➡️ Be intentional with your focus. Don’t let the noise around you drive your direction. Spend time thinking about where you want to go- industry, research, startups, or something unconventional , and align your actions with that.

➡️ Build your network. Meet people in person, attend conferences, career fairs, and events. One conversation today might become the stepping stone to your future career.

➡️ Create instead of just consuming. Write, share, build, and put your work out there. Your credibility grows when people can see your journey, not just your degree.

Looking back, I realize that these years weren’t just about academics. They were about shaping focus, experimenting with ideas, and building the foundation for a meaningful career.

So if you’re starting your semester now, don’t just drift. Be intentional. Find your path. And create the version of yourself you want to become.

Many people are excited about large language models, and rightfully so.
But in most companies today, the core machine learning systems still rely on traditional ML.

If you’re starting your data science journey, I encourage you to first master the fundamentals. These are the core algorithm families that power real-world systems:

1️⃣ Classification → Churn, fraud, lead scoring
2️⃣ Clustering → Customer segmentation, anomaly detection
3️⃣ A/B Testing → Pricing, UX, causal lift
4️⃣ Time Series Forecasting → Demand, finance, traffic
5️⃣ Ensembles → Boosted accuracy with models like XGBoost
6️⃣ Recommenders → Personalized products or content
7️⃣ Text Generation → BERT, RoBERTa, LLMs, fine-tuned with domain-specific data

These techniques aren’t outdated. They’re production-proven, interpretable, and effective.

Start here. Build deep intuition.
Then bring in GenAI when the use case truly needs it.

Most engineers shy away from the idea of personal branding.
The common misconception is that it means becoming an influencer on social media. But that’s not the case.

Personal branding is simply about how you show up, whether inside your organization or to the outside world. And the way you approach it depends on your goals:

→ Are the opportunities you’re looking for tied to your company and leadership team? That’s internal personal branding.

→ Do you want to attract new roles, projects, or industry visibility? That’s external personal branding.

Both are valid. Both are powerful. And both are subjective to where you want to grow.

I often break it down into the 4P approach:

👉 Persona – Define your target audience. Who are you trying to reach, and how do you want to be perceived?
👉 Peculiarity – Highlight what makes you unique. Your skills, your perspective, your edge.
👉 Promise – Communicate the value you bring. Not just what you do, but how you solve problems and make lives easier.
👉 Proof – Back it up with evidence. Showcase projects, outcomes, or impact that validate your credibility.

For engineers, personal branding doesn’t mean adding “content creator” to your resume. It means being intentional about how you present your work, your expertise, and the value you create, so the right people recognize it.

The question is: have you thought about what your brand says about you today? 

I built something I wish I had earlier in my career.
My LinkedIn Growth Playbook is now open for pre-orders.

✦ Building trust signals that make your profile work for you

--- Post 151 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 364 reactions, 37 comments, 41 reposts (Score: 2,272)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7431019519793340416

If you’re getting started in the AI engineering space, here’s the mental model you need.

AI systems are distributed across five layers. If you only understand one of them, you’re not building systems yet. You’re just stitching tools together.

Layer 1: Infrastructure.
This is where hardware and compute live. GPUs, specialized chips, storage, networking. You can think of it as physical hardware plus cloud platforms working together. This layer decides cost, latency, and scale before you even write a single prompt.

Layer 2: Data.
This is where you integrate with knowledge bases, data lakes, warehouses, and vector databases. It’s ingestion, cleaning, labeling, retrieval. If your data layer is weak, your RAG pipeline will be weak. No model can rescue messy data.

Layer 3: Model Development and LLMOps.
This includes foundation models, fine tuning, deployment frameworks, registries, monitoring. It’s not just about picking an LLM. It’s about evaluating it, versioning it, fine tuning it when needed, and running it reliably in production.

Layer 4: Application and Orchestration.
This is where you actually build systems. Orchestration frameworks like LangChain or LangGraph. RAG pipelines. Agentic workflows. Tool calling. APIs. This is where reasoning meets system design.

Layer 5: User Interface and Experience.
This is how people interact with your AI. Chat interfaces. Copilots inside coding IDEs. Embedded AI in enterprise search. If the UX is confusing, adoption dies, no matter how good the backend is.

And then there’s something important: guardrails, compliance, bias detection, and model monitoring are not a separate afterthought. They should exist across every layer. You want observability and control from infrastructure all the way up to the user interface. That’s how you get full visibility into your stack.

If you want to become a strong AI engineer in 2026, stop thinking in prompts. Start thinking in layers.

============================================================

--- Post 152 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 403 reactions, 25 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 2,267)

I came across Canva’s case study recently and thought it was pretty interesting. 

When your product reaches hundreds of millions of users, feedback starts coming in from every direction - support tickets, surveys, app store reviews, social media, and community forums.

But here’s the real challenge: even though there’s more feedback than ever, it gets harder to actually hear what users are saying. Manual tagging doesn’t scale, insights get buried in noise, and PMs end up relying on anecdotes or chasing spreadsheets instead of real patterns.

That’s what made Canva’s approach stand out to me. They used Enterpret’s customer intelligence AI to turn scattered feedback into something teams could act on.

Here’s how they did it 👇

1. Unified Knowledge Graph
All structured and unstructured feedback from 30+ channels centralized in real time.

2. Feedback Modeling
Mapped sentiment, bugs, and feature requests across cohorts, plan types, and product surfaces.

3. Natural Language Querying
PMs could simply ask, “What are new users on mobile frustrated with this week?” and get an instant, structured answer.

4. Automated Detection
AI agents flagged anomalies like spikes in complaints or sudden sentiment drops before metrics started showing the impact.

The outcome wasn’t just faster analysis, it changed how teams think about feedback - from something reactive to something operational.

If you’re scaling a product and struggling to make sense of feedback at scale, Enterpret 2.0 (just launched) is worth checking out. It brings deeper AI capabilities and a new UI that makes listening and acting a lot easier.

I highly recommend reaching the case study as well, link in comments :)

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7386929021906804736

When evaluating AI agents, accuracy alone is a poor proxy for performance.

An agent’s goal isn’t to produce a correct answer, it’s to complete a task. And how reliably it does that depends on more than just model precision.

Three metrics matter most:

1. Task Success Rate (TSR)
Measures the percentage of end-to-end tasks completed correctly.
This captures real-world reliability – can the agent consistently finish what it starts?

2. First-Try Success (FTS)
Tracks how often the agent succeeds on its first attempt.
This reflects reasoning quality and prompt grounding – whether it understands the task context accurately before acting.

3. Recovery Speed
Captures how quickly, or in how many steps, the agent self-corrects after a mistake.
This is the best signal of adaptability and robustness, which are critical for agents operating in dynamic environments.

In complex, multi-step workflows, these metrics often tell a more complete story than accuracy or BLEU scores.

An agent that can self-correct and adapt is far more valuable than one that only performs well under static test conditions.

--- Post 156 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 306 reactions, 65 comments, 19 reposts (Score: 2,218)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7361791752980078592

If you’re building with AI in 2025, you should absolutely understand how agents self-evolve.

AI agents aren’t just pre-trained and deployed. They adapt, learn, and improve continuously.
Here’s how that actually works 👇


1️⃣ Learning Paradigm: Offline → Online

Most agents don’t learn in the wild from day one.
They start with offline learning:
→ Data generation → Filtering → Model fine-tuning
→ This builds a strong foundation without any online noise
→ But it comes with risks, mostly distribution shift when deployed

Once deployed, they switch to online learning:
→ The agent interacts with the environment
→ Learns from every step, every outcome
→ Continuously updates itself in real time

Offline = stability
Online = adaptability
The strongest systems combine both.

2️⃣ Policy Consistency: On-Policy vs Off-Policy

→ On-policy agents learn from their own current experience (e.g., PPO, A3C)
→ You get tight feedback loops, but it’s less sample efficient
→ Off-policy agents can learn from anything, replay buffers, past runs, human demos, even other agents (e.g., Q-Learning, SAC)
→ Higher sample efficiency, but riskier in terms of policy drift

🔥 Pro tip: A lot of modern systems are offline-to-online hybrids
Train with curated data → then carefully adapt in the wild. It’s the best way to scale performance without collapsing the policy

3️⃣ Reward Granularity: How You Shape Behavior

This one’s underrated. How you design rewards will literally shape how your agent behaves.

→ Process-based reward = feedback at each step
→ Outcome-based reward = feedback only at the end
→ Hybrid reward = the mix of both, optimized for long-horizon tasks

If you’re working with multi-step reasoning or decision-heavy tasks, hybrid reward setups give you control and strategic flexibility.

This is how we move from agents that are just functional to agents that are resilient, adaptive, and continuously improving.

Generative AI has taken “AI” out of the hands of specialists and placed it into every industry that has a problem to solve. 

When teams can build with natural language, integrate with existing systems, and map human intent instead of rigid filters, AI stops being a lab project and becomes a business capability.

I was going through some recent case studies from Publicis Sapient and one of them really stood out to me. It captures something important about where we are in this GenAI wave. We finally have AI that is not limited to technical teams. It is being used directly to reshape customer experience in ways that people can actually feel.

The Homes and Villas by Marriott Bonvoy project is a great example of this shift. Publicis Sapient and Marriott built a generative search experience using Azure OpenAI that turns natural language intent into real, bookable vacation homes. Not filters, not rigid queries. Actual human intent.

A few technical details from the case study that I loved:

✦ Intent parsing over keyword search
 Travelers can describe feelings or preferences. The system uses LLMs to infer constraints, property attributes, and destination suggestions across 150K listings.

✦ GPT based retrieval pipeline
 LLMs enrich the query, expand candidates, and rerank results based on nuanced signals which reduces dead ends and increases high confidence matches.

✦ Real time context generation
 Weather, activities, and travel ideas are synthesized for each result which turns simple search into discovery.

✦ Enterprise scale rollout acceleration
 Once the pattern was built, Marriott cut expansion time from a year to three months which shows how GenAI lowers the cost of experimentation inside large organizations.

For a long time, we were told that the only way to win in tech was to go extremely narrow.

Pick one niche. Go deep. Stay there.

That worked when knowledge was slow and information was siloed.

In AI, things move differently.

Yes, you need depth. If you are building production systems, you cannot be “kind of good” at modeling or infrastructure. Your nine to five demands real specialization.

But here is what I have learned the hard way.

Careers today reward people who can connect dots.

The engineers who grow fastest are not just strong technically. They understand product tradeoffs. They can explain business impact. They can sit in a room with leadership and translate complexity into clarity.

That is not hyperspecialization. That is range.

And range does not dilute your expertise. It amplifies it.

Professional platforms like LinkedIn make this even more obvious. They are not about posting for attention. They are about building a visible portfolio of how you think. When you consistently show that you understand both depth and context, opportunities find you.

I have seen this in my own career. Being able to move between deep technical discussions and broader strategic conversations has opened doors that pure technical skill alone would not have.

So no, I am not anti-specialization.

Be specialized in your role.

But build a generalist mindset around it.

That balance is what I break down in my LinkedIn Growth Playbook. Not how to “go viral,” but how to build credibility and visibility in a way that actually compounds your career.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7410510490868658176

If you’re an AI engineer trying to understand and build with GenAI, RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) is one of the most essential components to master.

It’s the backbone of any LLM system that needs fresh, accurate, and context-aware outputs.

Let’s break down how RAG works, step by step, from an engineering lens, not a hype one:

🧠 How RAG Works (Under the Hood)

1. Embed your knowledge base

→ Start with unstructured sources - docs, PDFs, internal wikis, etc.
→ Convert them into semantic vector representations using embedding models (e.g., OpenAI, Cohere, or HuggingFace models)
→ Output: N-dimensional vectors that preserve meaning across contexts

2. Store in a vector database

→ Use a vector store like Pinecone, Weaviate, or FAISS
→ Index embeddings to enable fast similarity search (cosine, dot-product, etc.)

3. Query comes in - embed that too

→ The user prompt is embedded using the same embedding model
→ Perform a top-k nearest neighbor search to fetch the most relevant document chunks

4. Context injection

→ Combine retrieved chunks with the user query
→ Format this into a structured prompt for the generation model (e.g., Mistral, Claude, Llama)

5. Generate the final output

→ LLM uses both the query and retrieved context to generate a grounded, context-rich response
→ Minimizes hallucinations and improves factuality at inference time

📚 What changes with RAG?

Without RAG:
🧠 “I don’t have data on that.”

With RAG:
🤖 “Based on [retrieved source], here’s what’s currently known…”

Same model, drastically improved quality.

🔍 Why this matters

You need RAG when:
→ Your data changes daily (support tickets, news, policies)
→ You can’t afford hallucinations (legal, finance, compliance)
→ You want your LLMs to access your private knowledge base without retraining

It’s the most flexible, production-grade approach to bridge static models with dynamic information.
Arvind and I recently conducted 2 workshops to help you deep-dive into building RAG Systems. If you want to learn about RAG both fundamentally and with code-labs this is the best resource for it.

Part 1: Getting started with building RAG System: https://lnkd.in/gjx5WNP6

Part 2: Advanced RAG Patterns: https://lnkd.in/dJGR3zQU
