--- Post 161 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 298 reactions, 65 comments, 27 reposts (Score: 2,194)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7371204882197893120

If you’re building with AI in 2025, you need to understand how agents self-evolve.

LLMs gave us static reasoning. Agents go further - they adapt, retain, and improve over time. 

Here’s how that actually works 👇

🤔When does evolution happen?
→ Intra-task evolution happens during inference. Agents adapt mid-task using in-context learning, memory lookup, or dynamic tool usage.
→ Inter-task evolution happens across episodes. This includes supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, or meta-learning to improve behavior between tasks.

Strong systems combine both - fast task-level adaptation and longer-term improvement across workflows.

🤖 How do agents evolve?
→ Reward-based: Learning from success signals, proxy metrics, or human feedback.
→ Imitation-based: Learning from demos, whether human, self-generated, or from other agents.
→ Population-based: Evolving across agent variants running in parallel, selecting the best performers.

Most real-world systems blend these - imitation for bootstrapping, reward for refinement, and population methods for scaling.

📝 What tradeoffs are you managing?
→ Online vs offline learning: Do you allow the agent to adapt in production or only in training windows?
→ On-policy vs off-policy: Is the agent learning from its own actions or from broader data like replay buffers, past runs, or human examples?
→ Granularity: Are you evolving the prompt stack, the memory schema, routing logic, or the core policy?

These choices define how fast you can evolve, how stable it is, and what infrastructure is required.

✅ Where does self-evolution work best?
→ General-purpose agents operate across broad, unpredictable tasks. Feedback is noisy, which makes evolution harder, but worth it.
→ Domain-specific agents - for coding, GUI automation, finance, or healthcare - benefit from structured environments and clearer reward signals, which accelerate feedback loops and enable faster evolution.

⚖️ How do you evaluate progress?
You can’t rely on static benchmarks. You need to measure across five axes:

Adaptivity → Retention → Generalization → Efficiency → Safety

Use both short-horizon and long-horizon evaluation setups to capture real gains over time.

--- Post 164 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7431373664345923584

“Everyone is now a coder with AI.”

Yes, and no.

Vibe coding has made shipping something absurdly easy. Open a co-pilot, describe an idea, get a working UI in minutes. That part is real.

What’s also real is the explosion of AI-generated slop.

I keep hearing: “AI coding copilots gives me messy code.”

Most of the time, the problem isn’t the agent. It’s the spec.

You wouldn’t expect a product manager to ship a feature from a vague, stream-of-consciousness doc. Good PMs write crisp specs: inputs, outputs, constraints, tradeoffs, acceptance criteria.

AI coding agents need the same treatment.

If your prompt reads like a wish, your output will look like a guess.

A few best practices I recommend:

✦ Define the problem clearly before opening the tool. Write down inputs, outputs, constraints.

✦ Ask the agent for a plan before code. Force it to outline the approach and tradeoffs.

✦ Build in layers: core logic → edge cases → tests → optimization.

✦ Be specific in follow-ups. Describe what broke, what you expected, and what actually happened.

AI makes shipping fast.

Engineering thinking makes it correct.

If you treat coding agents like junior engineers who need a clear spec, you’ll stop calling the output “slop” and start calling it leverage.

============================================================

--- Post 165 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 329 reactions, 42 comments, 37 reposts (Score: 2,139)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7398894011962454016

If you’re building anything with LLMs, your system architecture matters more than your prompts.

Most people stop at “call the model, get the output.”

But LLM-native systems need workflows, blueprints that define how multiple LLM calls interact, how routing, evaluation, memory, tools, or chaining come into play.

Here’s a breakdown of 6 core LLM workflows I see in production:

🧠 LLM Augmentation
Classic RAG + tools setup.
The model augments its own capabilities using:
→ Retrieval (e.g., from vector DBs)
→ Tool use (e.g., calculators, APIs)
→ Memory (short-term or long-term context)

🔗 Prompt Chaining Workflow
Sequential reasoning across steps.
Each output is validated (pass/fail) → passed to the next model.
Great for multi-stage tasks like reasoning, summarizing, translating, and evaluating.

🛣 LLM Routing Workflow
Input routed to different models (or prompts) based on the type of task.
Example: classification → Q&A → summarization all handled by different call paths.

📊 LLM Parallelization Workflow (Aggregator)
Run multiple models/tasks in parallel → aggregate the outputs.
Useful for ensembling or sourcing multiple perspectives.

🎼 LLM Parallelization Workflow (Synthesizer)
A more orchestrated version with a control layer.
Think: multi-agent systems with a conductor + synthesizer to harmonize responses.

🧪 Evaluator–Optimizer Workflow
The most underrated architecture.
One LLM generates. Another evaluates (pass/fail + feedback).
This loop continues until quality thresholds are met.

If you’re an AI engineer, don’t just build for single-shot inference. Design workflows that scale, self-correct, and adapt.

📌 Save this visual for your next project architecture review.

--- Post 166 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 310 reactions, 53 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 2,110)

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7394912634149539840

I learned Design Thinking on my first day as a data scientist at IBM, and it quietly became one of the most useful mindsets in my AI career. It taught me to slow down, understand the real problem, and build solutions that actually land with users.

Here’s how I still use it today:

→ Solve the right problem
Most AI teams jump into modeling too early. Design Thinking forces you to reframe, question assumptions, and check if the problem is even defined correctly. 

→ Diverge before you converge
Explore many possibilities, then narrow down. This prevents tunnel vision and leads to better architectures, better experiments, and better product decisions

→ Use the 2x2 grid
Am I exploring or deciding?
Am I thinking about the problem or the solution?
This simple check saves me from going down the wrong path. 

→ Observe behavior, not opinions
The real insights come from watching users, not asking them. The same applies when evaluating agents, prototypes, or internal tools. 

→ Prototype fast, assign ownership
Ideas die when nobody owns them. Execution is where Design Thinking turns into impact. 

This mindset shaped how I build AI systems then, and it still shapes how I build them now.

♻️Share this with your network

I have a love-hate relationship with using ChatGPT for writing.
It’s fast, helpful, and creative, BUT it also flattens my voice, forgets context, and makes everything sound like a mildly enthusiastic AI intern.

Lately I’ve been experimenting with TextJam, and it’s honestly one of the best writing tools I’ve tried in a long time.

It is a tool MEANT for writing! It’s not a chatbot. It’s a collaborative AI editor that actually respects your tone, your edits, and what I love about it the most is that I can do it with my team.

Here’s what stood out for me:
✍️ Pen vs Pencil mode
Write in pen when a sentence is final. Write in pencil when you want help.
The AI learns from what’s in the pen, so it adopts your voice instead of rewriting it.

🧠 Inline prompting
You can leave instructions inside the document, not in a sidebar chat.
Makes your intent clear to both teammates and the AI, and cuts down the back-and-forth.

🕰️ Time-travel + authorship tracking
Watch your doc evolve over time. See exactly who (or what) wrote every sentence. Total transparency, useful for teams, classrooms, and publishing.

🤝 Multi-user + multi-model writing
You can write with teammates and switch between GPT, Claude, Grok, or even open-weight models.

Sometimes I even ask for “second opinions” from two models on the same draft.

It’s the first AI tool I’ve used that feels like a true co-writing space, not just a solo chat with AI in the corner.

If you’re a writer, educator, creative team, or builder who cares about tone, clarity, and authorship, give it a try: textjam.com

Happy writing ✏️

#TextJamPartner

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7372427528214482946

I’ve realized the biggest growth spurts in my career didn’t happen when things were smooth. They happened when I felt uncomfortable. Nervous. Slightly out of my depth.

That’s the essence of a growth mindset- believing you can get better through consistent effort, and actually putting yourself in situations that force you to.

Here are a few practices that have helped me (and can help you too):

👉 Notice when you’re too comfortable. If you’re cruising on autopilot, that’s a red flag. Say yes to a project that intimidates you, learn a new skill, or put yourself in a room where you’re the least experienced person.

👉 Treat your career like a series of experiments. I try things, fail at some, succeed at others, and every outcome becomes data for the next step. Fail fast, but learn faster.

👉 Reframe self-talk. My inner critic is loud. I’ve had to train myself to flip “What if I fail?” into “What if this helps me grow?”. That shift changes everything.

👉 Push boundaries intentionally. Every quarter, I commit to one thing that makes me uncomfortable, whether that’s public speaking, leading a stretch project, or pitching an idea I’m not 100% sure about.

👉 Seek help. Growth doesn’t mean going it alone. I lean on mentors, peers, and feedback. Other people’s playbooks have accelerated my journey in ways I couldn’t have managed solo.

👉 Track your “stretch moments.” Writing down one uncomfortable thing I did each week gives me a record of progress, even when I feel stuck.

The truth is, talent might open the door, but consistency and resilience are what keep you moving forward.

So let me ask you- what’s one uncomfortable thing you’re willing to take on this week?

============================================================

I didn’t start teaching because I had it all figured out. I started because I remember exactly what it felt like to not understand anything, and pretend that I did.

There were barely any resources back then. ML felt like it was built for people who already knew ML.

Andrew Ng’s course was one of the first things that actually made things click for me. And once they clicked, I couldn’t stop. I wanted to learn everything. And then I wanted to share everything.

That’s the thing about teaching, it forces you to actually understand what you’re talking about. You can’t fake your way through it.

Years of learning, building, and sharing eventually led to me becoming a LinkedIn Top Voice in Data and AI. On the same list as Andrew Ng. That still doesn’t feel real to me.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025

If you’re serious about building AI agents, start here 👇

To design agents that actually reason, plan, and act. You need to understand the spectrum of agent architectures.

Here are 9 types of AI agents every AI engineer should know:

💡 1. Simple Reflex Agents
React to current input using rule-based logic (IF-THEN).
→ No memory or learning.
Example: A thermostat that turns on if temp < 20°C.

💡 2. Model-Based Reflex Agents
Use an internal model of the world to infer hidden state.
→ Enable smarter decisions in partially observable settings.
Example: A robot navigating a known room layout to avoid collisions.

💡 3. Goal-Based Agents
Act to achieve defined objectives.
→ Involve search, planning, and pathfinding.
Example: Navigation system choosing optimal route to destination.

💡 4. Utility-Based Agents
Maximize a utility function (e.g. safety, speed, efficiency).
→ Handle trade-offs between competing outcomes.
Example: A self-driving car balancing passenger comfort with urgency.

💡 5. Learning Agents
Improve via feedback—using RL, supervised learning, or both.
→ Adapt and evolve over time.
Example: Chess agent refining tactics through self-play.

💡 6. Multi-Agent Systems (MAS)
Multiple agents interact, collaborate, or compete.
→ Used in swarm robotics, distributed simulations, and games.
Example: Financial agents negotiating trades in a simulated economy.

💡 7. Agentic AI Systems (LLM-based agents)
This is where it gets powerful.
→ Built on large language models + tools + memory + control flow.
→ Can decompose tasks, call APIs, search docs, invoke sub-agents.
Examples: Autogen, CrewAI, LangGraph.

💡 8. Embodied Agents
Agents with a physical presence—robots, drones, etc.
→ Interact with the real world via sensors and actuators.
Example: Boston Dynamics Spot navigating stairs autonomously.

💡 9. Cognitive & Conversational Agents
Human-facing agents designed for natural language interaction.
→ Chatbots, virtual assistants, AI tutors.
Examples: ChatGPT, Alexa, Claude.

If you’re an engineer, don’t just study these, build them. Get hands-on with open-source agent stacks:

→ Autogen (multi-agent orchestration):
https://lnkd.in/d36cU42f
→ CrewAI (LLM agents with roles + memory):
https://lnkd.in/dHBCPmkX
→ LangGraph (agentic control flow, built on LangChain):
https://docs.langgraph.dev

Start with their cookbooks or clone a working repo.
The fastest way to understand agents… is to ship one.

AI agents are only as powerful as the context they operate in.

When context is static, agents plateau. But when context evolves- when it’s refreshed, connected, and accessible, that’s when things start to get interesting.

Take a customer support agent, for example. The most effective ones don’t just respond; they learn. They automatically pull the latest documentation, adapt to new policies, and refine their knowledge base the moment a new feature or update goes live, no manual retraining, no complex setup.

Or look at what’s happening with MCP-connected agents. These systems can browse, search, and act across the web- handling tasks like building a travel plan, managing e-commerce operations, or reacting to real-time data streams to make decisions on their own.

That’s the direction the industry is heading, not toward bigger models, but toward smarter context.
The real progress in AI adoption is happening in how well agents can access, interpret, and act on live information.

Static workflows are becoming the bottleneck. Adaptive, context-aware systems are becoming the differentiator.

They’re transforming how we think about automation, customer experience, and operational intelligence, moving from rule-based processes to systems that continuously learn and evolve.

If you want to explore how this is being applied in practice, Zapier and Apify are hosting a live session on TOMORROW (October 23) showcasing how real-time web data can make agents more dynamic and self-updating.

It’s free to attend, and you’ll get the recording afterward: https://bit.ly/47dwvn2

Happy learning 🚀


hashtag
#ZapierPartner

Date: Oct 19, 2025

Evaluations for LLMs often get treated as an afterthought. But without them, you’re essentially shipping blind.

Honestly, evaluating LLMs is not straightforward.
Some of the biggest challenges I see teams run into:
→ No consistent framework for benchmarking models across tasks
→ Evals are run ad-hoc, not integrated into CI/CD pipelines
→ Hard to simulate multi-turn, agentic workflows with tools + memory
→ Regression detection (“did I break the prompt?”) is manual and error-prone
→ Test suites don’t evolve as systems retrain and scale

Eval Protocol (EP) directly addresses these pain points.
It’s an open protocol + SDK built by Fireworks AI, that brings engineering rigor (unit tests, regression checks), CI/CD pipelines to LLM applications.

Why it matters:
→ EP supports both single-turn and multi-turn/agentic evals
→ You can define evals with thresholds, rollout processors, and even simulate agents with MCP configs
→ It scales with your workflow, from local model selection to long-term system maintenance

If you’re building GenAI applications at scale, you should check this out: evalprotocol.io

Because reliable systems don’t just depend on good prompts or models, they depend on disciplined evaluation.

How are you currently approaching evals in your workflow?

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 276 reactions, 51 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,912)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7380654883198124032

If you’re an AI engineer, this paper is a fascinating exploration of what happens when large language models move from reasoning to acting.

I was reading “Collaborating Action by Action: A Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Embodied Reasoning”, and it’s one of the most fascinating reads I’ve come across lately. It introduces MINDCraft, a framework that lets LLM agents collaborate, plan, and act inside the open-world environment of Minecraft.

What “multi-agent LLM with embodied reasoning” means?
Multiple LLM-driven agents act in a world, not just in text. They see partial state, plan over long horizons, talk to each other, and use tools to change the environment. This setup stresses multi-turn dialogue, partial observability, and quantitative evaluation of behavior, not just answers.

Unlike standard benchmarks that test LLM reasoning in static or text-based settings, MINDCraft gives models agency, letting them see, decide, and act in a complex sandbox environment.

Here’s what makes the framework so insightful:

→ It introduces a task-driven workflow where players (or configs) define high-level goals, like “build a house”, and the agent autonomously plans, queries the LLM, and executes step-by-step commands in Minecraft.

→ The Agent Runtime handles communication between the player’s task, the LLM API, and the Minecraft environment via a rich library of commands and history logs.

→ This setup makes it possible to evaluate not just output accuracy, but behavioral intelligence, things like persistence, resource gathering, planning depth, and tool use.

→ It’s also modular, allowing researchers to plug in different LLMs, run comparative tests, and measure emergent capabilities in a grounded world.

I found this a fascinating read because it moves us closer to understanding how language models perform when they’re not just “thinking,” but acting, reasoning through goals, feedback, and real-world constraints.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025

If you’re an AI engineer today, you should absolutely get familiar with Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Responses API. These two are becoming the backbone of modern agentic systems.

Let’s unpack why 👇

1️⃣ MCP: The Connector Layer

Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes how models connect to external tools, APIs, and live data.

Think of MCP as the wiring system that allows your LLM to talk to the outside world, like tools, databases, vector stores, or your company APIs.

When you build or host your own MCP server, you’re giving your model new abilities to reason, act, and fetch context beyond what’s in its prompt.
This is what turns a plain LLM into an agent.

If you’re working on production-grade AI systems, you’ll often need to:
→ Integrate custom tools or data sources
→ Maintain context across multiple steps
→ Ensure reliable tool invocation and streaming

MCP makes all that possible.

2️⃣ Fireworks AI Responses API

Once your MCP setup is ready, the Responses API is how you connect it to the model.

It’s a next-generation SDK that powers conversational AI. It supports:
→ Stateful conversations (previous_response_id)
→ Real-time streaming for interactive apps
→ Tool calling and safe execution

The Fireworks Responses API follows the same schema and format as OpenAI’s Responses endpoint.

So if you’ve been using OpenAI models, you can switch to open-source models on Fireworks AI with almost zero code change.

You’ll unlock faster inference, lower latency, and broader model choice, including open models like Qwen, Mistral, Llama, and Gemma hosted natively on Fireworks AI.

You can even mix and match:
✅ Use MCP to extend model capabilities
✅ Use Responses API to orchestrate reasoning, tool use, and streaming

That’s the magic combo powering many of today’s most advanced agentic systems.

3️⃣ Why This Matters

Agentic systems aren’t built with just a model call.
They’re built with context, memory, and actions. MCP and the Responses API make that orchestration reliable, stateful, and production-ready.

If you’re serious about building enterprise-grade AI apps, start experimenting with:
→ MCP Examples (connecting to MCP servers and invoking tools)
→ Conversation Chaining (using previous_response_id to continue dialogue)
→ Streaming Output (for responsive real-time interfaces)
→ Ephemeral Mode (for privacy-friendly, non-persistent interactions)

Happy Building 🚀

--- Post 184 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 258 reactions, 57 comments, 14 reposts (Score: 1,888)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7368683518815535104

If you’re building with LLMs, it’s not enough to just know “how to prompt” or “how to fine-tune.”
To design better systems, you need to understand the full learning journey of these models.

LLMs evolve through four major paradigms, each with its own purpose:

➡ 1. Model Offline Pretraining (MOP):
This is the foundation. Models are trained on massive amounts of static data (books, websites, code) to learn language patterns. Think of this as giving the model its basic vocabulary, grammar, and reasoning abilities.

➡ 2. Model Online Adaptation (MOA):
Once pretrained, models need to be adapted for real-world use. This is where supervised fine-tuning and alignment come in- teaching the model to follow instructions, align with human values, or handle specific tasks like summarization or Q&A.

➡ 3. Multi-Agent Orchestration (MAO):
One model isn’t always enough. In many applications, multiple agents interact, exchanging messages, debating, or collaborating. This is how you get workflows where one model can plan, another can check the plan, and a third can execute. It makes systems more reliable and scalable.

➡ 4. Multi-Agent Self-Evolving (MASE):
The frontier. Here, agents don’t just interact with each other- they learn from their environment. They can optimize their memory, tools, or workflows based on feedback, making the system more adaptive over time without constant retraining.

Why does this matter❓
Because every production-grade AI system you build will live somewhere on this spectrum. 

Understanding these paradigms helps you answer:
→ Is my model only pretrained, or has it been adapted for my use case?
→ Would orchestration across multiple agents make it more reliable?
→ Do I need self-evolving feedback loops to keep improving performance?

LLMs are becoming part of dynamic systems that adapt, interact, and improve, so it's important to design based on which paradigm fits best for the problem we’re solving.

Image inspiration from paper: https://lnkd.in/dUBK3igS

If you want to future-proof your career, don’t get stuck trying to be the best at just one thing.

The reality is, careers don’t grow in straight lines anymore. The people who stand out are the ones who build range. The ones who layer skills, adapt fast, and connect dots that others don’t see.

Here are four ways to start building your moat right now:

➡️ Layer your skills. If you’re great at design, add some business sense. If you’re strong with data, work on communication. Every new layer makes you more valuable and harder to replace.

➡️ Get comfortable with pivots. Tech shifts overnight. The people who thrive aren’t the ones who resist change, they’re the ones who pick up new tools quickly and aren’t afraid to start from scratch when needed.

➡️ Show outcomes, not just skills. Don’t stop at saying “I know Python.” Talk about the results you drove with it. Did you save your team time? Did you build something that scaled? That’s what people actually care about.

➡️ Go deep, then go wide. Master one area first, then branch out. The real edge comes when you bring together skills from different domains and create something unique.

This is the advice I wish I had heard ten years ago. If it clicks with you, share it forward. Someone in your network probably needs to hear this today.

--- Post 188 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 295 reactions, 36 comments, 5 reposts (Score: 1,845)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7366279340029804544

Most engineers shy away from the idea of personal branding.

The common misconception is that it means becoming an influencer on social media. But that’s not the case.

Personal branding is simply about how you show up, whether inside your organization or to the outside world. And the way you approach it depends on your goals:

→ Are the opportunities you’re looking for tied to your company and leadership team? That’s internal personal branding.

→ Do you want to attract new roles, projects, or industry visibility? That’s external personal branding.

Both are valid. Both are powerful. And both are subjective to where you want to grow.

I often break it down into the 4P approach:

👉 Persona – Define your target audience. Who are you trying to reach, and how do you want to be perceived?
👉 Peculiarity – Highlight what makes you unique. Your skills, your perspective, your edge.
👉 Promise – Communicate the value you bring. Not just what you do, but how you solve problems and make lives easier.
👉 Proof – Back it up with evidence. Showcase projects, outcomes, or impact that validate your credibility.

For engineers, personal branding doesn’t mean adding “content creator” to your resume.
It means being intentional about how you present your work, your expertise, and the value you create, so the right people recognize it.

The question is: have you thought about what your brand says about you today? 

Tell me in the comments below, and I will add my thoughts to it 😊

------------
If this resonated with you, share this with your network ♻️ 

I poured my heart into writing my book "What's your worth?" which is a practical handbook for anyone to build a personal brand. It is not a one-size-fits-all book, it will challenge you to find your ikigai and help you discover the true passion that leads you to your personal brand. 

📕 You can find a blurb to read from my book here: whatsyourworthbook.com

--- Post 192 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 271 reactions, 40 comments, 39 reposts (Score: 1,833)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7428125525623304192

If you’re serious about building AI agents, start here 👇

To design agents that actually reason, plan, and act. You need to understand the spectrum of agent architectures.

Here are 9 types of AI agents every AI engineer should know:

💡 1. Simple Reflex Agents
React to current input using rule-based logic (IF-THEN).
→ No memory or learning.
Example: A thermostat that turns on if temp < 20°C.

💡 2. Model-Based Reflex Agents
Use an internal model of the world to infer hidden state.
→ Enable smarter decisions in partially observable settings.
Example: A robot navigating a known room layout to avoid collisions.

💡 3. Goal-Based Agents
Act to achieve defined objectives.
→ Involve search, planning, and pathfinding.
Example: Navigation system choosing optimal route to destination.

💡 4. Utility-Based Agents
Maximize a utility function (e.g. safety, speed, efficiency).
→ Handle trade-offs between competing outcomes.
Example: A self-driving car balancing passenger comfort with urgency.

💡 5. Learning Agents
Improve via feedback—using RL, supervised learning, or both.
→ Adapt and evolve over time.
Example: Chess agent refining tactics through self-play.

💡 6. Multi-Agent Systems (MAS)
Multiple agents interact, collaborate, or compete.
→ Used in swarm robotics, distributed simulations, and games.
Example: Financial agents negotiating trades in a simulated economy.

💡 7. Agentic AI Systems (LLM-based agents)
This is where it gets powerful.
→ Built on large language models + tools + memory + control flow.
→ Can decompose tasks, call APIs, search docs, invoke sub-agents.
Examples: Autogen, CrewAI, LangGraph.

💡 8. Embodied Agents
Agents with a physical presence—robots, drones, etc.
→ Interact with the real world via sensors and actuators.
Example: Boston Dynamics Spot navigating stairs autonomously.

💡 9. Cognitive & Conversational Agents
Human-facing agents designed for natural language interaction.
→ Chatbots, virtual assistants, AI tutors.
Examples: ChatGPT, Alexa, Claude.

If you’re an engineer, don’t just study these, build them. Get hands-on with open-source agent stacks:

→ Autogen (multi-agent orchestration):
https://lnkd.in/d36cU42f
→ CrewAI (LLM agents with roles + memory):
https://lnkd.in/dHBCPmkX
→ LangGraph (agentic control flow, built on LangChain):
https://docs.langgraph.dev

Start with their cookbooks or clone a working repo.
The fastest way to understand agents… is to ship one.

If you are aspiring to be an AI engineer or upskill yourself in AI in 2026, understanding how to evaluate LLM applications is very crutial.
Most teams underestimate this part. Building an LLM app is relatively fast. Knowing whether it actually works in production is the hard part.

Evaluating LLM systems is challenging because you are no longer judging just correctness. You are evaluating usefulness, safety, faithfulness to sources, consistency across runs, cost, latency, and real user outcomes across prompts, tools, RAG pipelines, and agents. 

Because of this complexity, many teams default to "vibe-eval". If outputs look good in a few examples, they ship. This works until models change, prompts regress, retrieval breaks, or user behavior shifts. Without evals, failures show up only after users complain.

Learning AI evals matters because:
✦ Evals turn LLM development from guesswork into engineering
✦ They help catch regressions when models, prompts, or tools change
✦ They are essential for scaling RAG systems and agentic workflows
✦ In 2026, strong teams use hybrid evals combining automated metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, and targeted human review 

If you want to upskill yourself for AI roles in 2026, evals are no longer optional. They are a core skill.

Arvind Narayanamurthy and I are hosting an upcoming workshop (15th Feb) on AI evals where we go deep into how this is done in real systems. If you want to level up your AI skills for 2026, do register for it: https://lnkd.in/dQSR7yhU

--- Post 197 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 266 reactions, 43 comments, 18 reposts (Score: 1,796)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7367578856209166337

Prompt engineering is not dead, but it’s no longer enough. Context Engineering is what is filling the gaps 👇

If you’ve ever built a serious LLM workflow, you already know: most failures don’t come from bad prompts, they come from missing, outdated, or misformatted context.

That’s why I’ve shifted from obsessing over the perfect "prompt", to designing robust context systems around the model.

Andrej Karpathy famously tweeted- "+1 to context engineering, over prompt engineering"

Let’s talk about Context Engineering.

🔍 What is it?

Context engineering = building dynamic systems that deliver the right info, in the right format, at the right time so the model can actually accomplish the task.

It’s more than just phrasing. It’s about memory, retrieval, structured outputs, history, and tool awareness.

Context engineering is what allows the model to act coherently across long conversations, use retrieved knowledge intelligently, and collaborate with other agents/tools.

🧱 Four Pillars of Context Engineering:

→ Write: Persist info that lives outside the context window
✦ Scratchpads, logs, long-term memory

→ Select: Pull in only relevant context
✦ Redis retrieval, memory recall, tool specs

→ Compress: Summarize to save tokens
✦ Auto-trimming (like Claude), compact context windows

→ Isolate: Prevent interference between contexts
✦ Multi-agent sub-contexts, sandboxed processes

🔄 Lifecycle of Context Engineering:
Acquire → Process → Store → Retrieve → Update
It’s an entire system. Not a one-shot prompt.

🧠 Prompt vs. Context Engineering:

✦ Prompt Engineering = phrasing the instruction
✦ Context Engineering = building the system around the model (memory, retrieval, tools, workflows)

If you’re building agents, multi-turn apps, or systems with state, context engineering is non-negotiable.

⚙️ Some tools I use:
→ LangGraph / LangSmith for tracing + long-term state
→ LlamaIndex, vector DBs for retrieval
→ Orchestration libs for multi-agent workflows

🚫 Common pitfalls to avoid:
→ Overengineering too early
→ Garbage in, garbage out (poor validation)
→ Token bloat, context drift, or stale data
→ Ignoring security/privacy of memory layers

The future isn’t just about prompt engineering. It’s about designing intelligent context layers around your models.

Save this for your next AI system design session.

--- Post 200 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 240 reactions, 52 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 1,738)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7362513476319096833

Most LLM prompting strategies today follow a simple Bayesian lens: Input → Model → Output

This single-shot inference assumes the model will generate the best answer in one go.

But real-world reasoning isn’t always that linear, not for humans, and certainly not for LLMs.

Here are 3 powerful reasoning enhancements you should be using 👇

1️⃣ Chain of Thought (CoT)
Prompt the model to reason step by step instead of giving the final answer immediately.
Much like how a person solves a math problem or breaks down a riddle, CoT encourages explicit intermediate steps.

→ Prompt style: “Let’s think step by step…”
→ Use it for: logic puzzles, math problems, reasoning-heavy tasks
→ Why it works: helps reduce hallucinations and improves interpretability


2️⃣ Tree of Thought (ToT)
LLMs explore multiple reasoning paths instead of just one.
This turns reasoning into a search problem, where the model generates various branches and then evaluates the best one.

→ Think of it like a planning tree
→ Use when there are multiple valid paths or creative strategies
→ Great for: multi-step planning, ideation, decision-making


3️⃣ Self-Consistency with CoT
Instead of sampling one CoT response, you sample many, and pick the most consistent final answer.

→ LLMs are stochastic, different samples yield different reasoning paths
→ Multiple responses → Diverse reasoning → Majority vote improves reliability
→ Best for: increasing accuracy, reducing variance, and improving robustness

Prompting is no longer just about what you ask, it’s about how the model reasons through it.

As prompting becomes a form of reasoning orchestration, techniques like CoT, ToT, and self-consistency are essential tools in every AI engineer’s workflow.

💬 If you’re working on reasoning-heavy tasks, don’t just prompt. Engineer the reasoning.

Text-only RAG is already outdated!

In real world, your data isn’t just text. It’s scanned contracts. Screenshots from customers. Financial dashboards. Product images. Tables and charts.

And if your RAG pipeline can’t process those, you’re missing half the picture.

That’s why Multimodal RAG matters.

Instead of treating non-text data as an afterthought, it allows you to:
→ Retrieve and reason over images + text together
→ Parse OCR, diagrams, and screenshots natively
→ Feed structured/tabular data directly into the context window
→ Build apps that can actually read dashboards, handle screenshots, or review scanned contracts

This isn’t just an optimization. It’s the difference between a demo app and a production system that works with real-world inputs.

We’ll be breaking down Multimodal RAG and much more in RAG Workshop: Towards Mastery (Part 2) 🔥

In Part 1, we focused on foundations- retrieval pipelines, hybrid search, metadata filters.

In Part 2, Arvind Narayanamurthy and I are going deeper:
→ RAG as a memory layer for short- and long-term recall
→ Context engineering: selecting, compressing, and ordering context
→ Architectural trade-offs across RAG patterns
→ Live build 1: Multimodal RAG app (text + images)
→ Live build 2: Graph RAG app with LangChain + graph database

--- Post 204 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 259 reactions, 37 comments, 22 reposts (Score: 1,709)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7430659538053214208

Anthropic just published their 2026 Agentic Coding Trends Report, and it’s worth a read.

Coding has always been one of the most powerful generative AI use cases. 

Among all the GenAI experiments happening across industries, it consistently ranks as one of the highest ROI applications. And this report shows why that’s only accelerating.

Here are my top takeaways:

1/ The role is shifting from implementer → orchestrator. Engineers are spending less time writing code and more time directing agents, reviewing output, and making architectural decisions. The “full-stack engineer” now has a new meaning.

2/ Multi-agent systems are becoming the new default. Single agents are giving way to coordinated teams of specialized agents working in parallel. One company cut warehouse staffing time from weeks to under 72 hours using this approach.

3/ Task horizons are expanding dramatically. Early agents handled bug fixes in minutes. Now they’re building entire features over hours. For example- Rakuten had Claude Code implement a complex ML method in a 12.5M line codebase, autonomously- in 7 hours, with 99.9% accuracy.

4/ About 27% of AI-assisted work is work that wouldn’t have happened otherwise. This stat hit me. It’s not just speed. It’s unlocking entirely new capacity.

5/ The human is still very much in the loop. Developers report using AI in ~60% of work but fully delegating only 0–20% of tasks. It’s deeply collaborative, not replacement.

My 2 cents 🤌
I’m genuinely bullish on agentic coding, and I think it’s still massively underexplored.

There's an enormous amount of legacy software out there. COBOL systems, decade-old codebases, technical debt that teams simply don’t have bandwidth to touch. Generative models are uniquely positioned to translate, refactor, and fix this code at scale. That’s a trillion-dollar problem hiding in plain sight.

And beyond new development, I’m especially bullish on agents for code review and code fixing. Not just catching bugs, but systematically improving security, consistency, and quality across large codebases. That’s where I think the next wave of value gets unlocked.

We’re still early. The teams figuring this out now will look very different from everyone else by end of 2026.

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419559670618652672

One of the most thought provoking conversations I have ever had was with Reid Hoffman.

What really stayed with me was his perspective on AI. Yes, we absolutely need to be aware of the risks and the side effects of AI as a toolkit. But when we put all our focus on fear or pessimism, we lose sight of the mission that brought us here in the first place.

Reid frames AI as an enhancer of human agency, not a replacement for it. A way to amplify what humans can do, from creativity to productivity to access to opportunity. That idea resonated deeply with me. Progress does not come from being blind to risks, but it also does not come from letting fear dominate the conversation.

I loved diving into this philosophy with him and reflecting on how we build, deploy, and talk about AI in a way that stays grounded in optimism and responsibility.

Watch the full podcast here: https://lnkd.in/dgAh7KyZ

--- Post 207 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 267 reactions, 30 comments, 31 reposts (Score: 1,697)

If you’re an aspiring AI engineer trying to understand how the industry is moving beyond LLMs, here’s a quick eagle’s-eye view of one of the most fascinating frontiers in AI today: 𝗔𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗰 𝗔𝗜 𝗦𝘆𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗺𝘀.

We’ve reached a point where large language models can generate text, summarize papers, write code, and even reason, but that’s not enough anymore.

The next leap isn’t about bigger models. It’s about autonomy, with systems that can not only generate but also decide, act, and adapt in the real world.
That’s where Agentic AI Systems come in.

These are goal-driven, adaptive platforms capable of orchestrating complex workflows, making independent decisions, and using memory to 𝗥𝗲𝗮𝘀𝗼𝗻 → 𝗔𝗰𝘁 → 𝗔𝗱𝗮𝗽𝘁.

 Instead of just prompting a model for a single response, you’re designing a network of intelligent components that:
→ Understand goals and constraints
→ Plan actions through orchestration frameworks
→ Execute via tools, APIs, or other agents
→ Observe results, learn, and improve over time

This shift, from intelligence to autonomous intelligence, is why agentic systems have become one of the most important topics for modern AI engineers.

𝗪𝗵𝘆 𝗧𝗵𝗶𝘀 𝗠𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝘀
→ For AI Engineers:
Agentic architectures are redefining how applications are built- from RAG pipelines and copilots to autonomous research or data systems. Understanding gateways, planners, orchestrators, memory layers, and evaluation loops will become a must-have skill set.

→ For Tech Leaders:
If you’re leading teams or evaluating where AI fits into your business, this is your blueprint for understanding how next-gen systems will operate- safely, scalably, and with clear policy and observability layers.

Happy learning & Happy Building 🚀

--- Post 208 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 235 reactions, 47 comments, 18 reposts (Score: 1,681)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7389542213627654144

If you’re an aspiring AI engineer, RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) is one of the most essential systems to understand.

Almost every serious AI product today, from copilots to research assistants uses RAG to combine retrieval and generation for accurate, grounded responses.

Here’s a simplified way to look at it:

𝗛𝗼𝘄 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗦𝘆𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗺𝘀 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸
RAG is made up of three key layers:

- 𝗥𝗲𝘁𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿: Finds the most relevant documents or chunks using embeddings and vector search.
- 𝗚𝗲𝗻𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗼𝗿: Produces an answer using both the user’s query and the retrieved context.
- 𝗢𝗿𝗰𝗵𝗲𝘀𝘁𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗼𝗿: Manages the flow, deciding what to retrieve, how to construct the prompt, and what goes into the LLM.

The pipeline looks like this:
𝗨𝘀𝗲𝗿 𝗤𝘂𝗲𝗿𝘆 → 𝗥𝗲𝘁𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿 → 𝗖𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗲𝘅𝘁 𝗙𝘂𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻 → 𝗚𝗲𝗻𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗼𝗿 → 𝗢𝘂𝘁𝗽𝘂𝘁

𝗠𝘆 𝟮 𝗰𝗲𝗻𝘁𝘀 🤌 
- Start simple with off-the-shelf retrievers like BM25 or bge-large-en and gradually move to hybrid retrieval.
- Chunk documents semantically, not by fixed token size.
- Evaluate retriever and generator separately before optimizing the full system.
- Cache embedding results and retrieval outputs to cut latency.
- Always measure recall@k and hallucination rate — not just accuracy.

𝗪𝗵𝗲𝗿𝗲 𝘁𝗼 𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗿𝘁
If you’re learning RAG for the first time:
- Build a small document QA system using open datasets like arXiv or Wikipedia.
- Experiment with LangChain or LlamaIndex to understand orchestration.
- Run side-by-side tests using different retrievers and observe how output quality changes.
- Finally, deploy a lightweight prototype with Fireworks or another inference engine to see how retrieval affects cost and performance in production.

If you want to have a deep-dive into RAG systems, Arvind and I did 2 workshops recently:
- 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗙𝗼𝘂𝗻𝗱𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝘀 (𝟰-𝗵𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸𝘀𝗵𝗼𝗽): 
https://lnkd.in/gjx5WNP6

- 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝗧𝗼𝘄𝗮𝗿𝗱𝘀 𝗠𝗮𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗿 (𝟰-𝗵𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸𝘀𝗵𝗼𝗽):
https://lnkd.in/dJGR3zQU

--- Post 210 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 275 reactions, 27 comments, 16 reposts (Score: 1,677)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7360339297251524609

If you’re building LLM applications today, reasoning is where the real leverage lies. And yet, I see a lot of engineers still treating LLM outputs as a single-shot black box.

LLMs can reason, but only if you give them the right scaffolding and the right post-training.

Here’s a mental model I’ve been using to think about LLM reasoning methods (see chart below):

✅ Inference-time reasoning methods:
These are techniques that can be applied at inference time, without needing to retrain your model:

→ Tree of Thoughts (ToT), search through reasoning paths

→ Chain of Thought (CoT) prompting, prompt models to generate intermediate reasoning steps

→ Reasoning + Acting, use tools or function calls during reasoning

→ Self-feedback, prompt the model to critique and refine its own output

→ Episodic Memory Agents, maintain a memory buffer to improve multi-step reasoning

→ Self-consistency, sample multiple reasoning paths and select the most consistent answer

✅ Training-time enhancements:
Where things get really powerful is when you post-train your model to improve reasoning, using human annotation or policy optimization:

→ Use Preference pairs and Reward Models to tune for better reasoning (RFT, Proximal PO, KL Regularization)

→ Apply RLHF, PPO + KL, Rejection Sampling + SFT, Advantage Estimation, and other advanced techniques to guide the model’s policy

→ Leverage multiple paths, offline trajectories, and expert demonstrations to expose the model to rich reasoning signals during training

Here are my 2 cents 🫰

If you want production-grade LLM reasoning, you’ll need both,
→ Smart inference-time scaffolds to boost reasoning without slowing latency too much
→ Carefully tuned post-training loops to align the model’s policy with high-quality reasoning patterns
→ We’re also seeing increasing use of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and reference-free grading to further improve reasoning quality and stability.

I’m seeing more and more teams combine both strategies, and the gap between "vanilla prompting" and "optimized reasoning loops" is only getting wider.

3️⃣ Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (RLVR)
Use simulated or programmatic reward models when human feedback is too expensive.

Learn how to build Retrieval-Augmented Generation systems- ingest documents, create embeddings, stitch context, and prompt LLMs.
https://lnkd.in/dFkgD6RQ

Master multi-hop retrieval, hybrid sparse-dense search, and complex reranking for multi-document use cases.

Benchmark sentence encoders, tune indexes (FAISS/Pinecone), and understand latency vs recall trade-offs.

Design prompts that guide reasoning step-by-step, manage templates, and dynamically inject context.

Use Model Context Protocol (MCP) to coordinate LLMs with APIs and other agents for seamless A2A handoffs.
https://lnkd.in/dfjvxhvP

9️⃣ Long-Context Window Strategies
Handle 100k+ token sequences using smart chunking, hierarchical retrieval, and memory-augmented architectures.
https://lnkd.in/djgkXGaZ

🔟 Model Efficiency: Quantization & Distillation
Shrink models with post-training quantization (4/8-bit), pruning, and knowledge distillation for faster, cheaper inference.

1️⃣1️⃣ LLMOps & Model Monitoring
Build CI/CD pipelines for LLM deployment, track drift, monitor hallucinations, and set up alerting for cost or performance issues.

Measure with perplexity, ROUGE, F1, and human evaluations. Automate benchmarks to compare models at scale.

--- Post 217 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 252 reactions, 33 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 1,602)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7361200450911559680

It’s not every day you get to see one of your biggest inspirations live on stage.
Today at Ai4 - Artificial Intelligence Conferences in Las Vegas, I had the incredible experience of watching Geoffrey Hinton speak, and wow, he’s just as sharp, witty, and thought-provoking as ever.

Hinton has been one of the people who inspired me to work in AI in the first place, and seeing him in person reminded me why he’s called the Godfather of AI. At 75+, his clarity of thought and his ability to simplify the most complex ideas is unmatched.

A few takeaways that really stayed with me:

→ On why AI can surpass human intelligence: He used a simple but powerful analogy. As humans, our experiences live in our own minds — we can share them, but it’s slow and imperfect. AI models, on the other hand, can be trained in completely different domains and instantly share their “experiences” by exchanging model weights. That means knowledge transfer at a speed and scale we simply can’t match. It’s a reminder that the real leap with AI isn’t just capability, but connectivity.

→ On AI in healthcare: He spoke about how MRI and CT scans capture far more data than a human can process, and how AI has already uncovered patterns and diagnoses that even top specialists missed. This isn’t just about working faster; it’s about revealing insights that were previously invisible, opening doors for earlier diagnosis, better treatment, and even accelerated drug discovery.

→ On building superintelligence responsibly: If we’re creating something more capable than ourselves, we must embed what he calls a motherly instinct- a built-in sense to avoid causing harm, even when pursuing a goal. That’s not a technical feature; it’s a design philosophy.

→ On global regulation: Collaboration will be hard without shared interests, but he believes areas like counter-terrorism and cybercrime will push countries to work together. It’s a good reminder that sometimes progress on the big challenges starts with the most urgent and tangible ones.

→ On protecting AI research in academia: He’s deeply concerned about funding cuts to university research. Nearly every major AI breakthrough began in academia before industry adoption, removing that pipeline risks slowing the long-tail innovation we all depend on.

→ On what’s next: He believes the next 5–20 years will be about learning to coexist with superintelligence, and that this future is coming faster than most expect. For me, that’s a call to approach AI not just as a technology problem, but as a human growth problem.

Walking out of that room, I felt the same mix of curiosity, urgency, and optimism that first brought me into AI.

The models will keep getting smarter. Our challenge is making sure we do too, and find the best way to co-exist with superintelligence.

============================================================

--- Post 219 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 258 reactions, 26 comments, 20 reposts (Score: 1,590)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7390783500934713344

Reinforcement Learning(RL) has quietly become one of the most important techniques shaping the evolution of LLM fine-tuning.

For years, we optimized models through supervised learning, predicting the next token or minimizing cross-entropy loss.

But as generative models scaled, we needed them to reason, align with intent, and adapt to human feedback in more complex ways. That’s where Reinforcement Learning (RL) entered the picture.

At its core, RL is about interaction and feedback.
An agent learns by interacting with an environment to maximize reward.

In the context of large language models, the agent is the model itself.

Each action is the next token it generates, and the reward is a signal derived from metrics or human preferences that measures how aligned the output is with the desired goal.

Here’s a quick technical primer on the RL methods now powering GenAI fine-tuning:

1. RL Fine-Tuning (RLFT)
We adapt a pre-trained model to new objectives like truthfulness, coherence, and safety using policy gradient algorithms such as PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization). Instead of minimizing loss, the model improves through iterative reward-driven optimization.

2. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)
Human preference data trains a Reward Model (RM), which then guides fine-tuning through PPO. RLHF was key in aligning early LLMs, making outputs more helpful, factual, and instruction-following.

3. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)
A newer, more efficient approach. DPO skips the Reward Model and the full RL loop. It reframes alignment as a direct optimization task, teaching the model to prefer human-approved responses through a simplified objective function.

It’s computationally stable, theoretically grounded in RL, and rapidly becoming a standard for GenAI alignment.

Reinforcement Learning is no longer just a research concept. It is the foundation of how large language models learn to reason, align, and self-improve.

♻️ Share this with your network to spread learning
🔔Follow me for more data and AI insights

--- Post 220 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 258 reactions, 27 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,582)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7387897511572627456

If you’re preparing for an AI engineering interview, here’s a concept you can’t skip: context engineering.

In most LLM systems, the biggest reason models fail isn’t because the prompt is bad, it’s because the context is incomplete, irrelevant, or misaligned. The model can only reason as well as the information it’s given.

That’s why modern AI engineers don’t just “prompt” a model, they design context pipelines that deliver the right information at the right time.

Context engineering is about building systems that help an LLM think more effectively by combining memory, retrieval, summarization, and tool awareness. It’s what allows a model to handle multi-turn conversations, reference prior steps, and stay coherent across workflows.

Here’s a simple way to think about it:
→ Write - store what matters beyond the context window (logs, scratchpads, memory stores)
→ Select - retrieve only what’s relevant (using vector search, embeddings, or structured metadata)
→ Compress - summarize long histories to fit token limits without losing signal
→ Isolate - separate agent contexts to prevent cross-task interference

TL;DR, prompt engineering helps you speak to the model effectively, while context engineering helps you build an environment the model can reason within.

If you’re building AI agents, RAG pipelines, or any application that depends on long-term memory or dynamic reasoning, context engineering isn’t optional, it’s foundational.

Happy learning 🚀

============================================================

If you’re getting started with building AI agents, this Agentic AI Toolkit Cheatsheet will come in handy 👇

You’ll quickly realize it’s not just about choosing a model. You need orchestration, memory, retrieval, evaluation, and a solid deployment stack.

And if you’re not careful, your architecture turns into a mess of disconnected tools.

That’s why I put together this Agentic AI Toolkit Cheatsheet.

It organizes the full stack of what goes into building production-grade AI agents- from model hosting to feedback loops, so you can stop guessing what’s missing in your pipeline and start shipping actual systems.

Here’s what it covers:

🔧 Foundation Models & Hosting
🎛️ Model Customization (SFT, RFT, RLHF)
🤖 Agent Frameworks
🧩 Orchestration & Workflow
📥 Data Ingestion & Preprocessing
🧠 Embedding & Retrieval
🧷 Memory & Knowledge Storage
🔌 Tool Integration
🧪 Evaluation & Monitoring
🖥️ User Interfaces
🚀 Deployment & Hosting


💡 A few tips if you’re working on your first agentic stack:

→ Choose one tool per layer to start, depth over breadth.
→ Build iteratively and introduce evaluation as early as possible.
→ Use event-driven workflows to manage complexity.
→ Think about memory and feedback as first-class components, not afterthoughts.


📌 Bookmark this.
📚 Use it to audit your stack.
🛠️ Build with it.
📤 Share it with a teammate who’s lost in the sea of GenAI tooling.

--- Post 229 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 213 reactions, 47 comments, 2 reposts (Score: 1,539)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7429924624043495424

AI agents are still one of the most talked-about topics in the AI landscape right now.

Rightfully so, but there are a few things which aren't spoken about as much (which we should now)

If you are thinking seriously about where AI is headed in 2026, I believe there are four areas that deserve much more attention.

1/ Hyperscale AI data centers. The competitive advantage is increasingly shifting toward inference efficiency. Training breakthroughs matter, but real-world adoption depends on cost-efficient, low-latency inference at scale. Energy efficiency, hardware and software integration, and infrastructure optimization are becoming strategic differentiators. The companies that can run large models reliably and affordably in production will move faster than everyone else.

2/ Persistent AI systems, sometimes described as AI companions. We are moving beyond one-off interactions toward long-running systems with memory and context. This introduces new engineering challenges around privacy, alignment, lifecycle management, and reliability. Building a system that remembers users responsibly and performs consistently over time is far more complex than building a conversational interface.

3/ Generative coding in production environments. Coding models are improving rapidly, but the real shift is not about replacing engineers. It is about changing the abstraction layer at which they operate. Engineers are spending less time on routine implementation and more time on system architecture, evaluation, guardrails, and long-term maintainability. Code quality, correctness, and accountability still remain human responsibilities.

4/ Mechanistic interpretability. As AI systems begin to influence higher-stakes decisions, interpretability moves from research curiosity to operational necessity. Organizations will need practical methods to understand, audit, and constrain model behavior. Enterprise trust and safety depend on this capability.

Agents will continue to evolve, and experimentation will continue. But infrastructure, efficiency, persistent system design, and interpretability are the foundations that will shape the next real phase of AI deployment.

What I see is, 2026 will not just be about what models can generate. It will be about who can deploy them responsibly and sustainably at scale.

Source: MIT Technology Review

============================================================

--- Post 230 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 241 reactions, 31 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,537)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7411642084589350912

🚨BREAKING: Meta just acquired Manus AI 

What stands out to me about Manus is not novelty, but direction.

Manus has been building agentic systems that focus on execution: planning tasks, navigating browsers, using tools, and completing workflows with limited human input. That’s a very different bet than optimizing AI for better conversation or more polished responses.

And I think this acquisition makes one thing clear.

We’re moving away from AI as something you interact with and toward AI as something that operates.

For a while, progress was measured by how well models talked. Now the question is whether systems can act reliably in the real world: across tools, across steps, under constraints, and with failure modes that actually matter.

From that lens, Meta’s move feels less like a product acquisition and more like an architectural one. Pairing agentic capabilities with Meta’s distribution means AI stops being an assistive layer and starts becoming embedded infrastructure. Something that runs in the background of real workflows, not just prompts.

This shift also raises harder questions than we’re used to asking.

Autonomy changes the trust model. Execution surfaces new risks. Evaluation, control, and guardrails stop being abstract concerns and become first-order design problems.

In my view, this is where the next phase of AI competition will play out.

Not in who has the biggest model, but in who can build systems that act well, recover gracefully, and remain aligned at scale.

The Manus acquisition isn’t the end state.
It’s a signal of what the industry is optimizing for next.

============================================================

--- Post 236 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 201 reactions, 45 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,485)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7413630297298255872

Not all multi-agent AI systems should be designed the same way.

When people talk about “AI agents,” it often sounds like there’s one right architecture. In reality, multi-agent system design depends heavily on what problem you’re solving and what constraints you’re operating under.

A simple way to think about it is to start with the core challenge.

✦ Speed and simplicity needed
A centralized agent setup works best here. One decision-maker, fewer moving parts, easier to build and operate. This is why many MVPs and early-stage products start with a centralized agent coordinating tools and workflows.

✦ System must never fail
If reliability and fault tolerance are non-negotiable, decentralized agents make more sense. Each agent operates independently, reducing single points of failure. This pattern is common in critical systems where resilience matters more than simplicity.

✦ 10+ agents that need coordination
Once you’re dealing with many agents, a hierarchical design helps. You introduce manager or orchestrator agents that delegate tasks, monitor execution, and maintain control. This structure scales better for large teams and organizations.

✦ Complex, real-world domains
Most enterprise systems end up hybrid. They combine centralized control where needed, decentralized execution for resilience, and hierarchy for coordination. Food delivery apps, large enterprise workflows, and production agent systems usually fall into this category.

TL;DR
- Start centralized for your MVP
- Add decentralized components as reliability becomes important
- Move toward hierarchical coordination as the system grows
- End up hybrid at enterprise scale

Multi-agent AI design is less about following a trend and more about making deliberate architectural choices based on your use case.

Diagram credits- Galileo

--- Post 237 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 219 reactions, 37 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 1,477)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7393092304636616704

Vision-Language Models connect what AI sees with what it reads and reasons.

They’re the foundation of AI systems that can interpret charts, medical images, retail shelves, or product catalogs.

But a generic VLM doesn’t understand your domain’s visual language.
That’s where fine-tuning becomes essential.

𝐖𝐡𝐲 𝐟𝐢𝐧𝐞-𝐭𝐮𝐧𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐚 𝐕𝐋𝐌 𝐦𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐬

A pretrained VLM already knows the basics of visual-text reasoning.
Fine-tuning helps it specialize for your domain.

→ In healthcare, it learns to detect anomalies in MRIs and X-rays.
→ In retail, it interprets shelf images and product layouts.
→ In enterprise, it extracts structured data from invoices and reports.

You’re not rebuilding intelligence, you’re refining perception to fit your use case.

𝐇𝐨𝐰 𝐋𝐨𝐑𝐀 𝐦𝐚𝐤𝐞𝐬 𝐢𝐭 𝐞𝐟𝐟𝐢𝐜𝐢𝐞𝐧𝐭

Full model fine-tuning is expensive and compute-heavy.
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) keeps the base model frozen and trains only small adapter layers.

That means:
→ Faster training cycles
→ Smaller memory footprint
→ Lower compute costs
→ Domain adapters that are easy to swap in and out

You can maintain one base model and multiple lightweight adapters for each use case such as invoices, medical forms, or retail analytics.

𝐈𝐧𝐬𝐢𝐝𝐞 𝐚 𝐕𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐨𝐧-𝐋𝐚𝐧𝐠𝐮𝐚𝐠𝐞 𝐌𝐨𝐝𝐞𝐥

A VLM has three main components:
→ 𝐕𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐄𝐧𝐜𝐨𝐝𝐞𝐫 converts pixels into visual tokens.
→ 𝐅𝐮𝐬𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐋𝐚𝐲𝐞𝐫 combines visual and text context for reasoning.
→ 𝐋𝐚𝐧𝐠𝐮𝐚𝐠𝐞 𝐃𝐞𝐜𝐨𝐝𝐞𝐫 generates captions, summaries, or structured responses.

Each layer introduces potential failure modes like poor resolution, misaligned regions, or verbose hallucinations. Fine-tuning improves alignment and reliability across these components.

𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐟𝐢𝐧𝐞-𝐭𝐮𝐧𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐥𝐢𝐟𝐞𝐜𝐲𝐜𝐥𝐞

→ 𝐃𝐚𝐭𝐚 𝐝𝐞𝐬𝐢𝐠𝐧: Collect diverse, high-quality, clearly labeled visuals.
→ 𝐓𝐚𝐬𝐤 𝐝𝐞𝐟𝐢𝐧𝐢𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 : Choose the right setup: captioning, VQA, extraction, or localization.
→ 𝐋𝐨𝐑𝐀 𝐭𝐫𝐚𝐢𝐧𝐢𝐧𝐠: Train adapters for each domain efficiently.
→ 𝐄𝐯𝐚𝐥𝐮𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧: Use both quantitative metrics and human review for grounding and accuracy.

Always evaluate across different slices such as document type, lighting, and template to surface hidden biases.

𝐆𝐨𝐯𝐞𝐫𝐧𝐚𝐧𝐜𝐞 𝐚𝐧𝐝 𝐫𝐞𝐥𝐢𝐚𝐛𝐢𝐥𝐢𝐭𝐲

Each domain adapter should have its own dataset lineage, version, and evaluation score.
Reliability requires attention to fairness, privacy, consistency, and uncertainty handling.
Fine-tuning doesn’t just improve accuracy, it strengthens governance and ethical alignment.

LoRA fine-tuning makes VLMs faster to adapt, cheaper to deploy, and more aligned with your real-world data.

--- Post 238 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025

I’ve worked in data science for a decade, and I’ve seen the field evolve a lot. But nothing compares to what’s happened in the last three.

Generative AI has completely reshaped our workflows. What used to take weeks of manual data prep and iteration now happens in days or even hours. The role of a data scientist is shifting fast: less about repetitive coding, more about designing intelligent workflows that solve real business problems.

I recently came across Google's new Practical Guide to Data Science, and here are a few insights that stood out for me:

➝ The agentic shift
Most of a data scientist’s day used to be cleaning data, tuning models, and writing the same pipelines again and again. Now AI agents automate those parts. The value we bring is moving to analysis, interpretation, and driving business outcomes.

➝ Multimodal data
For years, our work was limited to structured tables. But most enterprise data is unstructured like images, PDFs, audio, and free text. With BigQuery, you can now analyze this directly with SQL. That means questions that used to be impossible, like combining sales data with call transcripts, are finally within reach.

➝ Blending external intelligence with enterprise data
Foundation models bring real-world knowledge into the enterprise stack. Instead of writing rules for every scenario, you can ask nuanced questions like: Which of our products show high satisfaction based on quality? This type of reasoning used to take months of manual analysis.

➝ AI as a feature engineering engine
Instead of just running basic sentiment analysis, you can extract structured insights at scale. For example, pulling out sentiment specifically around “battery life” or “user interface” and joining it with sales data. Raw text turns into powerful features that drive models.

➝ In-place model development
Moving data around used to be the bottleneck. With BigQuery ML, you can now train and deploy models right where the data lives. Teams have seen deployment times cut by 10x, shifting the focus from infrastructure to speed of insight.

➝ Vector embeddings and semantic search
Vector search used to mean adding another system. Now it’s built into BigQuery. That means semantic product discovery, document retrieval, and multimodal analysis all within your data warehouse.

Data scientists role is changing, and now it's less about syntax, more about strategy. Less about writing every line of code, more about designing AI-powered workflows.

If you want to dive deeper, I recommend checking out the full guide. It’s packed with practical examples that show just how much the landscape has shifted

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

If you're building AI systems, you’ve probably faced this dilemma:
How do you evaluate outputs at scale, without hiring an army of human annotators or spending months building custom evaluation pipelines?

That’s where LLM-as-a-Judge (LLMaJ) comes in.
Now, AI is not just generating content, it’s also grading it.

When should you use LLM-as-a-Judge?
1️⃣ Subjective Evaluation: If you're assessing tone, creativity, or style, LLMs can capture nuances that traditional metrics miss. BLEU or ROUGE won’t tell you if your customer support bot sounded empathetic.

2️⃣ Complex Context Reasoning: In RAG, you might ask:
 “Did the answer actually come from the retrieved context, or did it hallucinate?”
 LLM-as-a-Judge can do that cross-check automatically.

3️⃣ High-Scale Evaluation: If your system handles millions of responses a day, human evaluation becomes impractical. LLM-based evaluation can scale instantly.

When should you avoid LLM-as-a-Judge? ❌ 

❗Binary Ground Truth Exists: If you can compute accuracy directly (like in math problems), use that. No need for subjective judgment.

❗High-Stakes Decisions: Legal, compliance, and medical cases still require human oversight.

❗Deterministic, Reproducible Results Needed: If evaluations need to be 100% explainable and auditable, combine LLM judges with traditional metrics and human checks.

But LLM-as-a-Judge isn’t perfect.

⚠️ Even though it’s powerful, there are real trade-offs:

→ Biases: LLMs can show nepotism bias (favoring their own outputs), verbosity bias (preferring longer answers), positional bias (favoring whatever comes first), and beauty bias (rewarding eloquence over accuracy).

→ Cost and Latency: Large models like GPT-4 are expensive to run, especially when you’re evaluating at production scale. They also come with high latency, which makes real-time guardrails or agent monitoring impractical.

That’s why more teams are shifting toward Specialized Models that are cheaper, faster, and optimized for evaluation.

Galileo built Luna-2, a small language model (SLM) optimized for real-time, high-scale evaluations.

💡 Real-time guardrails: Luna-2 catches hallucinations, tool failures, and safety violations on the fly, before outputs reach users.

💡 Cost-effective evaluations: Running evaluations with LLMs like GPT-4 is expensive. Luna-2 costs an order of magnitude less, while keeping comparable accuracy.

💡Production-grade latency: Luna-2 delivers sub-200ms verdicts even when running 10–20 checks at once. Perfect for agentic workflows.

💡Custom metrics & fine-tuning
Luna-2 lets you define specific metrics, whether you’re monitoring PII leaks, bias, tool error rates, or action completions.

As AI scales, evaluation can’t be an afterthought. Whether it’s a chatbot, agent, or summarizer, you need real-time, reliable evaluation.

Sometimes that means humans.
Sometimes LLM-as-a-Judge.

Increasingly, it’s SLMs like Luna-2 that are built for production.

--- Post 240 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 212 reactions, 38 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 1,466)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7375207079139061760

When we talk about large language models “reasoning,” we’re often lumping together very different processes. In reality, there are two distinct types of reasoning that LLMs exhibit: implicit reasoning and explicit reasoning.

📣 Explicit reasoning
This is the step-by-step style many of us encourage with prompts like “think step by step.”
In the math problem below, the model writes out:
→ Step 1: Calculate total markers
→ Step 2: Subtract what was given away
→ Step 3: Subtract what was lost
→ Final answer = 48

Advantages:
→ Transparent and auditable
→ Better performance on complex, multi-step tasks

Limitations:
→ Slower and more verbose
→ Can be harder to scale for large volumes of queries

💠 Implicit reasoning
Here, the model “jumps” directly to the final answer without writing intermediate steps. The reasoning still happens, but it’s compressed in the model’s hidden states.

It’s like solving a problem in your head vs. explaining it out loud.

Advantages:
→ Fast and efficient
→ Feels more natural for tasks like translation or summarization

Limitations:
→ Opaque- you can’t inspect how the model got there
→ Harder to debug or align behavior

⚖️ Why this matters
→ Implicit reasoning is good when efficiency matters.
→ Explicit reasoning is essential when correctness, safety, or interpretability are non-negotiable.

The future of LLM systems will depend on balancing both: implicit shortcuts for fluid performance, and explicit reasoning when decisions must be explained and trusted.

📚 If you want to go deeper, I’d highly recommend, reading these two resources:
- https://lnkd.in/dPJUR7dn
- https://lnkd.in/dAD8AUjA

--- Post 242 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 230 reactions, 26 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,440)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7415474721074700288

GPTs or Generative Pre-trained Transformers really revolutionized what artificial general intelligence looks like in practice.

The foundation goes back to 2017, when the Transformer architecture was introduced in the Attention Is All You Need paper. Attention made large-scale language modeling possible.

The real technical inflection point was GPT-3.

GPT-3 showed that scaling transformers leads to emergent behavior. Few-shot learning, cross-task generalization, and prompting as an interface. This was when language models stopped feeling narrow.

But GPT-3 was not built for humans. It was powerful, yet brittle.

GPT-3.5 changed that through instruction tuning and RLHF. The model was trained to follow instructions, hold conversations, and align better with human intent.

OpenAI launched ChatGPT on top of GPT-3.5, an improved GPT-3-class model. That is when LLMs became usable and mainstream.

At a high level, GPT works like this:

✦ Text is tokenized and embedded
✦ Attention models context
✦ Transformer layers build understanding
✦ The model predicts the next token

If you are getting started in AI engineering, this mental model matters more than any single tool.

Understanding how we got from GPT-3 to ChatGPT explains why modern AI works the way it does.

--- Post 243 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 235 reactions, 23 comments, 14 reposts (Score: 1,433)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7387528524804542464

I’m sure you’ve heard that the AI industry has moved from obsessing over prompt engineering to now understanding context engineering.

In every LLM application, there are different types of context that shape how the model reasons and acts.

At the simplest level, context can be:
→ User context: what the user says or does
→ System context: what the application provides as background
→ Memory context: what the model or agent recalls from past interactions

When agents interleave LLM calls and tool calls, they don’t just alternate blindly, so they use feedback from tools to dynamically refine the next LLM call.

This creates a continuous reasoning loop: predict → act → observe → re-prompt. That’s where context engineering starts to become an art.

Broadly, we can think of context engineering as four key operations:
 → Write context: crafting inputs that guide model behavior
 → Select context: retrieving or filtering relevant information
 → Compress context: distilling large knowledge into limited tokens
 → Isolate context: keeping different reasoning threads independent

At the end of the day, context is what gives intelligence its continuity. Models generate responses, but context is what gives them understanding.

If you want to learn more about Context Engineering, join our 1.5 hour hands-on workshop on 8th November where Arvind and I will be covering both theory + demo: https://lnkd.in/dvFmMiRr

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
