I had a conversation with Ravi Evani, GVP and CTO of Engineering at Publicis Sapient, and it reminded me why real enterprise AI work rarely looks like the glossy demos we see online. It’s slow, it’s meticulous, and it forces you to respect the structure and context of the business before you ever touch a model.

✦ Ravi talked about agentic systems the way seasoned engineers do: start with meaning before mechanics. If your data doesn’t have shared semantics, if your workflows don’t have clear boundaries, no agent is going to behave reliably. Ontologies and context graphs aren’t academic artifacts, they’re what let AI operate in the real enterprise, not just in a polished demo. They’re the difference between “sounds right” and “is right.”

✦ The Lending Manager example made that real. Everyone loves the headline of cutting loan processing time in half. But the actual win came from decomposing the workflow into what should be coded, what should be modeled, and what genuinely benefits from generative reasoning. When you separate those layers correctly, the system becomes predictable in a domain that tolerates exactly zero surprises. That’s what delivery looks like, not experimentation.

✦ Ravi said something I wish more AI teams internalized: deterministic logic deserves code, probabilistic tasks deserve ML, and only the interpretive, repetitive moments deserve GenAI. That mental model protects you from the accidental chaos GenAI can introduce in regulated environments packed with PII and fairness constraints. It’s a reminder that enterprise AI isn’t about bigger models, it’s about the right architecture for the outcome you need

✦ And adoption is never magic. You start by removing the friction points that experts have quietly tolerated for decades. You earn trust by improving their day-to-day reality, not by promising transformation in a slide deck.

============================================================

--- Post 246 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 188 reactions, 44 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,402)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7399255260046954496

The narrative that Google “missed the AI boat” has officially expired.

For the last few years, NVIDIA has been the default answer to almost every AI compute question. Incredible chips, an unmatched CUDA ecosystem, and a head start that turned into a landslide.

But something interesting has been happening in the background. A quiet player is stepping into the spotlight and changing the economics of large scale AI workloads: Google’s TPUs.

Every Gemini model is trained entirely on TPUs. And that’s not an accident.

The real story here isn’t GPUs vs TPUs in some vague hardware war. It’s about use cases.

NVIDIA GPUs grew up in graphics, so they evolved into flexible, general purpose parallel engines. They shine in research labs, startups, production deployments, and pretty much anywhere you need versatility.

Google TPUs were built with a different intent. They’re ASICs, hardware engineered from day one for matrix multiplications, the heartbeat of modern neural networks. You trade generality for efficiency and for massive, sustained AI workloads, that tradeoff wins.

And this is where Google’s vertical integration becomes a serious force.

Google controls the whole stack: chip design, data center fabric, cooling, scheduling, compiler optimizations, and the cloud interface where developers access it. That means:

→ Training a frontier model like Gemini 3.0 becomes significantly more efficient
→ The performance per watt is hard to ignore when you’re running billion dollar training runs
→ Google Cloud customers get better cost per token because the company can pass those optimizations straight through the stack

Major AI labs are already leaning in. Not to replace GPUs, but to diversify. The era of single vendor dependency is ending.

This doesn’t erase NVIDIA’s dominance. CUDA is still the most mature ecosystem in the world, and GPUs are unbeatable for flexible workloads, rapid experimentation, and smaller scale deployments.

But for hyperscalers, cloud providers, and teams training frontier models, TPUs have become a real competitive alternative that changes the economics of large scale AI.

This next chapter is a multi vendor world. And it’s going to get interesting.

============================================================

--- Post 248 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 207 reactions, 31 comments, 25 reposts (Score: 1,395)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7382134264915488768

Agentic AI Design Patterns are emerging as the backbone of real-world, production-grade AI systems, and this is gold from Andrew Ng 

Most current LLM applications are linear: prompt → output.

But real-world autonomy demands more. It requires agents that can reflect, adapt, plan, and collaborate, over extended tasks and in dynamic environments.

That’s where the RTPM framework comes in. It's a design blueprint for building scalable agentic systems:

➡️ Reflection
➡️ Tool-Use
➡️ Planning
➡️ Multi-Agent Collaboration

Let’s unpack each one from a systems engineering perspective:

🔁 1. Reflection
This is the agent’s ability to perform self-evaluation after each action. It's not just post-hoc logging—it's part of the control loop.

Agents ask:
→ Was the subtask successful?
→ Did the tool/API return the expected structure or value?
→ Is the plan still valid given current memory state?

Techniques include:
→ Internal scoring functions
→ Critic models trained on trajectory outcomes
→ Reasoning chains that validate step outputs

Without reflection, agents remain brittle, but with it, they become self-correcting systems.

🛠 2. Tool-Use
 LLMs alone can’t interface with the world. Tool-use enables agents to execute code, perform retrieval, query databases, call APIs, and trigger external workflows.

Tool-use design involves:
→ Function calling or JSON schema execution (OpenAI, Fireworks AI, LangChain, etc.)
→ Grounding outputs into structured results (e.g., SQL, Python, REST)
→ Chaining results into subsequent reasoning steps

This is how you move from "text generators" to capability-driven agents.

📊 3. Planning
 Planning is the core of long-horizon task execution. 

Agents must:
→ Decompose high-level goals into atomic steps
→ Sequence tasks based on constraints and dependencies
→ Update plans reactively when intermediate states deviate

Design patterns here include:
→ Chain-of-thought with memory rehydration
→ Execution DAGs or LangGraph flows
→ Priority queues and re-entrant agents

Planning separates short-term LLM chains from persistent agentic workflows.

🤖 4. Multi-Agent Collaboration
 As task complexity grows, specialization becomes essential. Multi-agent systems allow modularity, separation of concerns, and distributed execution.

This involves:
→ Specialized agents: planner, retriever, executor, validator
→ Communication protocols: Model Context Protocol (MCP), A2A messaging
→ Shared context: via centralized memory, vector DBs, or message buses

This mirrors multi-threaded systems in software—except now the "threads" are intelligent and autonomous.

Agentic Design ≠ monolithic LLM chains.

It’s about constructing layered systems with runtime feedback, external execution, memory-aware planning, and collaborative autonomy.

Here is a deep-dive blog is you would like to learn more: https://lnkd.in/dKhi_n7M

============================================================

Planning separates short-term LLM chains from persistent agentic workflows.

🤖 4. Multi-Agent Collaboration
 As task complexity grows, specialization becomes essential. Multi-agent systems allow modularity, separation of concerns, and distributed execution.

This involves:
→ Specialized agents: planner, retriever, executor, validator
→ Communication protocols: Model Context Protocol (MCP), A2A messaging
→ Shared context: via centralized memory, vector DBs, or message buses

This mirrors multi-threaded systems in software—except now the "threads" are intelligent and autonomous.

Agentic Design ≠ monolithic LLM chains.

It’s about constructing layered systems with runtime feedback, external execution, memory-aware planning, and collaborative autonomy.

Here is a deep-dive blog is you would like to learn more: https://lnkd.in/dKhi_n7M

============================================================

I wrote What’s Your Worth because I kept seeing the same pattern over and over again.

Smart, capable people doing great work… and still feeling stuck. Still waiting for the next role, the next promotion, the next “tap on the shoulder” to move their career forward.

The reality is, careers don’t work the way they used to.

Your 9–5 alone is no longer enough to open doors. Not because your work isn’t good, but because discovery has changed. People form opinions about you long before they meet you. They Google you. They LinkedIn you. They look for how you think, not just where you’ve worked.

That’s what personal branding actually is.
Not aesthetics. Not being an influencer.
It’s having control over your career narrative instead of leaving it to algorithms, assumptions, or chance.

When you build visibility around what you’re good at, what you care about, and the problems you know how to solve, something shifts. Your career becomes portable. Opportunities come to you. You stop relying on a single company or title to define your worth.

I didn’t set out to build a personal brand. I was just sharing what I was learning and building. Over time, it opened rooms and conversations that my 9–5 alone never could.

That’s why I put together a detailed LinkedIn Growth Playbook, to help professionals build real career leverage through visibility.

If you’re thinking long-term about your career and want more control over where it goes, you might find it useful: https://lnkd.in/ePQg3K5K

AI won't replace your job.
But someone who uses AI better than you? They might.
I get asked this question every single week: "Aish, is my role safe?"

Yes, the velocity of AI advancement is faster than any other technology. You have 2 choices- either keep complaining or adapt!

I built a 5-question self-assessment to help you understand where you stand with the "Will my job be replace with an AI tool?"

→ Is your work mostly repeatable patterns?
→ Do you solve ambiguous problems?
→ Does your role need deep domain knowledge?
→ Do you coordinate across people and systems?
→ Can your output be graded by a machine?

Each question has a clear "If YES" and "If NO" path, with specific moves to future-proof yourself right now.

Here's what I've seen working almost 10 years in AI: The people who get replaced aren't the ones with fewer skills. They're the ones who stayed in execution mode while the job shifted underneath them.

--- Post 253 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 217 reactions, 25 comments, 21 reposts (Score: 1,377)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7410005541848231936

GenAI Observability is not optional anymore. It is production hygiene.

Once you move past demos, the problem is no longer “did the model respond?”
It is “why did it respond that way, and will it keep doing that in production?”

A simple way to think about it:
✦ Observability tells you what is happening inside your GenAI system
Prompts, retrieval, model calls, tool usage, outputs.

✦ Monitoring tells you how the system behaves over time
Speed, cost, quality, safety, reliability.

If you are shipping GenAI, there are a few signals that matter more than everything else:
- Latency: time to first token and total response time
- Cost: spend per 1K tokens and per feature
- Quality: faithfulness, groundedness, relevance
- RAG health: are the right docs being retrieved
- Safety: jailbreaks, PII, toxic outputs
- Reliability: errors, tool failures, retries

Why this matters:
- GenAI systems rarely fail loudly.
- They hallucinate quietly, retrieve the wrong context, slow down, or get expensive over time.

Some practical tips:
✦ Log prompts and outputs from day one
✦ Track cost and latency before optimizing prompts
✦ Evaluate RAG explicitly, not by vibes
✦ Review traces regularly, not only when something breaks

GenAI is not a black box API. It is a probabilistic system that needs visibility to earn trust.

〰️〰️〰️

Agentic AI is forcing enterprises to rethink how their systems are built. The challenge is not about running a single model. It is about coordinating multiple policies, data sources, tools, and evaluation loops across hybrid cloud environments. 

You need observability, governance, workload optimization, and an execution layer that can support agents acting across many steps.
IBM has partnered with Amazon Web Services (AWS) to solve exactly this. The goal is simple. Make agentic AI operational at enterprise scale.

Here is the part I find exciting from a systems perspective:
– watsonx.governance integrates directly into AWS services like SageMaker so guardrails run inside the workflow and not as an external check.

– Instana gives real time visibility into agent chains so you can track latency, drift, failures, and errors across hybrid environments.

– Turbonomic continuously optimizes compute and cost profiles so agent pipelines stay performant even as workloads shift.

– Hybrid cloud support makes it possible for agents to act where the data and applications already live, not where the model happens to sit.

This is the architecture shift the industry is making. Less focus on a single LLM. More on the “system” that keeps autonomous behavior reliable, compliant, and measurable.

IBM and AWS will be showcasing this direction at re:Invent. If you want to understand how agentic systems actually run in production, their booth will be one of the most useful stops.

On a personal note, this moment feels full circle. I started my AI journey at IBM, and collaborating again with IBM on something so foundational to the future of enterprise AI means a lot.

If you are attending re:Invent, stop by the IBM booth to see these systems in action.

Learn more: https://lnkd.in/d4fF7U9H

If you want to upskill in AI engineering, this is a topic you cannot skip.

✦ When fine-tuning makes sense and when it does not

✦ An end-to-end, code-based demo from raw data to a specialized model

"Prompt Engineer" as a job title is quietly disappearing from job boards.

But the actual skill has never been more valuable. We just call it something different now.

In 2023, prompt engineering meant tweaking words in a chatbox. 

In 2026, it means designing the entire context a model uses to make a decision- system instructions, retrieved documents, memory state, tool outputs, all wired together in code.

The industry started calling this Context Engineering, and honestly the name fits better.

The standalone title is fading in product teams, but the skill is thriving in QA, model safety, red-teaming, and agentic system design. It didn't die, it got absorbed into more specialized, higher-impact work.

If you're building an AI career right now, the move isn't perfecting a single prompt. It's learning how to design robust systems around how models actually think.

Full video here: https://lnkd.in/g4D9KSpP

Your personal brand turns your career from being chosen by chance into being chosen on purpose.

1 week to go! 
Arvind and I are super excited to be hosting an AI evals hands-on workshop on February 15.

It’s a 90-minute session where we’ll cover the fundamentals of AI evaluations and then actually do it together. Not theory only. You’ll walk away having built and reasoned through real evals yourself. 

If you’re aspiring to be an AI engineer or planning to upskill for AI roles in 2026, understanding how to evaluate LLM applications is critical.

Building an LLM app is often the easy part. Knowing whether it truly works in production is where most teams struggle.

LLM evaluation is not just about correctness anymore. You are judging usefulness, safety, faithfulness to sources, consistency across runs, cost, latency, and real user outcomes across prompts, RAG pipelines, tools, and agentic workflows.

Because this is hard, many teams fall back to vibe-evals. If a few outputs look good, they ship. That approach breaks quickly when models change, prompts regress, retrieval fails, or user behavior shifts. Without evals, problems surface only after users complain.

Why learning evals matters:
✦ Evals turn LLM development from guesswork into engineering
✦ They help catch regressions as models, prompts, or tools evolve
✦ They are essential for scaling RAG systems and agentic workflows
✦ In 2026, strong teams use hybrid evals combining automated metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, and targeted human review

Evals are no longer optional. They are a core AI skill.

In this workshop, Arvind and I will walk through how AI evals are done in real systems, with hands-on exercises so you build intuition.

This is designed for AI engineers and tech leads building systems, and even business leaders who want intuition for what “good” really means in production. Even if you are a beginner, don't worry, we will give you the foundations for you to understand everything comprehensively, and enough resources that you can review at your own pace.

--- Post 273 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 183 reactions, 35 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 1,291)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7415107772914089984

Embedding models are one of those things in AI that don’t get talked about enough, even though they sit underneath almost everything we build.

They’re not new. We’ve been using embeddings long before LLMs showed up. In traditional ML systems, embeddings were how we represented things like users, products, words, or images as numbers so we could compare them, rank them, and make decisions at scale.

What’s changed is how central they’ve become. At a very basic level, embedding models turn unstructured data into vectors that capture meaning. Things that are similar end up closer together. That’s it. Simple idea, massive impact.

In the LLM world, this becomes even more important. Models don’t magically know your data. They don’t remember your docs, your codebase, or your company’s knowledge. Embeddings are how we bridge that gap. They’re what make retrieval systems work, whether you call it RAG, search, or context injection.

If you’re an AI engineer, this is not a “nice to know” concept. Embeddings show up in semantic search, recommendations, personalization, evaluation, and most production-grade AI systems. You can get surprisingly far without thinking about them deeply, but you’ll hit a wall the moment you try to scale or make things reliable.

--- Post 274 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 154 reactions, 48 comments, 12 reposts (Score: 1,274)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7417062643816882176

If you are building AI agents or learning about them, then you should keep these best practices in mind 👇 

Building agentic systems isn’t just about chaining prompts anymore, it’s about designing robust, interpretable, and production-grade systems that interact with tools, humans, and other agents in complex environments.

Here are 10 essential design principles you need to know:

➡️ Modular Architectures
Separate planning, reasoning, perception, and actuation. This makes your agents more interpretable and easier to debug. Think planner-executor separation in LangGraph or CogAgent-style designs.

➡️ Tool-Use APIs via MCP or Open Function Calling
Adopt the Model Context Protocol (MCP) or OpenAI’s Function Calling to interface safely with external tools. These standard interfaces provide strong typing, parameter validation, and consistent execution behavior.

➡️ Long-Term & Working Memory
Memory is non-optional for non-trivial agents. Use hybrid memory stacks, vector search tools like MemGPT or Marqo for retrieval, combined with structured memory systems like LlamaIndex agents for factual consistency.

➡️ Reflection & Self-Critique Loops
Implement agent self-evaluation using ReAct, Reflexion, or emerging techniques like Voyager-style curriculum refinement. Reflection improves reasoning and helps correct hallucinated chains of thought.

➡️ Planning with Hierarchies
Use hierarchical planning: a high-level planner for task decomposition and a low-level executor to interact with tools. This improves reusability and modularity, especially in multi-step or multi-modal workflows.

➡️ Multi-Agent Collaboration
Use protocols like AutoGen, A2A, or ChatDev to support agent-to-agent negotiation, subtask allocation, and cooperative planning. This is foundational for open-ended workflows and enterprise-scale orchestration.

➡️ Simulation + Eval Harnesses
Always test in simulation. Use benchmarks like ToolBench, SWE-agent, or AgentBoard to validate agent performance before production. This minimizes surprises and surfaces regressions early.

➡️ Safety & Alignment Layers
Don’t ship agents without guardrails. Use tools like Llama Guard v4, Prompt Shield, and role-based access controls. Add structured rate-limiting to prevent overuse or sensitive tool invocation.

➡️ Cost-Aware Agent Execution
Implement token budgeting, step count tracking, and execution metrics. Especially in multi-agent settings, costs can grow exponentially if unbounded.

➡️ Human-in-the-Loop Orchestration
Always have an escalation path. Add override triggers, fallback LLMs, or route to human-in-the-loop for edge cases and critical decision points. This protects quality and trust.

If you found this insightful, share this with your network ♻️

Follow me (Aishwarya Srinivasan) for more AI insights and educational content.

--- Post 275 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 205 reactions, 21 comments, 16 reposts (Score: 1,267)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7403230273670144000

A lot of people ask me, what’s the roadmap to become an AI engineer?

It’s not just about finishing a few ML courses, it’s about mastering the fundamentals, applying them in real projects, and building the skills to ship AI systems into production.

Here’s a step-by-step path that works in 2025.

📓Step 0 – Build a Strong Technical Foundation
→ Learn Python deeply (AI’s primary language) plus working knowledge of C++, Java, or R.
→ Master data structures, algorithms, and software engineering principles.
→ Strengthen math for AI: linear algebra, calculus, probability, and statistics.

📌 Tip: Code along with tutorials and build small utilities like dataset parsers, algorithm implementations, or math simulations.


📗Step 1 – Learn ML & AI Fundamentals
→ Understand core ML concepts: supervised vs. unsupervised learning, regression, classification, clustering.
→ Explore deep learning: CNNs, RNNs, Transformers, NLP, generative AI.
→ Work hands-on with PyTorch, TensorFlow, and scikit-learn.

📌 Tip: Build a neural network from scratch using only NumPy to really understand how backpropagation works.


📘Step 2 – Apply Knowledge Through Projects
→ Start with small, scoped projects: image classification, NLP chatbots, recommendation engines.
→ Use public datasets from UCI, Kaggle, or Hugging Face Datasets.
→ Contribute to open-source AI projects or Kaggle competitions.

📌 Starter Ideas:
→ Sentiment analysis with real-time inference
→ Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) PDF summarizer
→ Price prediction model with explainable AI dashboards


📕Step 3 – Learn Data Engineering, MLOps & Deployment
→ Practice data preprocessing and visualization with pandas, NumPy, matplotlib.
→ Learn to deploy models on AWS Sagemaker, GCP Vertex AI, or Azure ML.
→ Master MLOps: CI/CD pipelines, retraining workflows, monitoring, model version control.
📌 Tip: Build an end-to-end pipeline - from raw data ingestion to a deployed model API - using Docker + FastAPI.


📙 Step 4 – Specialize in an AI Domain
→ Choose a focus: Computer Vision, NLP, Generative AI, Reinforcement Learning, or Robotics.
→ Read research papers, replicate results, and keep up with top AI conferences (NeurIPS, ICML).
📌 Tip: Maintain a “replication repo” where you implement recent papers in your chosen field.


📔Step 5 – Build a Portfolio & Network
→ Showcase your projects on GitHub with clear READMEs and deployment instructions.
→ Write technical blogs, create short videos, or speak at meetups about your projects.
→ Join AI communities (Slack, Discord, LinkedIn), attend hackathons, and network with peers.

If you’re starting today, pick one problem you care about, find a dataset, and commit to shipping a working MVP in 3 weeks. That’s how you start building momentum ❤️

〰️〰️〰️

They fail because no one measured them properly.

We’re in a phase where everyone is building with LLMs, agents, and RAG pipelines. But very few people can clearly answer:

How are you evaluating this?
What does “good” actually mean?
What happens when this hits production?

That gap is real. And companies are feeling it.

If you look at roles across OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, Microsoft, and serious AI startups, evaluation, benchmarking, red-teaming, and model quality are now embedded directly into AI engineering and applied AI roles. 

Even when the title doesn’t say “Evals,” the responsibility is there.

- AI engineers need to measure retrieval quality and hallucinations.
- Product leaders need guardrails before shipping AI features.
- AI PMs need to understand tradeoffs between quality, latency, and cost.

Evals are no longer optional. They’re foundational.

--- Post 279 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 209 reactions, 18 comments, 10 reposts (Score: 1,245)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7409035152481464320

Absolutely enjoyed my conversation with Shireesh Thota (CVP-Databases, Microsoft) on how databases are evolving in the age of AI.

A few takeaways from the discussion that are especially useful to internalize early in your career:

✦ Vector databases are not disappearing. They are becoming infrastructure. What looks like a “rise and fall” is actually consolidation. Vector search is settling into core databases as a native index, alongside transactions and consistency, rather than living as a separate system.

✦ RAG is what makes LLMs usable in real enterprise systems. Most valuable data cannot be used to train models. RAG turns databases into active context providers, letting AI reason over private, structured, and governed data.

✦ Relational and non-relational models still do most of the heavy lifting. Despite new modalities, these two paradigms continue to solve the vast majority of production use cases. Extensibility is winning over constantly introducing new engines.

✦ Operational and analytical workloads obey different physics. Millisecond-scale operational systems and batch analytics require fundamentally different optimizations. This distinction is why database engines and architectures still matter deeply.

✦ AI is changing how engineers work, not why they are needed. The constraint is not lack of work, but the ability to execute faster and reason at higher levels.
This was a very grounded discussion on where the database and AI stack is actually headed.

📹 Full conversation is up on YouTube: https://lnkd.in/e_Nq6722

Some weeks, content creation feels like a second full-time job.

Between scripting, filming, meetings, and travel, the overhead tasks quietly stack up. Headshots. Thumbnails. Clean visuals. B-roll. All of it matters, but all of it eats time. And time is usually the one thing I’m short on.

At some point, I had to get brutally honest about what actually requires me and what doesn’t. That’s when I started automating every creative task that wasn’t in my zone of genius.

That’s also how Freepik slipped into my workflow and never left.
It feels like having a design studio permanently open on my desktop. I can upload a simple reference photo and instantly generate studio-quality headshots in whatever style I need. Clean portraits when I’m filming a course. A whole different aesthetic when I’m building something seasonal. One prompt, new vibe.

What surprised me is how far I can push each asset.
✦ Retouch little details without opening Photoshop.
✦ Expand a frame so there’s room for text on thumbnails.
✦ Upscale everything so it actually looks polished on course pages.
✦ And even turn the same still images into super realistic B-roll clips.

It genuinely feels like a tiny creative team running in the background while I focus on the actual work.

Because everything is AI powered and in one place, I’m not juggling tools or losing flow. I get what I need quickly, and I can pour more energy into the parts of content creation that make my work mine.

Here’s the heart of it. When you automate the parts that drain your time, you create more space for the parts that make your work special.

Freepik lets me do that every day. And if you want to see how far generative AI has come for images and video, it’s worth testing it out yourself.


hashtag
#freepikPartner

============================================================

--- Post 284 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 180 reactions, 32 comments, 7 reposts (Score: 1,234)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7426078570348810240

Here’s my honest pattern with almost every AI tool I try.

I end up using a lot of tools, usually early access. After repeating this cycle enough times, I realized my reactions are almost predictable.

👉First hour
There’s real excitement.
I’m clicking around, testing odd prompts, trying things that used to fail. No strategy yet, just my curiosity and momentum.

👉Next few hours
I start comparing.
Where would this actually fit in my work?
What would I stop using if I kept this?
Is this clearly better, or just new?

👉Later that day
I experiment without guidance.
I skip the docs and examples on purpose. I try incomplete ideas, messy workflows, prompts I would never put in a demo. This tells me more than any tutorial ever could.

👉Day two
Then I look at the examples the team shared.
They usually work smoothly. They’re polished. They show the tool at its best, which is exactly what they’re meant to do.

👉After a few days
This is where the real understanding kicks in.
I start tweaking those examples. Changing assumptions. Using it in ways that feel realistic, not ideal. That’s when the boundaries show up. Not frustration. Just clarity.

Here’s the takeaway I’ve learned the hard way:
If you haven’t noticed limitations yet, you probably haven’t pushed the tool very far.

And honestly, if I use something consistently for three days and still can’t find a meaningful flaw, that’s rare. That earns real respect.
I haven’t experienced that yet.

Curious how others see this.
Are there tools you’ve used deeply where the limits still haven’t shown up?

============================================================

--- Post 286 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 195 reactions, 22 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,217)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7434646138416242688

For a long time, many companies built AI systems around a simple idea: choose the most powerful large language model available and use it across the entire workflow.

One large model handling classification, summarization, routing, reasoning, and generation.

What I am seeing now, especially going into 2026, is a clear architectural shift.

Teams are moving away from the “one giant model does everything” approach. Instead, they are decomposing workflows and assigning different models to different layers of the system. Smaller, more specialized models are being used for well-defined tasks, while larger models are reserved for complex reasoning where their breadth actually matters.

For those who are newer to this space, a SLMs typically refers to a model in the 1B to 12B parameter range. These models are optimized for efficiency, lower latency, and narrower domains. They are not designed to replace frontier-scale models, but to handle specific tasks extremely well.

There are two practical reasons why I believe 2026 will be a high-adoption year for SLMs:

✦ Cheaper, faster, and more customizable
For tasks like classification, structured extraction, lightweight reasoning, or domain-specific summarization, a smaller model is often more than sufficient. It runs with lower latency, costs less to scale, and if it is open source, it can be fine-tuned and adapted to your internal data and workflows. That level of customization gives teams real control over performance and differentiation.

✦ On-device and edge intelligence
As more AI moves closer to the user, on-device and edge inference become critical. Mobile assistants, IoT systems, and privacy-sensitive enterprise applications cannot always rely on sending every request to a large cloud model. Small models make local inference feasible, improving both responsiveness and privacy.

Large models are still essential for open-ended reasoning and complex generation. But the most mature systems will not rely on a single model.

They will be orchestrated systems, where each model is chosen based on what it is best at.

Model size is no longer the strategy, architecture is.

============================================================

--- Post 287 ---

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7416163181670428672

A re-ranking algorithm is what differentiates a basic RAG setup from a production-grade RAG system.

When you step back and look at RAG from an engineering lens, it is not a single model. It is a pipeline, and each stage solves a different problem.

✦ Retrieval
This is where embedding models are used. The user query is converted into a vector and compared against document vectors in a database. The system retrieves the top-K chunks that are closest in vector space. This step is optimized for speed and coverage. It answers a broad question: what information is likely related to this query?

✦ Augmentation
The retrieved chunks are prepared for the prompt. This is where you decide what context is included, how it is structured, and how much of it the model will see.

✦ Generation
The language model generates an answer using the augmented context.

If you rely only on embeddings for retrieval, you will often get results that are topically related but not strictly relevant to the user’s question. This is not a bug in embeddings. It is a design tradeoff.

Re-ranking addresses this gap.

A re-ranking model takes the top-K retrieved chunks and scores them again, conditioning on the full query and the full text of each chunk. Instead of measuring vector similarity, it evaluates relevance directly.

Consider a simple example.

The query is:
“How does the refund policy differ for monthly versus annual plans?”

Embedding-based retrieval may return:
- A general refund policy document
- A pricing page that mentions annual plans
- A billing FAQ that references refunds
- Subscription terms with partial overlap

All of these are semantically close, so they surface.

With a re-ranking step, the system reorders these results and prioritizes the chunks that explicitly compare monthly and annual refunds. The generic documents move down. The most relevant context moves up.

Nothing about the data changed.
What changed was how relevance was evaluated.

From a RAG system perspective, this improves retrieval precision, reduces noisy context, and leads to more reliable generation. It is one of the highest-leverage improvements you can make without changing the underlying LLM.

This is why re-ranking should be thought of as part of retrieval itself, not an optional add-on. In practice, it is the layer that turns a RAG pipeline into something you can trust in production. 

If you want to get started with using embedding & re-ranking with open-source models, do check out Fireworks AI: https://lnkd.in/ez38FwZC

============================================================

--- Post 291 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7408909395088076802

If you are getting started in AI engineering or trying to level up, eval engineering is a concept you need to understand early.

Traditional AI evals break down because most teams rely on LLM-as-a-judge. Accuracy plateaus around 60–70%, costs scale fast, and teams end up evaluating only a small slice of production traffic. That means real failures slip through.

Eval engineering treats evaluation as a system, not a metric.
✦ Start with LLM-as-a-judge to establish a baseline
✦ Involve subject matter experts to surface real failure modes
✦ Refine prompts and criteria using production data
✦ Train a small, fast judge model
✦ Deploy it for low-cost, low-latency evals across 100% of traffic
✦ Continuously feed failures back into the loop

The goal is simple: move from sampled guesses to full observability, and from “it looks fine” to “we trust this in production.”

Image inspiration: Galileo

✦✦✦

--- Post 293 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 153 reactions, 39 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 1,173)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7394547063272681472

If you’re trying to understand how AI agents actually think, start with the two core paradigms that power most agentic systems today: ReAct and ReSum.

🤖 ReAct (Reason + Act) is the classic loop.
The agent receives a query → thinks → performs an action (like calling a tool or API) → observes the result → thinks again → takes the next action. 
Every thought, observation, and tool response gets appended to the full context. For short, well-bounded tasks, this is elegant and efficient. The chain of reasoning stays transparent, and the agent has the entire history available as it decides the next step.

⚠️ But ReAct has a hard limit: context size.
Each step increases the token load. When you add multiple tool calls, long search results, multi-turn exploration, eventually the context window maxes out. This means the agent cannot continue reasoning, even if the task isn’t done. ReAct is great for small tasks, but it breaks when the workflow is long, multi-hop, or uncertain.

🐙 This is where ReSum optimizes things
Instead of carrying the entire raw history forward, the agent periodically invokes a summarization tool. This tool condenses the conversation and tool outputs into a compact, structured state. Only the essential information, constraints, and remaining gaps are kept. The agent then continues reasoning from this compressed snapshot.

This has two huge benefits:
- The agent no longer hits the context limit during deep exploration.
- The reasoning stays grounded, not diluted by accumulated noise.

ReSum effectively creates “checkpoints” of the agent’s understanding, enabling it to explore indefinitely without losing track of the problem.

⁉️ When should you use which

ReAct is ideal for:
- short tasks
- deterministic flows
- simple tool interactions
- cases where transparency of each reasoning step matters

ReSum is ideal for:
- long-running tasks
- multi-hop research
- workflows with many tool calls
- scenarios where the problem isn’t known upfront and the agent must explore

If you’re building or evaluating AI agents, mastering these two paradigms is essential. They define how far your agent can think, how deeply it can explore, and how reliably it reaches the final answer.


Happy Building 🚀

The future of data centers isn’t on Earth, it’s in orbit!
As AI workloads scale exponentially, our planet is running out of room and resources, to keep up. Power, cooling, and sustainability have become existential bottlenecks for compute.

That’s what makes Starcloud’s partnership with NVIDIA such a breakthrough. Their upcoming Starcloud-1 launch will be the first time an NVIDIA H100 GPU, a true data-center-class processor, operates in space.

By using the vacuum of space as a natural heat sink and tapping into unlimited solar energy, Starcloud aims to run large-scale AI compute with 10x lower energy costs and zero water usage for cooling.

The implications go far beyond energy efficiency. This is a glimpse into how sustainable, high-performance computing could evolve over the next decade.
Starcloud’s work reminds us that the next frontier for AI isn’t just smarter models, it’s smarter infrastructure. It is rethinking where and how we run intelligence itself.

I am proud to back the Starcloud team early on, and seeing their vision come to life in partnership with NVIDIA is nothing short of inspiring.

Keep at it Philip & team!!

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 176 reactions, 26 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,170)

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7425953133828157440

Deep research AI agents are quietly becoming one of the most important workloads in modern AI systems.

When Manus introduced the Manus agent, it caught a lot of attention for a reason. It showed how far agentic systems could go when planning, tool calling, retrieval, and iteration are treated as first-class design problems, not afterthoughts. 

The efficiency wasn’t just about speed. It was about structure. Tool calls are expensive, if you don't build the context well- it explodes, and if the chunking and retrieval is not right- the model produces garbage.

Since then, almost every serious AI lab is pushing hard on this problem. OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta (they acquired Manus), GenSpark, and others working on browser automation, long-horizon tasks, and reliable research workflows. 

Different implementations, same core goal: build agents that can reason over time, use tools well, and produce grounded outputs instead of shallow summaries.

If you’re getting started in AI engineering, this architecture is worth studying early. Deep research agents force you to think about system design, context management, retrieval strategies, and failure modes. These are skills that transfer far beyond a single model or framework.

Some resources I’d strongly recommend:
✦ The Context Engineering blog by Manus AI. It’s one of the clearest explanations of how these agents are actually built in practice.

✦ ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models
https://lnkd.in/gwpspnxr

✦ The paper “Deep Research Agents: A Systematic Examination and Roadmap” https://lnkd.in/gHV-GUBc

PS: I have been writing a lot of deep-dive blogs on Substack, and I will be writing one on Deep Research Agents. Make sure you're subscribed to my newsletter: https://lnkd.in/dpBNr6Jg

--- Post 298 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 180 reactions, 25 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 1,162)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7420920715799068672

Evaluating AI agents is very different from evaluating single-turn LLMs.
Agents act over many steps. They call tools, change state, and adapt as they go. Because of this, many failures don’t show up in the first response. They emerge only after several turns. That’s why prompt-level benchmarks often give a false sense of progress.

What Anthropic highlights well is a mindset shift:
✦ Evals are not ad-hoc experiments, they are engineering infrastructure
✦ The goal is to measure final outcomes, not just intermediate reasoning
✦ Reliability matters more than occasional success
✦ Consistency across multiple runs is a stronger signal than one “good” trajectory

Good agent evals look more like a system: clearly defined tasks, repeated trials, full transcripts, and a mix of code-based, model-based, and human graders.
The big takeaway for me: evals are how you move from reactive debugging to intentional iteration. Without them, it’s very hard to know whether an agent is actually improving or just getting lucky.

If you’re building agents for real use cases, this is worth studying carefully.
Arvind Narayanamurthy and I are hosting an upcoming workshop (15th Feb) on AI evals where we go deep into how this is done in real systems. If you want to level up your AI skills for 2026, do register for it: https://lnkd.in/dQSR7yhU

--- Post 301 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 168 reactions, 27 comments, 16 reposts (Score: 1,142)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7363374843347558400

Most ML projects don’t fail because the model is bad. They fail because the system around the model is broken.

I’ve seen teams build incredible architectures, but if you can’t reproduce a training run, explain why a model changed, or safely roll back a deployment, it’s not a product, it’s a science experiment. That’s the gap MLOps is meant to close.

𝗟𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗹 𝟬 – 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝘂𝗮𝗹 & 𝗙𝗿𝗮𝗴𝗶𝗹𝗲
This is where many teams still live:
 → Training in notebooks with no reproducibility
 → Copy-paste deployments
 → No versioning of data, code, or artifacts
 → No real observability or rollback path
At this stage, no one really trusts the pipeline.

𝗟𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗹 𝟭 – 𝗔𝘂𝘁𝗼𝗺𝗮𝘁𝗲𝗱 & 𝗥𝗲𝗽𝗲𝗮𝘁𝗮𝗯𝗹𝗲
Now you’re treating ML like engineering:
 → Pipelines are orchestrated and triggered by CI/CD
 → Datasets, models, and configs are versioned and logged
 → Deployments are reproducible, traceable, and monitored
This isn’t about chasing the newest tool. It’s about building trust.
You know exactly which data and code produced which model. You can roll back. You can iterate safely.

My 2 cents 🫰
→ ML projects rarely die because the model didn’t work.
→ They die because no one could explain what changed between the last good version and the one that broke.
→ MLOps isn’t overhead, it’s the only way to scale reliably.
→ Start small. Build systematically. Treat the pipeline as a product.
If you’re building for reliability instead of just performance, you’re already ahead.

Workflow inspired by: Google Cloud

Read full blog on my Substack: https://lnkd.in/dFjKK5Fy

I sat down with Natalie Han, PhD, the VP and CPO of Gen AI at SAP, and the conversation stayed with me long after we wrapped.

SAP operates at a scale where every AI decision must balance two forces that usually collide: the speed of innovation and the stability that enterprise systems demand. And Natalie broke down how they navigate that tension with remarkable clarity.

✦ Enterprise AI isn’t the same game as consumer AI. SAP optimizes for responsible, reliable, and relevant AI because their customers run mission-critical workloads.

✦ Their foundation layer, AI Core, powers a multi-LLM strategy so customers can choose the model that fits their regional, privacy, or compliance needs. Flexibility at scale is a different engineering challenge altogether.

✦ Privacy and security aren’t afterthoughts. They’re constraints that shape the architecture from day one.

✦ Evaluation isn’t a one-off benchmark. SAP continuously uses internal datasets to guide what gets rolled out to customers and how quickly.
Natalie captured SAP’s philosophy perfectly: they look at innovation through a velocity lens, but with minimal volatility. Stability isn’t a constraint. It’s the reason enterprises can trust AI for massive, high-stakes workloads.

I sat down with Natalie Han, PhD, the VP and CPO of Gen AI at SAP, and the conversation stayed with me long after we wrapped.

SAP operates at a scale where every AI decision must balance two forces that usually collide: the speed of innovation and the stability that enterprise systems demand. And Natalie broke down how they navigate that tension with remarkable clarity.

✦ Enterprise AI isn’t the same game as consumer AI. SAP optimizes for responsible, reliable, and relevant AI because their customers run mission-critical workloads.

✦ Their foundation layer, AI Core, powers a multi-LLM strategy so customers can choose the model that fits their regional, privacy, or compliance needs. Flexibility at scale is a different engineering challenge altogether.

✦ Privacy and security aren’t afterthoughts. They’re constraints that shape the architecture from day one.

✦ Evaluation isn’t a one-off benchmark. SAP continuously uses internal datasets to guide what gets rolled out to customers and how quickly.
Natalie captured SAP’s philosophy perfectly: they look at innovation through a velocity lens, but with minimal volatility. Stability isn’t a constraint. It’s the reason enterprises can trust AI for massive, high-stakes workloads.

I get this question all the time from aspiring AI engineers.

“How do I build a portfolio that actually stands out if I don’t have internship experience?”

Here’s the honest answer.

A basic chatbot project or a weekend tutorial clone is not going to move the needle anymore. The bar is higher now. Most hiring managers are trying to figure out one thing: can you think like someone who has built real systems?

When I review portfolios, I’m not looking for flashy demos. I’m looking for signals. Do you understand how data flows through a system? Do you think about evaluation? Latency? Edge cases? Failure modes? Have you made architectural decisions and documented why?

That’s why I put together five portfolio project ideas that go much deeper than “build an app with an LLM API.”

These projects push you to:

✦ Design production-level systems, not just scripts
✦ Integrate generative models thoughtfully, not superficially
✦ Build agentic workflows that actually solve a problem end to end

None of these are half-day projects. They should stretch you. They should force you to read docs, debug weird issues, rethink your architecture, and document everything clearly. That process is the real signal.

If you’re serious about breaking into AI engineering this year, your portfolio needs to reflect how you think, not just what you can copy.

I’ve broken all five down in detail here: https://lnkd.in/gM_ymc8H

--- Post 326 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 161 reactions, 27 comments, 5 reposts (Score: 1,085)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7432994872841900032

A lot of my early growth in data science came from one thing: statistical learning.

Before I spent time on ML systems and pipelines, I was learning how to think clearly about data, distributions, and what conclusions you can and cannot draw from what you observe. That foundation shaped how I approach almost everything I do in this field.

If you’re a student, or you’re working toward a data science role, here’s a quick mental model that will save you a lot of confusion later. It’s also one of those basics that shows up constantly in real work, especially when you’re cleaning data, building features, or designing a pipeline.

Broadly, data falls into two buckets:

✦ Qualitative (categorical): non numerical information like labels, words, images, or audio
✦ Nominal: categories with no natural order (examples: country, product type)
✦ Ordinal: categories with a clear order (examples: education level, satisfaction rating)

✦ Quantitative (numerical): measurable values that you can count or compute on
✦ Discrete: counted in whole numbers (examples: number of users, tickets, purchases)
✦ Continuous: values across a range (examples: time, height, temperature)

This matters more than people think.

It affects how you store data, how you encode it, what transformations make sense, what models behave well, and even what “average” means in a given column. 

If you’re a data engineer, this shows up in schema decisions and how you prepare data for downstream consumers.

If you’re building ML, it shows up in feature engineering and evaluation.

============================================================

Today, I got to attend Fei-Fei Li's keynote at Ai4 Conference and I’m still processing how powerful it was.

She’s one of the reasons I’m even in this field. I remember watching her TED talk back in 2015, it wasn’t just inspiring, it shaped the way I saw AI, and somewhere prepared me for the level of conviction you need to have as a women in the AI space. 

And today, almost a decade later, hearing her speak in person felt like a full-circle moment.

🧠She opened with a reflection that stuck with me: "Socrates, in many ways, was the first prompter". Not of machines, but of minds. He taught us to ask better questions- and that, at its core, is what AI should be helping us do.

👩‍🏫 Fei-Fei spoke about AI in education as one of the most underrated opportunities of our time. Not enough people are talking about it.

She challenged the fear-based mindset that sees AI as cheating in the classroom. Instead, she urged us to reframe it- as a tool to empower students, to move away from rote memorization, and toward deeper understanding, curiosity, and creativity. That’s a vision I resonate with deeply.

🌍 She articulated so well, what Human-Centered AI really means. AI is built by people, and it’s meant to serve people. So humans must always stay at the center of how we design, deploy, and govern these systems.

And perhaps the most honest and refreshing take of all: She doesn’t see AI through a dystopian or utopian lens. She places herself in what she called “the boring middle”- a space of thoughtfulness, caution, and grounded optimism. It’s a mindset I think more of us in the industry need to embrace.

She spoke of a future where AI helps us not replace who we are, but elevate it.

We are creative beings. We are learners. We are storytellers. And AI, at its best, should help us grow- not just in productivity, but in potential.

Thank you, Fei-Fei, for continuing to lead with clarity, compassion, and conviction. The field is better because of voices like yours ❤️

============================================================

--- Post 331 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 164 reactions, 22 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,070)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7428286541418041344

In AI, one of the most fundamental distinctions is this:
Are you building a system that assumes the world is predictable? 
Or one that assumes the world is uncertain?

That’s the difference between deterministic and probabilistic models.

> A deterministic model follows exact rules. Same input, same output. Always.
> A probabilistic model assumes uncertainty. Instead of one fixed answer, it models likelihoods.

Most modern AI systems, especially GenAI systems, are fundamentally probabilistic.

Large language models do not “retrieve the correct sentence.” They model probability distributions over tokens.

Image models do not “draw the right image.” They sample from learned distributions.

Even retrieval systems in RAG pipelines rank documents probabilistically. Re ranking models assign confidence scores. Evaluation systems estimate uncertainty and calibration.

If you treat these systems as deterministic, you will misunderstand how they behave. That’s why this concept is critical for data scientists and AI engineers.

You need to understand:
- Why outputs can vary
- Why temperature changes behavior
- Why confidence scores matter
- Why calibration and evaluation are essential
- Why uncertainty must be designed for

The strongest production AI systems today combine both worlds.
> Deterministic layers handle guardrails, compliance rules, orchestration logic, and safety checks. 
> Probabilistic layers handle reasoning, generation, ranking, and prediction.

My tip, you should read this: There is ongoing research on reducing non determinism in LLM inference. Thinking Machines Lab published a detailed blog on this topic. They explore how randomness enters the inference pipeline and how to better control it through decoding strategies, seed management, and system level configurations.

〰️〰️〰️

--- Post 332 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 159 reactions, 25 comments, 12 reposts (Score: 1,069)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7421578034622713856

One thing that doesn’t get talked about enough when we build LLM systems is evaluation.

We spend a lot of time discussing models, prompts, agents, infra, latency, cost. All important. But without solid evals, you’re mostly guessing whether your system is actually getting better.

At a high level, most real-world LLM systems end up using a mix of three evaluation approaches:

- Human evaluations
This is direct feedback from users or expert labelers. Thumbs up, thumbs down, comments, SME reviews. It’s slow and expensive, but it’s still the gold standard for subjective tasks and alignment with human expectations.

- Code based evaluations
These are automated checks. Does the code compile? Did the API call succeed? Is the output structured correctly? They’re fast, deterministic, and cheap. Great for objective tasks, but they tell you nothing about reasoning quality or usefulness.

- LLM based evaluations, often called LLM-as-judge
Here, another model evaluates outputs using carefully designed criteria. This scales well, works for open-ended tasks, and can even generate explanations. But it needs calibration and enough volume to reduce noise.

In practice, strong systems don’t rely on just one of these. They layer them. Code based checks for guardrails, LLM judges for iteration speed, and human evals for grounding and trust.

As a builder and as a leader, it’s very important to understand AI evals deeply. As AI professionals, this is one of the most underrated topics today. And it’s about to blow up as more teams push agents and LLMs into production.

To help people truly understand this, Arvind and I are putting together a detailed workshop with hands-on exercises focused on AI evaluations in real systems.

Arvind Narayanamurthy and I are hosting an upcoming workshop (15th Feb) on AI evals where we go deep into how this is done in real systems. If you want to level up your AI skills for 2026, do register for it: https://lnkd.in/dQSR7yhU

Qwen team keeps shipping: Qwen3 Coder 480B is live on Fireworks AI - on par with Sonnet 4 for coding! 🤯

https://lnkd.in/dzXgynZf 

Quick impressions:
• very strong agentic coding performance: SWEBench, Aider-Polyglot and other benchmarks are at the level of Claude Sonnet 4!
• native 256K context window which can be extended to 1M tokens (when using Fireworks dedicated deployments)
• great agentic performance outside of coding (general tool use): SOTA TauBench results

🧐 Interesting that Qwen decided to specialize: earlier this week, they split thinking and non-thinking. Coding model is separate, too. Combined with customization power given by open weights, it strongly matches our experience at Fireworks: there’s rarely “one size fit all”. Model and inference specialization are needed to build with GenAI.

Architecture compared to freshly released Qwen3-235B:

• shallower: 1.5x less layers, almost the same attention setup, more KV cache efficient
• bigger: 1.5-1.7x wider hidden dimensions: individual experts have 2.5x more parameters
• wider: 1.25x more experts, but the same number of activated ones
• overall ~2x more weights: 2.5*1.25/1.5 ~= 480B/235B
• only 1.6x more active parameters, because attention weights are same size

Congrats and thank you to Qwen team again! Looking forward to what people will build with Qwen3 Coder on Fireworks. It’s fun to use, I just one-shotted a visual below.

Synthetic data generation used to be a core ML component. Now it has become essential for GenAI applications too. As models move from simple prediction to multi turn reasoning and alignment, the limiting factor isn’t model size. It’s the availability of clean, structured, high signal training data. Synthetic pipelines solve that gap.

At a high level, the goal is simple. Take raw, messy text and convert it into structured instruction–response–preference signals that models can actually learn from.

Where does this matter?
✦ Enterprise copilots that require policy alignment
✦ Customer support agents tuned to domain tone
✦ AI assistants that need safe, multi step reasoning
✦ RAG systems that need structured supervision signals

As GenAI gets more capable, synthetic data becomes the capability multiplier that decides whether your system is merely functional or truly reliable.

--- Post 343 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 139 reactions, 33 comments, 10 reposts (Score: 1,045)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7373512576296435712

Everyone’s debating when we’ll reach AGI.
But very few are asking: what does the path to AGI actually look like?

This is how OpenAI is said to break down the 5 Levels of AGI from a systems engineering lens:

→ Level 1: Conversational AI
Where most models are today- great at text generation, decent at simulating conversation, but reactive and stateless. They wait for your prompt and respond. That’s it.

→ Level 2: Reasoners
These models go beyond surface-level chat. They can tackle complex, multi-step tasks like a human expert- solving math problems, debugging code, writing research summaries, without relying on external tools. It’s a major leap in cognitive capability.

→ Level 3: Agents
Now we’re talking autonomy. These systems can plan, act, and adapt over time. Think agents that remember context, make decisions, manage workflows, and use tools on your behalf. They’re not just responding, they’re operating.

→ Level 4: Innovators
This is the inflection point. AI that doesn’t just follow instructions but generates novel ideas- new inventions, product concepts, even original research. Creativity without being prompted. That’s when AI becomes a true collaborator.

→ Level 5: Organizations
The final frontier. Imagine an AI that runs an entire org- strategizing, optimizing, and executing with precision at scale. At this level, the system isn’t just helping teams… it is the team.

We’re somewhere between Levels 2 and 3 today.
But the shift to 4 and 5 will change everything, from how we build software to how we structure companies.

We’re not jumping from GPT-4(s) to full AGI overnight. (We will see what GPT-5 has to offer today 😄)

We’re climbing the stack- one level of agency, reasoning, and autonomy at a time.

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025

Cartesia Sonic-3 is the first AI voice model I’ve seen that nails Hindi perfectly.

For years, even the best text-to-speech (TTS) models struggled with Hindi. The rhythm, tonality, and emotional micro-expressions just didn’t sound human and the accent was inaccurate.

This model doesn’t just translate Hindi.
It is specially trained for it, with precise control over pacing, expressions and  tonality, all rendered in real time.

Under the hood, Sonic-3 is engineered for low-latency voice generation optimized for conversational AI agents, clocking in 3–5x faster than OpenAI’s TTS while maintaining superior transcript fidelity.

What makes it stand out technically:
→ 𝗚𝗿𝗮𝗻𝘂𝗹𝗮𝗿 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗹 𝘁𝗮𝗴𝘀 let developers dynamically modulate speed, volume, and emotion inside the transcript itself. ("Can you repeat that slower?" now works in production.)

→ 𝟰𝟮-𝗹𝗮𝗻𝗴𝘂𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝗺𝘂𝗹𝘁𝗶𝗹𝗶𝗻𝗴𝘂𝗮𝗹 𝗺𝗼𝗱𝗲𝗹 built on a single unified speaker embedding, so one voice can switch between languages like Hindi, Tamil, and English natively while maintaining accent continuity.

→ 𝟯-𝘀𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗻𝗱 𝘃𝗼𝗶𝗰𝗲 𝗰𝗹𝗼𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 powered by a low-sample adaptive cloning pipeline that enables instant personalization at scale.

→ 𝗥𝗲𝗮𝗹-𝘁𝗶𝗺𝗲 𝗶𝗻𝗳𝗲𝗿𝗲𝗻𝗰𝗲 𝘀𝘁𝗮𝗰𝗸 achieving sub-300 ms end-to-end latency at p90, tuned for live interactions like support agents, NPCs, and healthcare assistants.

→ 𝗙𝗶𝗻𝗲-𝗴𝗿𝗮𝗶𝗻𝗲𝗱 𝘁𝗿𝗮𝗻𝘀𝗰𝗿𝗶𝗽𝘁 𝗮𝗹𝗶𝗴𝗻𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁 that handles heteronyms, acronyms, and structured text (emails, IDs, phone numbers) which usually break realism in production systems.

🎧 Here is example of me trying Sonic-3’s Hindi. You have to hear it to believe it.

If you’re building voice agents, conversational AI, or multimodal assistants, keep an eye on Cartesia. They’ve raised $100M to build the most human-sounding voice models in the world, and Sonic-3 just set a new benchmark for multilingual voice AI.


hashtag
#CartesiaPartner

--- Post 347 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 170 reactions, 15 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,030)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7377884894741389312

This is a great new research from Thinking Machines Lab about Modular Manifolds.

So, here is the problem they are trying to solve 👇 
When we train large neural networks like LLMs, we usually think about data, GPUs, or parameters. But there’s a more subtle challenge inside the model itself: keeping the weights stable and well-behaved during training.

Researchers call this the “health” of tensors.
→ If weights grow too large or too small, training becomes unstable.
→ If updates to weights are unpredictable, learning slows down or even diverges.
→ Unlike activations, where we apply normalization (like layer norm), weight matrices don’t usually get the same treatment, so their behavior is harder to control.

This leads to training large models inefficient, less robust, and more expensive.

Here is what they are proposing with "Modular Manifolds"👇

The idea of Modular Manifolds is to use geometry to solve this problem.
Instead of letting weights float freely in space, they constrain them to live on the Stiefel manifold, a space where the weight matrix has orthonormal columns (which also means its singular values stay balanced).

→ In practice, the updates happen in the tangent space (a flat local approximation of the manifold).
→ Each optimization step is automatically adjusted so that weights don’t drift off-manifold.
→ After each update, the weights are “retracted” back to the manifold, keeping them healthy.

Here is how it optimized LLM training👇
By bringing geometry into the training loop, several big benefits emerge:
→ Training stays stable and the weights don’t explode or vanish, which makes the whole process more predictable.
→ The learning rate directly maps to how big each step is, so optimization feels much simpler.
→ Learning rates get spread across layers automatically, removing a lot of the manual guesswork.
→ The method brings built-in robustness, which matters even more as models get larger.
→ And in early tests on smaller benchmarks, it’s already beating AdamW while keeping the weights nicely balanced.

Training LLMs today is expensive and fragile, and "Modular Manifolds" provide a way to make optimization as principled and automatic as the forward pass itself. That means faster, safer training, less trial-and-error tuning, and potentially big savings in compute.

I believe this also would mean that we see more open-source LLM coming out from frontier labs, with the reduced investment and effort for this

This is an a pretty exciting direction that TML has proposed. If you want to read more, do check out the blog.

PS: I would recommend checking out the blog, the interactive geometric representation is very cool: https://lnkd.in/dqS82pt8

Happy Learning!

--- Post 349 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 167 reactions, 17 comments, 10 reposts (Score: 1,025)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7413236365187997697

If you’re getting started in AI engineering, you need to understand how LLM fine-tuning actually works.

Out of the box GenAI models are impressive.
They perform well on generic benchmarks and look great in demos.

But the real value shows up when these models are customized for real business workflows.

In production, most teams hit a familiar curve.
You get to ~80% quality fairly quickly with a strong base model.

Pushing from 80% to 85% often comes from better prompt engineering and system design.
Going from 85% to 90%+ is where fine-tuning starts to matter.

There are two fine-tuning approaches worth understanding early:

✦ Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT)
- You train the model on labeled input-output examples.

- This works well when the task is clearly defined and you know what “good” looks like.

- Common use cases include domain adaptation, output formatting, and style control.

✦ Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT)
- Here, the model learns from feedback signals instead of fixed answers.

- This becomes useful when quality is nuanced, such as reasoning, ranking, or decision-making.

- It’s often used when examples alone stop moving the needle.

In real systems, this is rarely a binary choice.
Models usually evolve as requirements get stricter and expectations increase, moving from example-driven learning to feedback-driven refinement.

If you want to get hands-on and actually try this end to end, here’s a solid resource to start with: https://lnkd.in/eTmktaVp

============================================================

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7408560396841861120

Dynamic agents are AI systems that can plan, adapt, and act at runtime instead of following a fixed, pre-defined workflow.

With generative AI models, this matters because the model is no longer just producing text. It is making decisions.

A dynamic agent takes a high-level goal, reasons about the current context, decides what action to take next, and adjusts its strategy based on intermediate results. The sequence of steps is not hardcoded. It emerges from the model’s reasoning.

This is especially relevant for modern GenAI systems because large language models are good at:
✦ Decomposing problems
✦ Reasoning over partial information
✦ Choosing tools conditionally
✦ Adapting when assumptions change

Instead of building one workflow per use case, dynamic agents let the same model handle many variations of a task by deciding the control flow on the fly.
That is why dynamic agents show up in research assistants, complex RAG systems, and enterprise copilots. They turn generative models from passive responders into active problem-solving systems.

The key shift is simple: from generating outputs to driving decisions.

--- Post 353 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 153 reactions, 23 comments, 8 reposts (Score: 1,011)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7388609718572879873

If you’re an AI engineer building multi-agent systems, this one’s for you.

As AI applications evolve beyond single-task agents, we’re entering an era where multiple intelligent agents collaborate to solve complex, real-world problems.

But success in multi-agent systems isn’t just about spinning up more agents, it’s about designing the right coordination architecture, deciding how agents talk to each other, split responsibilities, and come to shared decisions.

Just like software engineers rely on design patterns, AI engineers can benefit from agent design patterns to build systems that are scalable, fault-tolerant, and easier to maintain.

Here are 7 foundational patterns I believe every AI practitioner should understand:

→ 𝗣𝗮𝗿𝗮𝗹𝗹𝗲𝗹 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Run agents independently on different subtasks. This increases speed and reduces bottlenecks, ideal for parallelized search, ensemble predictions, or document classification at scale.

→ 𝗦𝗲𝗾𝘂𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗮𝗹 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Chain agents so the output of one becomes the input of the next. Works well for multi-step reasoning, document workflows, or approval pipelines.

→ 𝗟𝗼𝗼𝗽 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Enable feedback between agents for iterative refinement. Think of use cases like model evaluation, coding agents testing each other, or closed-loop optimization.

→ 𝗥𝗼𝘂𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Use a central controller to direct tasks to the right agent(s) based on input. Helpful when agents have specialized roles (e.g., image vs. text processors) and dynamic routing is needed.

→ 𝗔𝗴𝗴𝗿𝗲𝗴𝗮𝘁𝗼𝗿 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Merge outputs from multiple agents into a single result. Useful for ranking, voting, consensus-building, or when synthesizing diverse perspectives.

→ 𝗡𝗲𝘁𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸 (𝗛𝗼𝗿𝗶𝘇𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗮𝗹) 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Allow all agents to communicate freely in a many-to-many fashion. Enables collaborative systems like swarm robotics or autonomous fleets.
✔️ Pros: Resilient and decentralized
⚠️ Cons: Can introduce redundancy and increase communication overhead

→ 𝗛𝗶𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗿𝗰𝗵𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗹 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻
Structure agents in a supervisory tree. Higher-level agents delegate tasks and oversee execution. Useful for managing complexity in large agent teams.
✔️ Pros: Clear roles and top-down coordination
⚠️ Cons: Risk of bottlenecks or failure at the top node

These patterns aren’t mutually exclusive. In fact, most robust systems combine multiple strategies. You might use a router to assign tasks, parallel execution to speed up processing, and a loop for refinement, all in the same system.

Visual inspiration: Weaviate

--- Post 354 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 135 reactions, 32 comments, 7 reposts (Score: 1,009)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7427532908963303425

Most teams think scaling agents is an architecture problem.
It reminds me of early deep learning times, when we thought better models alone would fix everything. Eventually we learned that data pipelines mattered just as much.

The Alibaba team’s recent work on Scaling Agents via Continual Pre-training makes a similar point for agent systems.

Today, teams doing agent training are doing this: 
- teams collect a batch of trajectories from tool calls or simulated environments
- fine-tune the model on those trajectories
- deploy the system, and then monitor performance

That works in dev environments. But agents don’t live in dev environments forever. When they are in prod- tools evolve, APIs change, the knowledge- base expands, and user workflows might also shift. 

Plus, when we train AI agents today, we take a general-purpose model and try to teach it agentic skills (tool use, planning, reasoning) all at once during fine-tuning. The model gets overwhelmed trying to learn everything at the same time.

What the Alibaba team proposed is - instead of going straight from pre-training → fine-tuning, they insert a new stage called Agentic Continual Pre-training (Agentic CPT). Think of it as an "agent bootcamp" before the real training begins.

Here is how Agentic CPT works, in 3 steps:
1️⃣ How to plan: given a complex question, break it down and figure out what tools to use first
2️⃣ How to reason: connect multiple pieces of information logically to reach an answer
3️⃣ How to self-correct: at every step, evaluate multiple options and verify "did I make the right call?"

That last one is the real breakthrough. Most agents learn what to do. This one learns how to judge whether what it did was right.

Their model, AgentFounder-30B, beats every open-source agent and even surpasses some of the best commercial products:
→ Outperforms OpenAI Deep Research on HLE (the hardest AI benchmark out there) 
→ Outperforms OpenAI o3 on GAIA and Xbench 
→ Beats models 2-3x its size like DeepSeek-V3.1

It's not the biggest model winning. It's the one with the best training data.

Why you need to pay attention!

We're in the middle of an agentic AI arms race, Claude computer use, OpenAI Operator, Google Mariner, Perplexity Deep Research. Everyone is building agents. But most teams are competing on the fine-tuning stage alone. This paper shows there's a huge untapped lever most people are ignoring: what happens before fine-tuning.

If you're building agents, note this:
- Your base model matters more than you think, fine-tuning can't fix a weak foundation
- Teach your agents to self-verify, not just act. Decision-making > imitation
- Build a data engine, not a data pipeline, the best agents will come from teams that continuously generate better training data

============================================================

--- Post 358 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 163 reactions, 15 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 977)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7385362206252576768

If you’re getting started in AI engineering, one of the most useful things you can learn is how information retrieval actually works under the hood.

1️⃣ Embedding models take your text, audio, or images and turn them into numerical vectors that represent meaning. For example, if you ask, “What’s the best laptop for travel?”, the model doesn’t look for those exact words - it looks for documents about lightweight laptops, battery life, and portability.

That’s because embeddings capture semantic similarity, not just keywords. They power the search layer in most GenAI systems by helping you find contextually relevant data quickly.

2️⃣ Re-ranking models then step in to fine-tune the results. They take the top candidates and figure out which ones truly answer your question.
So if embeddings bring you 10 possible answers, the re-ranker will reorder them so that the one that best matches your intent, say “best laptops for digital nomads”, comes first.

If embeddings are about recall, re-ranking is about precision.

Together, they form the foundation of RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) systems, the architecture that powers copilots, AI search tools, and enterprise assistants.

→ Embeddings fetch what’s relevant
→ Re-ranking ensures it’s the right answer

You’ll see these models in everything from chatbots and search tools to AI agents that need reliable memory or factual grounding.

If you want to build real-world GenAI systems, understanding embeddings and re-ranking is where you start thinking like a system designer, not just a prompt engineer.

Here is detailed blog that dives deeper into how these models work and how to implement them using open-source tools.

--- Post 362 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 163 reactions, 14 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 967)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7367722804521238528

Evaluating LLMs is not like testing traditional software.
Traditional systems are deterministic → pass/fail.
LLMs are probabilistic → same input, different outputs, shifting behaviors over time.

That makes model selection and monitoring one of the hardest engineering problems today.

This is where Eval Protocol (EP) developed by Fireworks AI is so powerful. 

It’s an open-source framework for building an internal model leaderboard, where you can define, run, and track evals that actually reflect your business needs.

→ Simulated Users – generate synthetic but realistic user interactions to stress-test models under lifelike conditions.

→ evaluation_test – pytest-compatible evals (pointwise, groupwise, all) so you can treat model behavior like unit tests in CI/CD.

→ MCP Extensions – evaluate agents that use tools, multi-step reasoning, or multi-turn dialogue via Model Context Protocol.

→ UI Review – a dashboard to visualize eval results, compare across models, and catch regressions before they ship.

Instead of relying on generic benchmarks, EP lets you encode your own success criteria and continuously measure models against them.

If you’re serious about scaling LLMs in production, this is worth a look: evalprotocol.io

============================================================
