If you are an aspiring data scientist, you need to deeply understand how time series analysis works.

Time series analysis is one of those core foundations that quietly powers a huge number of real business decisions. Anywhere data changes over time, this shows up.

At its core, time series analysis is about modeling patterns across time like trends, seasonality, cycles, and noise so we can forecast what comes next and understand why things move the way they do.

You see it everywhere in practice:
✦ Demand forecasting in retail and supply chain planning
✦ Revenue, churn, and growth forecasting in SaaS businesses
✦ Anomaly detection in finance and fraud monitoring
✦ Capacity planning and reliability metrics in infrastructure systems
✦ Forecasting user engagement, traffic, and conversions in product analytics

What trips people up is that time series is not just “run a model and plot a line.”
There are a lot of nuances once you start building real systems:
✦ Stationarity vs non-stationarity
✦ Seasonality and regime shifts
✦ Choosing between classical, statistical, and ML-based models
✦ Picking the right evaluation metrics for forecasts
✦ Understanding when forecasts break and why

That’s exactly why I put together a time series cheat sheet.
It’s designed to give you a quick, structured overview of:
✦ Common models and when to use them
✦ Key assumptions to watch out for
✦ Metrics interviewers actually expect you to know
✦ The big picture of how time series is applied in real business settings

Super useful both for interview prep and as a fast refresher when you’re working on real problems.

--- Post 366 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 132 reactions, 29 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 958)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7430285620603797505

Most AI teams are obsessed with the model. The real bottleneck in 2026 is Infrastructure.

When you’re building a GenAI system, everything feels fine. The outputs look good, the tools call correctly, and it all works on the hardware in front of you. But the moment you try to scale, a whole new set of problems shows up that were completely invisible before:

- Response times that worked on one machine fall apart across multiple GPUs
- Requests start failing under real traffic loads
- Data consistency becomes a nightmare across cloud regions
- Costs spiral faster than anyone budgeted for

That’s where most AI failures actually happen. Not during training, but at inference, when real users show up and the system needs to be fast, cheap, safe, and reliable all at once.

The hardware story is getting more complex too. It’s no longer just “get more GPUs.” Different hardware is purpose-built for different parts of the stack, and picking the wrong one isn’t just a performance issue, it quietly destroys your budget.

The teams winning right now aren’t always the ones shipping the best models. They’re the ones who built infrastructure that lets them iterate fast, deploy without drama, and scale without things quietly breaking.

That’s the real moat in 2026.

============================================================

Most people think building a good chatbot or RAG pipeline is all about picking the right model.

Context is the hidden power source behind every great LLM system. It decides whether your chatbot feels smart or clueless, your agent feels “alive” or forgetful, and whether your app costs $10 or $1000 to run.

In this workshop, we’ll break down what context really means, not just theoretically, but in the way engineers use it in production. We’ll talk about the invisible systems that make or break performance, and show you how to design them with intention.

If you’ve ever wondered why your chatbot goes off-track, your RAG feels bloated, or your agent keeps forgetting what it did two minutes ago, this session will change the way you design.

We’ll dive into:
→ What context really means in LLMs and how it shapes performance
→ The toolkit that helps you engineer it better: Summarization, Pruning, Query Rewrite, RAG, Context Offloading, Isolation, and Memory
→ How to optimize for accuracy, cost, and reliability across use cases
→ A live demo showing how these ideas come together in real time

This workshop is for every engineer and student who’s ready to move beyond prompt tuning and start thinking like a system designer.

🧩 Context Engineering
📅 November 8
🕐 1.5 hours
🎙️ with Arvind Narayanamurthy & me

🎟️ Save your spot: https://lnkd.in/dvFmMiRr

What are world models?
Recently, World Labs launched Marble, their spatial intelligence model, and it’s a good reminder of how important world models are becoming. In simple terms, a world model lets an agent build an internal understanding of its environment instead of reacting frame by frame.

Here’s the core idea:
- A vision module turns raw observations into a compact latent state.
- A memory module learns how that state changes over time.
- A controller uses both to choose the next action.

This structure gives agents the ability to predict, not just perceive. And prediction is what unlocks real capabilities in robotics, autonomous systems, and any task where the environment is dynamic and uncertain.

As spatial intelligence accelerates, world models are shaping the next generation of AI systems that can learn, plan, and adapt with far more stability and reliability.

Do watch out for this space!

============================================================

In my recent conversation with Natalie Han, PhD, VP and CPO of Gen AI at SAP, we got into a topic I think every company is wrestling with right now. When AI can theoretically touch every part of the business, how do you decide what to build first.

What stood out to me was how practical her lens is.
✦ It always starts with real customer engagement. Not whiteboard ideas, but the work of helping customers implement systems or streamline existing processes. That’s where the true signals show up.

✦ Some use cases consistently surface as high value. Text-heavy generative workflows tend to create immediate impact because they free up time in places people don’t even realize they’re losing it.

✦ Prioritization only works when it’s anchored in value. Looking at the total time spent, the impact created, and the actual effort saved keeps teams honest about what will move the needle.

✦ And none of it matters unless the technology is ready. The best opportunities sit where business need and technical feasibility meet.

The conversation reminded me that AI strategy isn’t about chasing the widest set of possibilities. It’s about choosing the few that create momentum, trust, and real wins for the people using the system.

============================================================

--- Post 379 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 134 reactions, 22 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 902)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7399142867140591616

Most teams get AI agents wrong before they even start building them.

People jump straight into tools and workflows without actually defining the agent itself.
And when the foundation is fuzzy, the system becomes unscalable, brittle, and impossible to debug.

I’ve been reading Sam Bhagwat's new book- Patterns for Building AI Agents, and one framework stood out because of how simple and timeless it is.

If you are building an AI agent, here is the structure you should start with:

1. Write down everything you want your agent to do
Ask repeatedly, “What are we missing?”
Comprehensiveness matters more than cleverness at this stage.

2. Group similar capabilities together
Look for tasks that
- pull from the same data sources
- could be performed by the same job title
- are returned by the same API call

3. Figure out the natural divisions
Separate by
- responsible teams
- the type of task like fetching vs. synthesizing vs. triggering actions
- the steps in your business process

4. Group related capabilities into agents
This gives you clean separation, clearer routing, and agents that actually reflect how your business works.

This is the part of AI engineering that gets skipped the most, but it’s the part that decides whether your agent system scales or collapses.

If you’re building an agentic system right now, start here. It will save you months of rework and help you move faster when it really matters.

〰️〰️〰️
♻️ Share this with your network 
🔔 Follow me (Aishwarya Srinivasan) for more data science and AI insights

--- Post 382 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 139 reactions, 18 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 893)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7410710837197213697

Supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement fine-tuning get mentioned together a lot, but in practice they answer very different questions when you’re customizing LLMs.

✦ Supervised fine-tuning is what I reach for when I know exactly what I want the model to do.

You use it when you have solid input-output examples, when the task has a clear notion of correctness, and when you care about things like structure, formatting, domain language, or consistency. It’s the most reliable way to teach a model how your system should respond. Stable, predictable, and easier to debug.

✦ Reinforcement fine-tuning comes in when “correct” is fuzzy.

That’s when you’re optimizing for preferences rather than answers. Helpfulness, tone, safety, reasoning quality, refusal behavior. Things you can evaluate, but can’t fully write down as labels. Reinforcement fine-tuning is powerful, but it’s also expensive and very sensitive to how you define the reward.

In real systems, this usually isn’t an either-or decision.

Most teams start with supervised fine-tuning to get the model speaking the right language. Reinforcement fine-tuning comes later, once the basics are in place, to shape how the model behaves in edge cases.

The mistake I see most often is jumping to either of the fine-tuning too early, start with really deep prompt engineering, then SFT, then RFT. 

If you can clearly specify what good output looks like, supervised fine-tuning is usually enough. If you can’t specify it but you can judge it, that’s when reinforcement fine-tuning starts to make sense.

LLM customization is less about using advanced techniques and more about knowing which problem you’re actually trying to solve.

============================================================

Mindset for AI adoption is becoming one of the biggest differentiators inside companies. In my conversation with Natalie Han, PhD, VP and CPO of Gen AI at SAP, we talked about what it actually takes for teams to work with AI instead of feeling like it’s replacing them.

Natalie’s perspective cuts through the noise.
✦ Uncertainty isn’t a bug. It’s the environment we're operating in. The teams that win are the ones that learn fast, adapt fast, and stay open to new tools shaping their workflows.

✦ Mindset is a capability. Continuous learning, training internal teams, and educating customers and partners is what creates real adoption. It’s the only way to move through change with confidence instead of resistance.

✦ AI shines when it frees people to do higher value work. Natalie shared SAP’s example from SuccessFactors: performance reviews. Managers spend hours gathering feedback, understanding goals, and writing documents instead of focusing on developing their people.

✦ SAP built an embedded AI agent that synthesizes feedback, surfaces insights, and cuts through the operational load. The goal isn’t to replace the manager. It’s to remove the part of their day that keeps them from coaching effectively.

The conversation reinforced something I see across every industry right now. AI doesn’t erase the human role. It removes the busywork so the human role can finally expand.

============================================================

--- Post 388 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 114 reactions, 29 comments, 3 reposts (Score: 866)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7424318489936568320

A lot of conversations around LLMs focus on prompts, tools, or demos. Much less attention is paid to how these models are actually trained, and that gap shows up quickly when things break or behave in unexpected ways.

At a high level, there are three phases that matter:
- Pre-training is where the model learns language and broad world knowledge from large-scale text.
- Post-training is where it learns how to do useful tasks through supervised and reinforcement fine-tuning.
- Alignment is where its behavior is shaped using human preferences so it behaves reasonably in real interactions.

These phases are doing very different things, and they explain a lot about why models have the strengths and limitations they do. If you don’t understand this progression, it’s easy to misuse fine-tuning, overestimate what prompting can fix, or misdiagnose model failures.

I’ve written a detailed post walking through all three stages, how they work in practice today, and how the training methods have evolved. 

The goal is to build a clearer mental model of what’s inside these systems, not to treat them as black boxes. 

Do give it a read: https://lnkd.in/dPbiEmiY

============================================================

--- Post 393 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Sep 19, 2025

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7368464281484382208

If you’re looking to land your first data science internship, here are 10 strategies that go beyond the obvious, and actually work:

1️⃣ Start with one tangible business problem
Don’t start with models - start with pain points. Find a local business, club, or nonprofit and ask: What decision do you struggle with the most? Then solve it with data.

2️⃣ Document the why, not just the how
It’s not impressive that you used XGBoost. What’s impressive is why you chose it, what didn’t work before, and how your decisions reduced error rates by 20%.

3️⃣ Master one “power tool” deeply
Pick SQL, Pandas, or scikit-learn - then go really deep. I don’t mean just syntax. Learn edge cases, performance trade-offs, debugging. You’ll stand out for how you think, not just what you know.

4️⃣ Quantify impact on your resume
“Built a dashboard” is vague. “Built a dashboard that saved 3 analysts 5+ hours/week” speaks volumes. Tie your work to time, money, or decisions.

5️⃣ Contribute to open-source meaningfully
Don’t just fix typos. Pick a bug tagged “good first issue,” and make sure it’s non-trivial. This shows real-world code fluency and willingness to work within large codebases.

6️⃣ Ask for code reviews - even informal ones
DM someone you admire and ask: Can I get your feedback on a small project? I’d love to hear what I’m missing. Most won’t respond. But the 1 who does? that is your edge!

7️⃣ Practice a two-minute “whiteboard walkthrough”
Internship interviews are not Kaggle competitions. Can you clearly explain your project, decisions, results, and trade-offs without opening your laptop?

8️⃣ Leverage hidden-curriculum courses
You don’t need another Coursera cert. Find courses that teach how to think like a DS, not just “how to build a model.” I loved fast.ai and made custom notes I still refer to.

9️⃣ Align with the team’s stack
Before you apply, reverse-engineer the role. Do they use Airflow? Snowflake? Hugging Face? Tailor your personal projects and resume accordingly. Match their environment.

🔟 Treat the interview like hypothesis testing
You’re not there to impress. You’re there to validate a fit. Ask sharp questions about the role, data maturity, and mentorship culture. You’re evaluating them too.

Internships aren’t just about “getting in”. They’re about compounding your learning so fast that by the time you graduate, you’re not looking for your first job - you’re choosing it.

♻️ Share it with someone who’s stuck in the “I need experience to get experience” loop

Follow me on IG https://lnkd.in/denE_Zpw for beginner-friendly tips, tools, and insights to get started!

============================================================

--- Post 395 ---

2025 was the year the conversation shifted from prompt engineering to context engineering.

Prompt engineering was never just “clever wording.” There’s real rigor behind writing good instructions. But when you start building AI agents, prompts alone stop being the main abstraction.

Today, prompt engineering is just one part of a much bigger surface area.

Context engineering is about everything an agent sees and uses to act:

✦ system and user prompts
✦ tools and function calls
✦ memory and long-term state
✦ conversation history
✦ structured outputs and constraints

Once agents can plan, call tools, persist memory, and operate across multiple steps, the quality of context matters more than any single prompt ever will.

This is also where most production failures come from. Not weak models. Not bad prompts. Missing, noisy, or poorly assembled context.

There’s still a big skill gap here!! 

A lot of people use the term “context engineering” without really understanding what falls under it or how these pieces work together.

If you’re an AI engineer trying to understand this deeply, I’ve put together a detailed blog breaking down context engineering end to end, with concrete examples and mental models.

Read it here: https://lnkd.in/gKC72euc

--- Post 404 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 112 reactions, 27 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 842)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7398439477293162496

Most people talk about AI agents
Very few talk about context engineering

Context engineering is quickly becoming the skill that separates agents that hallucinate from agents that reliably execute multi step workflows.

To make this concrete, look at Manus AI. They were among the first to ship a browser agent that can navigate, extract, summarize, and act inside the web environment. And they published one of the clearest deep dives into how a production agent actually manages context at scale.

After studying their pipeline, I put together a set of flashcards as a quick primer. Here is the distilled version.

1. Context is not a prompt. It is a system.
Manus structures context across instructions, tool outputs, files, memory, and agent states. They treat it like an engineered data flow rather than a single block of text. When a system runs hundreds of steps in a loop, this precision matters.

2. KV cache stability is free performance.
A stable prefix lets the model reuse its internal cache, which reduces latency and cost significantly. Even small variations, like a random timestamp, break the cache. Optimizing this alone changes the economics of long running agents.

3. Tools should be masked, not removed.
Removing a tool mid loop breaks schema predictability. Manus keeps the toolset constant and masks tools contextually. This makes agent behavior more stable and more interpretable.

4. External memory beats cramming.
When browser actions generate large observations, Manus writes them to files and stores only the references in context. This avoids context bloat and keeps the agent focused.

5. Recitation increases reliability.
When loops get long, agents forget. Reciting goals into a todo file, summarizing progress, and placing objectives near the attention window helps the agent stay grounded.

6. Failures belong in context.
They keep errors, stack traces, and broken actions. This allows the model to observe its own mistakes and correct future steps. It is the closest thing to real self improvement agents have today.

All of this makes one thing clear.
As agents move from novelty to infrastructure, the real differentiation will not come from prompting. It will come from engineering the context that shapes the model’s reasoning.

If you want a quick way to learn these concepts, I turned my notes into flashcards.

--- Post 405 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 134 reactions, 16 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 838)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7409279215205761024

Reinforcement Learning(RL) is becoming one of the most critical concepts to understand in Gen AI.

If you are new to RL. here is the simplest way to think about it 👇

- You have an agent that interacts with an environment.
- The agent takes an action, observes the outcome, and receives a reward or a penalty.
- Over time, the agent learns a policy that helps it take better actions to maximize long-term rewards.

This methodology is not new.

RL has historically been used in many parts of machine learning and AI. It has powered game-playing systems like AlphaGo, helped robots learn how to walk or manipulate objects, and optimized decision-making in areas like recommender systems and ad bidding.

What is new is how central RL has become to modern GenAI/ LLMs.

There are two RL algorithms you should absolutely know about:

✦ DPO (Direct Preference Optimization)
DPO directly optimizes a model using preference data. Instead of learning a separate reward model, it learns from comparisons like “this response is better than that one.”

This makes DPO simpler, more stable, and very popular for preference alignment in large language models.

✦ PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization)
PPO is a classic reinforcement learning algorithm that uses a reward model and carefully constrains updates to prevent the model from drifting too far.
It is more complex, but extremely powerful and still widely used in large-scale RLHF pipelines.

Why this matters in Gen AI:

✦ Reinforcement learning is the foundation of RLHF (Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback)

✦ It enables reinforcement fine-tuning, where models learn preferences, not just correctness

✦ It plays a key role in aligning models with human intent, safety, and usefulness

RL helps models improve over time through feedback, not just fixed datasets.

When clean, labeled data is scarce, reinforcement learning becomes a powerful way to boost model performance.

It enables learning from preferences and outcomes, even when success criteria are fuzzy or subjective.

This is why reinforcement learning plays a critical role in improving Generative AI systems beyond traditional fine-tuning.

Everyone is racing to build bigger models. But the companies that will actually win with AI are the ones that spent decades getting their data right. You can’t retrofit fifty years of business context in a sprint.

That’s the part that really hit me in my conversation with Natalie Han, PhD, VP and CPO of Gen AI at SAP. SAP isn’t trying to outmodel anyone. They’re building enterprise AI on top of the deepest operational data foundation in the world.

✦ The data foundation is the strategy. SAP has fifty years of business processes, transactions, and workflows baked into its ecosystem. That institutional memory becomes the context AI needs to be truly useful.

✦ Grounding changes everything. Their AI isn’t generic. It’s tied to real business processes customers already trust. That grounding is what makes enterprise AI accurate, stable, and usable at scale.

✦ SAP sits in a rare position. They control both the data layer and the intelligence layer. Connecting the two lets them build AI use cases that are more reliable and more context aware than anything you can bolt on after the fact.

✦ AI first isn’t a slogan for them. All applications and data flow into the business data cloud, creating one unified layer that powers responsible, reliable, and relevant AI.

✦ It creates a data flywheel. The more workflows flow into the foundation, the stronger and more valuable the AI becomes for customers.

What stayed with me is this. The future of enterprise AI won’t be defined by the flashiest model. It’ll be defined by the companies whose data foundation is strong enough for AI to actually deliver outcomes people can trust.

🔗 to learn more below

--- Post 411 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 128 reactions, 15 comments, 8 reposts (Score: 806)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7398080526978363392

Most ML systems don’t fail because of poor models. They fail at the systems level! 

You can have a world-class model architecture, but if you can’t reproduce your training runs, automate deployments, or monitor model drift, you don’t have a reliable system. You have a science project.

That’s where MLOps comes in.

🔹 𝗠𝗟𝗢𝗽𝘀 𝗟𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗹 𝟬 - 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝘂𝗮𝗹 & 𝗙𝗿𝗮𝗴𝗶𝗹𝗲

This is where many teams operate today.

→ Training runs are triggered manually (notebooks, scripts)
→ No CI/CD, no tracking of datasets or parameters
→ Model artifacts are not versioned
→ Deployments are inconsistent, sometimes even manual copy-paste to production

There’s no real observability, no rollback strategy, no trust in reproducibility.

To move forward:
→ Start versioning datasets, models, and training scripts
→ Introduce structured experiment tracking (e.g. MLflow, Weights & Biases)
→ Add automated tests for data schema and training logic

This is the foundation. Without it, everything downstream is unstable.


🔹 𝗠𝗟𝗢𝗽𝘀 𝗟𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗹 𝟭 - 𝗔𝘂𝘁𝗼𝗺𝗮𝘁𝗲𝗱 & 𝗥𝗲𝗽𝗲𝗮𝘁𝗮𝗯𝗹𝗲

Here, you start treating ML like software engineering.

→ Training pipelines are orchestrated (Kubeflow, Vertex Pipelines, Airflow)
→ Every commit triggers CI: code linting, schema checks, smoke training runs
→ Artifacts are logged and versioned, models are registered before deployment
→ Deployments are reproducible and traceable

This isn’t about chasing tools, it’s about building trust in your system.

You know exactly which dataset and code version produced a given model. You can roll back. You can iterate safely.

To get here:
→ Automate your training pipeline
→ Use registries to track models and metadata
→ Add monitoring for drift, latency, and performance degradation in production


My 2 cents 🫰

→ Most ML projects don’t die because the model didn’t work.
→ They die because no one could explain what changed between the last good version and the one that broke.
→ MLOps isn’t overhead. It’s the only path to stable, scalable ML systems.
→ Start small, build systematically, treat your pipeline as a product.

If you’re building for reliability, not just performance, you’re already ahead.

Workflow inspired by: Google Cloud

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

I’m honestly amused, by how often people are surprised when I tell them that I use a lot of custom GPTs in my day-to-day work.

They are trained on my own posts, video scripts, my technical blogs etc. And then there are a bunch of out-of-the-box GPTs from the GPT Store that I reach for regularly. None of this feels fancy to me anymore. It’s just how I get work done faster, stay consistent, and avoid starting from a blank page every time.

What I’ve realized though is that most people either don’t know you can do this, or they don’t quite know how to set it up in a way that’s actually useful. The idea of custom GPTs sounds powerful, but the practical “how” is missing for a lot of folks.

So I sat down and put together a very detailed, step-by-step guide. It walks through how I build my own custom GPTs and how I decide which out-of-the-box GPTs are actually worth using for different needs.

If this is something you’ve been curious about or slightly confused by, it might help: https://lnkd.in/gSCYxVS5

PS: I’ve been writing a lot more long-form pieces on Substack lately. If you’re enjoying this kind of behind-the-scenes thinking, you might like those too.

Here is a useful way to think about intelligence in AI agents.

Instead of treating an agent as one monolithic system, it helps to break intelligence down into the capabilities you are actually trying to automate.

This framework breaks agent intelligence into eight types. Not every agent needs all of them.

✦ Linguistic intelligence for understanding intent, instructions, tool calls, and multi-turn interaction

✦ Artistic intelligence for creativity, aesthetics, multimodal generation, and expressive output

✦ Numerical intelligence for planning, optimization, scoring, trade-offs, and reinforcement learning

✦ Sensory intelligence for grounding through vision, audio, touch, and real-time signals

✦ Spatial intelligence for navigation, mapping, object manipulation, and reasoning about physical space

✦ Emotional intelligence for human-facing interactions where tone, trust, comfort, and safety matter

✦ Physical intelligence for turning decisions into coordinated actions in the real world

✦ Reflective intelligence for memory, feedback loops, error analysis, and improvement over time

Once you think this way, system design becomes clearer.

You can break a workflow into tasks and sub-tasks, then ask:
What type of intelligence does this step actually require?

Some components need language.
Some need perception.
Some need control and execution.
Some need learning and self-correction.

This is especially useful for multi-agent systems. Instead of one “general” agent, you design smaller agents that are specialized by the intelligence they need.

〰️〰️〰️

Author: aishwarya-srinivasan

One thing I keep noticing with LLM applications is that teams overestimate how hard modeling is and underestimate how hard evaluation is.

A lot of energy goes into improving the model side of the system. That work is important. But without evaluation, it’s hard to know whether any change is actually moving the system forward or just changing its behavior.

In practice, evaluation for LLM applications usually comes down to a small set of fundamentals:

✦ Correctness. Is the answer factually right?
✦ Relevance. Does it actually answer the user’s question?
✦ Groundedness. Is the response supported by the retrieved context, or is the model improvising?
✦ Retrieval quality. Are we bringing the right information into the context in the first place?

If these aren’t measured, iteration becomes subjective. You can’t reliably compare versions. You can’t tell whether hallucinations are increasing or decreasing. You can’t reason clearly about tradeoffs.

Evaluation doesn’t have to be complicated, but it does have to be intentional. Once these signals are in place, every change becomes something you can reason about, not just react to.

That’s the difference between a system that looks good and a system you can confidently improve over time.

Arvind Narayanamurthy and I are going to be hosting a detailed, hands-on workshop on AI evaluation. This is relevant whether you’re a builder, a tech leader, or a product manager. 

You don’t have to be writing code day to day, but if you’re involved in building or owning LLM applications, this will help you build strong intuition for how LLM evaluation actually works. We’ll also be doing hands-on exercises as part of the workshop: https://lnkd.in/dQSR7yhU

--- Post 427 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 114 reactions, 8 comments, 8 reposts (Score: 666)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7409646766595182592

Hidden Markov Models (HMMs) are a foundational idea in machine learning for modeling sequential data.

An HMM assumes that the system has hidden states you cannot directly observe, and observations that are generated from those states.

The key assumption is the Markov property:
the current state depends only on the previous state.

An HMM is defined by three components:

✦ Hidden states (Q)
The true underlying state of the system.

✦ Transition probabilities (A)
P(state_t | state_{t−1})
How the system evolves over time.

✦ Emission probabilities (B)
P(observation_t | state_t)
How hidden states generate observations.

With this setup, HMMs answer three core problems:

✦ Likelihood
What is the probability of an observation sequence?

✦ Decoding
What hidden state sequence most likely produced the observations? (Solved using Viterbi)

✦ Learning
How do we learn the transition and emission probabilities? (Solved using Forward-Backward)

Why this still matters? 

- HMMs powered early speech recognition, NLP, and time-series models. Modern deep learning systems replaced the models, but not the idea.

- Latent states, temporal dependencies, and probabilistic sequence reasoning all come directly from HMMs.

HMMs are not outdated. They are foundational.

〰️〰️〰️

Arvind and I are super excited to be hosting an AI evals hands-on workshop on February 15.

It’s a 90-minute session where we’ll cover the fundamentals of AI evaluations and then actually do it together. Not theory only. You’ll walk away having built and reasoned through real evals yourself. 

If you’re aspiring to be an AI engineer or planning to upskill for AI roles in 2026, understanding how to evaluate LLM applications is critical.

Building an LLM app is often the easy part. Knowing whether it truly works in production is where most teams struggle.

LLM evaluation is not just about correctness anymore. You are judging usefulness, safety, faithfulness to sources, consistency across runs, cost, latency, and real user outcomes across prompts, RAG pipelines, tools, and agentic workflows.

Because this is hard, many teams fall back to vibe-evals. If a few outputs look good, they ship. That approach breaks quickly when models change, prompts regress, retrieval fails, or user behavior shifts. Without evals, problems surface only after users complain.

Why learning evals matters:
✦ Evals turn LLM development from guesswork into engineering
✦ They help catch regressions as models, prompts, or tools evolve
✦ They are essential for scaling RAG systems and agentic workflows
✦ In 2026, strong teams use hybrid evals combining automated metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, and targeted human review

Evals are no longer optional. They are a core AI skill.

In this workshop, Arvind and I will walk through how AI evals are done in real systems, with hands-on exercises so you build intuition.

This is designed for AI engineers and tech leads building systems, and even business leaders who want intuition for what “good” really means in production. Sequoia Capital just dropped a report called “2026: This Is AGI”, and I spent some time reading it this morning.

What I liked most is that they didn’t get stuck arguing over some academic definition of AGI. Because honestly, every AI researcher I’ve met has a slightly different answer anyway. The classic line has always been - "we’ll know it when we see it"

Sequoia’s take is refreshingly simple - "AGI is the ability to figure things out"

Which in my opinion makes more sense, than associating AGI with "smartest model". Any of the AI Agents that we are building today is meant to be Reasoning + Planning + Action based. The whole point of the AI Agents, are that they are designed for tasks that are not deterministic, and if that's the case, the best metric to measure AGI should be - how it navigates unknown territories.

What’s coming next feels more like delegation.
Instead of “help me think,” it’s “go do this.”
Instead of saving minutes, it’s owning chunks of work.
Instead of one interaction, it’s hours of sustained effort.

I have been playing around with Anthropic's Cowork on my computer for the last few weeks and I can't begin to tell you how much mature planning and agentic capabilities have become. I am really excited for the AI Agents as copilot tools!

If you haven't read the report, it is a fun one, check it out: https://lnkd.in/gyAV-ASQ

--- Post 433 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 88 reactions, 19 comments, 2 reposts (Score: 634)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7402756793690611712

When you spend enough time with companies that are experimenting with AI agents, one thing becomes clear very quickly.

Everyone is excited about what agents can unlock, and at the same time, everyone is trying to answer the same question: How do you actually define an AI agent the right way?

This has come up in almost every conversation I’ve had recently. And as teams move from “let’s try this” to “let’s actually build something real,” they’re looking for simple, dependable ways to break the problem down.

That’s why I really liked the way Sam Bhagwat frames it in Patterns of AI Agents. The visual below captures a clean structure that a lot of teams can use immediately.

1. Start with everything you want the agent to handle.
2. Group related capabilities.
3. Identify the natural divisions.
4. And from there, shape those clusters into agents.

It sounds simple, but it gives you a shared language to work with across engineering, product, and operations. And in a space where everyone is still figuring out the playbook, having that clarity helps teams move a lot faster.

Sam, thank you for sending me this book. Both Principles of AI Agents and Patterns of AI Agents are excellent 👌

============================================================

--- Post 434 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 103 reactions, 11 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 633)
Media: video
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7394771420796362752

World Labs just launched Marble, their first spatial intelligence model, and it marks an important shift in how we think about AI.

For the last few years, most progress has centered on language models. They’re powerful, but they operate in symbols. Spatial intelligence models operate in space. They learn 3D structure, depth, layout, and how objects relate to each other. Instead of predicting the next token, they generate consistent, navigable worlds.

I’ve been a fan of Fei-Fei Li’s vision ever since her 2015 TED Talk on helping computers understand images. She has consistently pushed the field toward grounding AI in the physical world, not just in language. Marble is a continuation of that direction.

Intelligence isn’t just about language. It’s about grounding AI in the physical world- where geometry, physics, and actions all matter.

Why this matters:
– Robotics needs realistic environments to train, test, and transfer safely
– Self-driving and drones rely on accurate spatial reasoning
– Smart cities and digital twins benefit from generative 3D worlds
– AR, VR, and gaming teams can build environments in minutes instead of months

Why this is hard: 3D is unforgiving. A language model can hallucinate and still be useful. A spatial model can’t produce impossible physics, broken geometry, or inconsistent lighting. Everything must hold together as an agent moves.

We’re moving from models that read the world to models that build it, and I personally super excited about it.

You can check out the renders here: https://lnkd.in/ggwqYitq

============================================================

--- Post 443 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 94 reactions, 10 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 578)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7433339213107863552

There is a very involved process behind why a LLMs gives you the output it gives you.

If you already have a sense of how LLMs are trained, here is one important concept you need to understand when you are trying to get the right kind of output from them:

Instruction tuning.

A base LLM is trained on massive unlabeled text.
Its objective is simple. Predict the next word.

That objective alone does not guarantee helpfulness. It does not guarantee instruction following. It just learns patterns.

Instruction tuning is the next step.

We take a pre-trained foundation model and fine-tune it on supervised instruction–response pairs.

✦ A human-written instruction
✦ An optional input context
✦ A high-quality target output

For example:
Instruction: “Summarize this text concisely.”
Input: A long paragraph.
Target output: A clean, structured summary.

The model is trained in a supervised loop to optimize for instruction following, not just next-token prediction.

The result is an instruction-tuned LLM.

More aligned with user intent.
Better at zero-shot tasks.
More consistent in tone and structure.

If you are building on top of LLMs, this distinction matters.
You are rarely interacting with a raw base model.
You are interacting with a model that has been explicitly trained to follow you.

============================================================

→ How Snowflake balances open-source (Llama, DeepSeek, Mistral) and frontier models (Anthropic, OpenAI)
→ Why fallback strategies, verified queries, and human-in-the-loop design are non-negotiable at scale
→ What it takes to make AI usable for analysts through AI SQL and Snowflake Intelligence
→ And how future systems will be powered by cascading models—not just one monolithic LLM

If you're serious about moving from agent demos to production systems, this conversation is loaded with engineering-first insight.

--- Post 446 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 91 reactions, 8 comments, 3 reposts (Score: 541)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7427023865718919168

5 days to go! 
Arvind and I are super excited to be hosting an AI evals hands-on workshop on February 15.

It’s a 90-minute session where we’ll cover the fundamentals of AI evaluations and then actually do it together. You’ll walk away having built and reasoned through real evals yourself. 

If you’re aspiring to be an AI engineer or planning to upskill for AI roles in 2026, understanding how to evaluate LLM applications is critical.

Building an LLM app is often the easy part. Knowing whether it truly works in production is where most teams struggle.

LLM evaluation is not just about correctness anymore. You are judging usefulness, safety, faithfulness to sources, consistency across runs, cost, latency, and real user outcomes across prompts, RAG pipelines, tools, and agentic workflows.

Because this is hard, many teams fall back to vibe-evals. If a few outputs look good, they ship. That approach breaks quickly when models change, prompts regress, retrieval fails, or user behavior shifts. Without evals, problems surface only after users complain.

Why learning evals matters:
✦ Evals turn LLM development from guesswork into engineering
✦ They help catch regressions as models, prompts, or tools evolve
✦ They are essential for scaling RAG systems and agentic workflows
✦ In 2026, strong teams use hybrid evals combining automated metrics, LLM-as-a-judge, and targeted human review

Evals are no longer optional. They are a core AI skill.

In this workshop, Arvind and I will walk through how AI evals are done in real systems, with hands-on exercises so you build intuition.

This is designed for AI engineers and tech leads building systems, and even business leaders who want intuition for what “good” really means in production. Even if you are a beginner, don't worry, we will give you the foundations for you to understand everything comprehensively, and enough resources that you can review at your own pace.

If you want to level up your AI skills for 2026, register here:
https://lnkd.in/dQSR7yhU

✅RAG fundamentals, fast recap: retrievers, vector stores, LLMs; what actually matters in a pipeline.

✅Memory that sticks: short- vs long-term memory, where “RAG as memory” breaks, and how to architect around it.

✅Context engineering: selecting, ordering, and compressing context across prompts, tools, and structured data.

✅Patterns & architectures: when classic Text RAG fits, and where Multimodal RAG and Graph RAG shine.

✅Multimodal RAG: stand up a working app using LangChain or LlamaIndex (docs + images).

✅Graph RAG: create a knowledge graph and plug it into a RAG QA pipeline (with LangChain).

📌 𝗬𝗼𝘂’𝗹𝗹 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝘃𝗲 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵:
✅Two starter repos, an evaluation checklist, and a POC rubric you can reuse at work.

✅A clear understanding of RAG and its applications.

✅Answers to your specific questions in the open-floor Q&A.

--- Post 474 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 40 reactions, 4 comments, 3 reposts (Score: 246)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419427622251741184

87% of GenAI prototypes never reach production. The reason? Poor evaluation. 📉

You can build a demo in 2 hours, but you can’t trust it without a robust testing pipeline. I’ve seen teams waste 100+ hours manually checking outputs, only to realize their model hallucinates on edge cases.

To fix this, you need to balance these 3 evaluation pillars:

1️⃣ Human Evaluations: Use this sparingly. It provides the highest quality signal but is too slow for daily iterations.

2️⃣ Code-Based Evaluations: Fast, free, and deterministic. If the JSON is broken or the code doesn't compile, reject it immediately.

3️⃣ LLM-as-a-Judge: The sweet spot. Evaluate 1,000s of interactions in minutes using a stronger model to grade a smaller one.

We are breaking down the exact code and frameworks to implement this on February 15th.
Don't let your AI project become a statistic. Join our upcoming AI Evals Workshop.

👇 Link in the comments!

============================================================

--- Post 475 ---
Author: aishwarya-srinivasan
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 40 reactions, 4 comments, 2 reposts (Score: 244)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7423072908803235840

Most people can build RAG systems. But it fails quickly in production, because the system might be: 

→ Generating factually correct answers that don't address the question
→ Hallucinating confidently with perfectly retrieved documents 
→ Missing context because retrieval failed upstream

Evaluation is the solution. Always check for:
→ Correctness: Is the answer factually accurate?
→ Relevance: Does it actually address the user's question? 
→ Groundedness: Is it supported by retrieved documents? 
→ Retrieval Relevance: Did we pull the right context?

A change that improves correctness might hurt groundedness. You need visibility across ALL metrics to build reliable systems. On February 15, The Gen Academy is hosting a workshop on evaluating LLM applications, led by two industry experts:

→ Aishwarya Srinivasan (Head of AI Developer Relations @ Fireworks AI | ex Google, Microsoft, IBM)
→ Arvind Narayanamurthy (Founder and Principal AI Consultant @ Eikos | ex Adobe, Microsoft, IBM)

Join us by using the registration link in the comments!

Save this framework for your next RAG project, and let us know how you evaluate your RAG pipelines.

============================================================

Not every LLM task needs to happen in real time. Sometimes, you just want to run a ton of calls- cheaply, reliably, and without hitting rate limits.

That’s why we built the Fireworks Batch API.

It’s the tool we wished we had when we were running prompt evaluations, synthetic data generation, and tool-call tests across thousands of inputs. Instead of wrangling infrastructure or hitting throttles, you just drop in a JSONL file and go.
✅ No rate limits
✅ Up to 50% cheaper than real-time calls, with an additional stacked cached prompt token discount
✅ 24-hour guaranteed turnaround (often way faster)
✅ Works across 1,000+ models—including your own fine-tunes
Whether you're experimenting at scale or shipping pipelines to production, Batch API gives you speed, scale, and simplicity, without the infra burden.

📖 Find the detailed Batch API blog here: https://lnkd.in/gpgpwhP6

There’s a lot of focus on RFT right now—and most evals are written with RFT workflows in mind. But many teams still miss the basics: prompt optimization can get you surprisingly far, and it stacks beautifully with RFT.

That’s exactly what Eval Protocol is built for: evaluate and improve both prompts and fine-tunes in one workflow.

In practice: optimize the prompt to get ~60% of the way there, then use RFT to squeeze out the final ~40%.

Start optimizing your prompt today—and get ready for an RFT future!

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7365112811976294400

When you optimize for the wrong rewards, models learn the wrong behavior.

In our Reward Hacking tutorial, we walk through how to design structured, multi-signal rewards to guide LLMs toward trustworthy, relevant outputs- while avoiding shortcuts that lead to poor generations.

This example highlights how to:
→ Use ROUGE-L and bullet recall for content relevance
→ Apply length gates to prevent verbose or bloated responses
→ Integrate fluency rewards for polished, readable output
→ Combine these signals in reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) workflows

Whether you're building summarization agents, recap tools, or evaluation pipelines, getting the reward design right is critical.

📖 Explore the full guide: https://lnkd.in/dBB2vbeP

On this 𝟮𝟳𝘁𝗵, we’re running 𝗥𝗔𝗚 𝟮.𝟬 with Arvind Narayanamurthy and Aishwarya Srinivasan —built for leaders, operators, and builders who want reliability without runaway costs.

𝗪𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝘆𝗼𝘂’𝗹𝗹 𝗱𝗼 (𝗹𝗶𝘃𝗲):

✅𝗠𝘂𝗹𝘁𝗶𝗺𝗼𝗱𝗮𝗹 𝗥𝗔𝗚 → stand up a working app using LangChain or LlamaIndex (docs + images).

✅𝗚𝗿𝗮𝗽𝗵 𝗥𝗔𝗚 → build a Neo4j knowledge graph and plug it into a RAG QA pipeline.

✅𝗢𝗽𝗲𝗻-𝗳𝗹𝗼𝗼𝗿 𝗤&𝗔 → bring your edge cases; we’ll troubleshoot them together.

𝗪𝗵𝘆 𝗶𝘁’𝘀 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝘁𝗵 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗮𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻𝗼𝗼𝗻:

Move from “bigger prompts” to 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗲𝘅𝘁 (retrievers, ranking, evals).

Learn where RAG-as-memory breaks—and what to use instead.

Leave with starter repos, an evaluation checklist, and a 30/60/90 adoption plan.

𝗦𝗼 𝘄𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝘆𝗼𝘂 𝘄𝗮𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗳𝗼𝗿?

𝗟𝗶𝗻𝗸 𝗧𝗼 𝗝𝗼𝗶𝗻 𝗜𝗻 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗙𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁 𝗖𝗼𝗺𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁
